It also says 'full plugin depth for TypeScript, Python, C#, C++, Dart, GDScript, Go, and Rust' and 'Desloppify is free for any individual — whether working independently or at a company — to use for their own work.' I opened https://raw.githubusercontent.com/peteromallet/desloppify/main/LICENSE ('Open Source Native License (OSNL) Version 0.2 - March 24, 2026'). It says 'Free for anyone to use internally, including commercial companies.' and 'Paid tiers only apply if you redistribute the Program in a product/service AND your company is not open source'. That contradicts the claim's 'commercial use otherwise is paid'. PyPI lists desloppify 1.0, uploaded 2026-05-13, with license OSNL-0.2.
- **land-20** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Lenarduzzi et al. (SANER 2020, arXiv v2 2019-12-19) studied 21 open-source Java projects. They found that SonarQube's own 'bug' classification predicts faults at about chance level (AUC 50.94%), and that individual violations are poor fault predictors. The abstract's '25 of 202 rules with relatively low fault-proneness' is ambiguously worded; the paper body says 26 violations were found fault-prone by the XGBoost model. The same paper reports that violations taken together in a trained XGBoost model reach an AUC of about 0.83. Its first lesson is that violations 'can be good predictors if considered together', and it recommends a customized model per company. A follow-up on 33 Apache Java projects (JSS 2020) found no difference in fault-proneness between classes with and without SonarQube issues, and only a small difference in change-proneness. So SonarQube's default rules and weights are poorly validated, but the evidence also supports calibrating weights to a project's own history, not abandoning rule-based measurement.
  - [Are SonarQube Rules Inducing Bugs? (Lenarduzzi, Lomio, Huttunen, Taibi)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1907.00376v2) (2019-12-19 (v2)): "violations considered as "bugs" by SonarQube were generally not fault-prone and, consequently, the fault-prediction power of the model proposed by SonarQube is extremely low."
  - Fact-check: I opened https://arxiv.org/abs/1907.00376v2. The cited quote appears verbatim in the abstract, alongside 'Among the 202 rules defined for Java by SonarQube, only 25 can be considered to have relatively low fault-proneness.' (v2 submitted 19 Dec 2019; SANER 2020). In the PDF body: 'Our results show that 26 violations can be considered fault-prone from the XGBoost model.' and 'an AUC of 50.94%, confirming that violations classified as "bugs" almost never resulted in a fault.' It also says 'Lesson 1: SonarQube violations are not good predictors of fault-proneness if considered individually, but can be good predictors if considered together.' with 'XGBoost AUC=83.32%±10%'. The follow-up is https://arxiv.org/abs/1908.11590 (JSS vol. 170, Dec 2020): 'As for fault-proneness, there is no difference between clean and dirty classes.'
- **land-21** [inference]: Inference: no surveyed tool combines all of the following: (a) one repository-level score with a numeric budget per PR, (b) deterministic, team-written hard-gate rules such as layer or ECS rules alongside soft metrics, and (c) change-scoped scoring for Rust. CodeScene (per-file improved/degraded verdict), Qlty SlopOne (per-file max-drop with an LLM-hybrid score), Codecov (budget for one metric) and Sonar (thresholds on new code; architecture analysis not available for Rust) each cover a part. This rests on findings land-1 to land-19; it does not rule out unindexed tools.
  - [slop-one - Qlty Docs](https://docs.qlty.sh/cli/commands/slop-one.md) (fetched 2026-11-07): "Compare the files changed since the merge base with REF against their versions there"
  - [CodeScene MCP Server - Tools](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/codescene-oss/codescene-mcp-server/main/docs/tools.md) (fetched 2026-11-07): "Returns quality gates (passed/failed) and per-file verdicts (improved, degraded, or stable)."

### Searches without relevant results

- 'open source code quality score gate for AI coding agents per pull request regression budget 2026' (extended, 2026-11-07): only blogs and vendor guides; no tool implementing a budget per PR on an aggregate score.
- 'Claude Code hook code health check block agent maintainability regression tool' (extended, 2026-11-07): only do-it-yourself hook write-ups (e.g. paulmduvall.com); no packaged tool for blocking maintainability regressions.
- 'SonarQube AI Code Assurance quality gate new code Rust support 2025' (standard, 2026-11-07): no official statement that Rust is covered by the AI-specific profile; the agentic profile docs later confirmed Java, JS/TS and Python only.
- 'Graphite Diamond AI reviewer custom rules Cursor acquisition 2025' (standard, 2026-11-07): found the acquisition, but did not open primary Graphite docs on custom rules, so no claim is made about them.
- Trunk hold-the-line HTML page (docs.trunk.io/check/configuration/hold-the-line) returned an auth/redirect shell via curl; the markdown docs page was used instead.
- Re-check (does not hold): 'open source code quality score gate for AI coding agents per pull request regression budget 2026' (extended, 2026-11-07): only blogs and vendor guides; no tool implementing a budget per PR on an aggregate score. — Re-ran in extended and standard mode. Extended returned blogs and listicles. Standard surfaced aislop (https://github.com/scanaislop/aislop), an MIT-licensed, deterministic CLI that supports Rust among 11 targets. I opened its README: "Scores every change 0–100." CI gates on an absolute threshold ('aislop ci exits 1 when score < threshold', failBelow) and on new findings against a baseline. Its agent-hook 'Quality-gate mode: Blocks if score regresses below baseline.' So 'only blogs and vendor guides' no longer holds. The narrower claim mostly still holds: I found no tool with a configurable per-PR delta budget on an aggregate score. aislop's hook mode is effectively a zero-tolerance regression gate on an aggregate score. Qlty SlopOne --max-drop is a budget per file, not per aggregate.
- Re-check (does not hold): 'Claude Code hook code health check block agent maintainability regression tool' (extended, 2026-11-07): only do-it-yourself hook write-ups (e.g. paulmduvall.com); no packaged tool for blocking maintainability regressions. — Re-ran in extended and standard mode. Extended again returned mostly do-it-yourself write-ups (paulmduvall.com, dev.to, medium); standard surfaced agenthook (a packaged lint hook). Packaged tools do exist. aislop ships 'aislop hook install --claude --quality-gate', which 'Blocks if score regresses below baseline' (README opened). The research's own findings show more: the CodeScene MCP pre_commit_code_health_safeguard and analyze_change_set tools report pass/fail Code Health gates (land-1), and qlty slop-one supports --trigger agent/pre-commit/pre-push with --max-drop (land-10). The negative result reflects the search snippets, not the actual landscape.
- Re-check (holds): 'SonarQube AI Code Assurance quality gate new code Rust support 2025' (standard, 2026-11-07): no official statement that Rust is covered by the AI-specific profile; the agentic profile docs later confirmed Java, JS/TS and Python only. — Re-ran in standard and extended mode. Results covered Rust support (2025-04-17 launch blog; Server 2025.3 docs) and AI Code Assurance docs separately, with no statement linking Rust to the AI profile. https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-cloud/standards/ai-code-assurance/quality-profiles-for-agentic-ai.md says "currently available for Java, JavaScript/TypeScript, and Python languages." Caveat: the quality gate itself (Sonar way for agentic AI) is not language-restricted on its docs page; only the profile is.
- Re-check (holds): 'Graphite Diamond AI reviewer custom rules Cursor acquisition 2025' (standard, 2026-11-07): found the acquisition, but did not open primary Graphite docs on custom rules, so no claim is made about them. — Re-ran; the results again cover the Cursor acquisition (agreement Dec 19, 2025; TechCrunch, SiliconANGLE) and note that Diamond was renamed to Graphite Agent. The statement accurately describes the earlier process. For completeness, I opened the primary docs at https://graphite-58cc94ce.mintlify.dev/docs/ai-review-customization.md (listed in graphite.com/docs/llms.txt). It says "AI reviews offer two primary customization options: Exclusions and Custom rules." These are natural-language rules for an LLM reviewer, not deterministic gates.
- Re-check (holds): Trunk hold-the-line HTML page (docs.trunk.io/check/configuration/hold-the-line) returned an auth/redirect shell via curl; the markdown docs page was used instead. — curl on https://docs.trunk.io/check/configuration/hold-the-line redirected to login.trunk.io (WorkOS sign-in page; stripped text: 'Sign in ... Continue with GitHub'). Caveat: on 2026-11-07 the .md variant (https://docs.trunk.io/check/configuration/hold-the-line.md) also returned the auth shell. Any Trunk claim built on the markdown page cannot be re-verified right now.

### Open questions

- How well do CodeRabbit, Qodo and Sourcery LLM rules work on Rust? They claim to be language-agnostic; this was not verified.
- Is the Qlty SlopOne score stable across reruns? It depends on an LLM; Qlty says its methodology and benchmarks are 'coming soon'.
- Can CodeScene's custom Code Health rules (rules_config_set_rule / set_threshold) express domain rules such as layering or ECS, or only tune built-in smells?
- Does the DeepSource Report Card aggregation have a documented formula and Rust-specific complexity metrics? The weighting is undocumented in the pages read.
- Is GitHub Code Quality now generally available? An InfoQ article from 2026-08 was fetched but its text could not be verified; GitHub docs give no status label.
- Does any Rust-specific architecture gate exist for checking layers across crates (e.g. cargo-deny, cargo-modules, custom dylint lints)? This area belongs to another research task.
- desloppify's license terms for commercial use were not read in full.

## Static code metrics and their empirical validity

- **size-1** [empirical-result]: Size confounds the classic OO design metrics. On a large C++ telecom framework, CK and a subset of Lorenz-Kidd metrics were associated with field-failure faults until class size was controlled; after that, none were. The earlier positive validation (Basili, Briand & Melo; 8 student-built C++ systems, 1995 TR / TSE 1996) had found several CK metrics useful for early fault prediction.
  - [El Emam, Benlarbi, Goel: The Confounding Effect of Class Size on the Validity of Object-oriented Metrics (NRC ERB-1062; journal version IEEE TSE 27(7), 2001)](https://www.ehealthinformation.ca/web/default/files/wp-files/1062.pdf) (1999-09 (TR); 2001 (TSE)): "After controlling for size none of the metrics we studied were associated with fault-proneness anymore. This demonstrates a strong size confounding effect"
  - [Basili, Briand, Melo: A Validation of Object-Oriented Design Metrics as Quality Indicators (UMD TR; IEEE TSE 22(10), 1996)](https://www.cs.umd.edu/~basili/publications/technical/T102.pdf) (1995-04 (TR); 1996-10 (TSE)): "Several of Chidamber&Kemerer’s OO metrics appear to be useful to predict class fault-proneness during the early phases of the life-cycle."
- **size-2** [empirical-result]: A study of about 730K Java methods from 47 OSS projects, using seven method-level metrics and change-proneness, explains why earlier studies contradicted each other. It concludes that models must account for the maintenance indicator used and for method size. Metrics work only when applied in a context-sensitive way.
  - [Chowdhury, Holmes, Zaidman, Kazman: Revisiting the debate: Are code metrics useful for measuring maintenance effort? (EMSE 27, art. 158)](https://research.tudelft.nl/en/publications/revisiting-the-debate-are-code-metrics-useful-for-measuring-maint) (2022-08-17): "should develop models that account for the maintenance indicator being used and the size of the methods being analyzed"
- **cc-1** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Cyclomatic complexity correlates with SLOC only moderately at method/function level. Across 17.6M Java methods and 6.3M C functions, linear R² was 0.40 for Java and 0.44 for C. Variance rises with size (heteroscedastic; Breusch-Pagan confirmed). The authors conclude CC is not redundant with SLOC, contradicting earlier literature claims of strong linear correlation. They also find that higher correlations appear after aggregating to file level or after log transforms. Jay et al. (2009) reported R² of about 0.87-0.97 after repeated median regression on file-level data, so Landman et al. do not directly refute Jay et al. For the many tiny 2-3 line subroutines, CC adds nothing over SLOC.
  - [Landman, Serebrenik, Bouwers, Vinju: Empirical analysis of the relationship between CC and SLOC in a large corpus of Java methods and C functions (J. Softw. Evol. Process, 2016; preprint)](https://homepages.cwi.nl/~jurgenv/papers/JSEP-2015.pdf) (2016 (preprint labelled 2015)): "The R2 of SLOC and CC is 0.40 for Java and 0.44 for C."
  - Fact-check: Opened the Landman et al. preprint (homepages.cwi.nl/~jurgenv/papers/JSEP-2015.pdf). The quote 'The R2 of SLOC and CC is 0.40 for Java and 0.44 for C.' is verbatim. The preprint does not name Jay et al. as contradicted. It says 'higher correlations can only be observed after aggregation to the file level or when we arbitrarily remove the larger elements from the corpus'. In Table I, Jay et al. [46] (file level, 2200 SourceForge projects) is described with 'the authors calculate a repeated median regression and recalculate R2 : 0.87, 0.93, and 0.97'. Also: 'enormous skew towards the smallest subroutines (2 or 3 lines), for which clearly CC offers no additional insight over SLOC.' The relevance note's 'overlap heavily' is a stretch for R²≈0.4.
- **mi-1** [fact]: The Maintainability Index (Oman & Hagemeister 1992/1994; used in Visual Studio as MAX(0,(171-5.2ln(HV)-0.23CC-16.2ln(LOC))*100/171)) has three problems: its coefficients were fitted to a few small HP C/Pascal systems; its inputs all correlate with LOC; and it averages metrics that follow a power law, which hides high-risk parts.
  - [Arie van Deursen: Think Twice Before Using the “Maintainability Index”](https://avandeursen.com/2014/08/29/think-twice-before-using-the-maintainability-index/) (2014-08-29): "these metrics follow a power law, and taking the average tends to mask the presence of high-risk parts."
  - [Arie van Deursen: Think Twice Before Using the “Maintainability Index”](https://avandeursen.com/2014/08/29/think-twice-before-using-the-maintainability-index/) (2014-08-29): "all underlying metrics (Halstead, Cyclomatic Complexity, Lines of Code) are directly correlated with size (lines of code)."
- **cog-1** [empirical-result]: SonarSource cognitive complexity (whitepaper v1.7, 2023-08-29) replaces CC's graph model with rules: ignore shorthand, +1 per break in linear flow, and extra increments for nesting. A meta-analysis of about 24,000 evaluations of 427 snippets found it correlates with comprehension time (pooled r=0.54, 95% CI 0.24-0.75, I²=85%) and with understandability ratings (r=-0.29, CI -0.49 to -0.06). It did not reliably correlate with correctness (r=-0.13, CI -0.45 to 0.21) or with physiological measures (r=0.00; one study, 12 snippets).
  - [G. Ann Campbell: Cognitive Complexity - a new way of measuring understandability (SonarSource whitepaper v1.7)](https://www.sonarsource.com/docs/CognitiveComplexity.pdf) (2023-08-29): "abandons the use of mathematical models for assessing control flow in favor of a set of simple rules for turning programmer intuition into numbers"
  - [Muñoz Barón, Wyrich, Wagner: An Empirical Validation of Cognitive Complexity as a Measure of Source Code Understandability (ESEM 2020)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2007.12520) (2020-07-24): "Cognitive Complexity positively correlates with comprehension time and subjective ratings of understandability. The metric showed mixed results for the correlation with the correctness of comprehension tasks"
- **und-1** [empirical-result]: In 444 evaluations from 63 developers, none of 121 code, documentation and developer metrics, including readability and complexity metrics, captured code understandability. Combining metrics in models helped only slightly.
  - [Scalabrino, Bavota, Vendome, Linares-Vásquez, Poshyvanyk, Oliveto: Automatically Assessing Code Understandability (IEEE TSE)](https://www.cs.wm.edu/~denys/pubs/TSE'19-Understandability.pdf) (2019): "none of the 121 experimented metrics is able to capture code understandability, not even the ones assumed to assess quality attributes apparently related, such as code readability and complexity"
- **neuro-1** [empirical-result]: An fMRI study (19 participants, more than 41 metrics in four classes) found that complexity metrics explain programmers' cognition only to a limited degree. Textual size drove attention and vocabulary size loaded working memory.
  - [Peitek, Apel, Parnin, Brechmann, Siegmund: Program Comprehension and Code Complexity Metrics: An fMRI Study (ICSE 2021)](https://www.se.cs.uni-saarland.de/publications/docs/PAP+21.pdf) (2021-05): "a code’s textual size drives programmers’ attention, and vocabulary size burdens programmers’ working memory"
- **neuro-2** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Gao, Hijazi, Durães, ... Madeira, 'On the accuracy of code complexity metrics: A neuroscience-based guideline for improvement', Frontiers in Neuroscience 16:1065366, published 2023-02-07. In an EEG and eye-tracking experiment with 27 programmers (students and professionals) reading Java programs, McCabe V(g) and SonarSource cognitive complexity deviated considerably from programmers' perceived complexity, with EEG cognitive load as the reference. They often did not show the expected monotonic behaviour.
  - [Hao et al.: Accurate identification of code complexity using EEG and eye-tracking measures (Frontiers in Neuroscience)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9942489/) (2023-02): "popular metrics such as V(g) and the complexity metric from SonarSource tools deviate considerably from the programmers’ perception of code complexity and often do not show the expected monotonic behavior"
  - Fact-check: Opened PMC9942489. The title, first author and journal volume in the finding are wrong: it is 'Gao et al.', not 'Hao et al.', and the title is not 'Accurate identification...'. The abstract says 'Participants (27 programmers)', 'The programmers' cognitive load measured using EEG was used as a reference'. The quote 'popular metrics such as V(g) and the complexity metric from SonarSource tools deviate considerably from the programmers' perception of code complexity and often do not show the expected monotonic behavior' is verbatim. The finding's substance holds.
- **weights-1** (corrected) [fact]: Feitelson (CACM 66(5):48-57, May 2023) concludes that code complexity is only one of many factors in comprehension, and perhaps not the major one. He states explicitly that Shao and Wang's construct weights rest only on intuition. For cognitive complexity the same point comes from its own whitepaper, which describes rules 'for turning programmer intuition into numbers'. Feitelson notes Campbell's nesting weights in the same passage without explicitly calling them intuition-based. He also cites controlled experiments that try to put such weights on an empirical footing.
  - [Dror G. Feitelson: From Code Complexity Metrics to Program Comprehension (Communications of the ACM 66(5))](https://www.cse.huji.ac.il/~feit/papers/Comp23CACM.pdf) (2023-05): "code complexity is only one of many factors affecting code comprehension, and perhaps not the major one"
  - [Dror G. Feitelson: From Code Complexity Metrics to Program Comprehension (Communications of the ACM 66(5))](https://www.cse.huji.ac.il/~feit/papers/Comp23CACM.pdf) (2023-05): "this assignment of weights is based only on intuition, which is not a scientifically valid way"
  - Fact-check: Opened Comp23CACM.pdf. It reads 'code complexity is only one of many factors affecting code comprehension, and perhaps not the major one' (verbatim). In context: 'Campbell... gives added weight to each level of nesting [7]. Shao and Wang suggest that if a sequence has weight 1, an if should have weight 2... But this assignment of weights is based only on intuition, which is not a scientifically valid way'. Grammatically, 'this assignment' refers to Shao and Wang. The SonarSource whitepaper v1.7 supplies the intuition basis for cognitive complexity.
- **read-1** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Buse & Weimer (TSE 36(4), 2010) trained a readability model on 12,000 judgments from 120 annotators of 100 short Java snippets, each three consecutive simple statements. The model was '80% effective' at predicting readability judgments. It correlated strongly with code changes, automated defect reports (FindBugs) and defect-log messages over more than 2.2M LOC. Agreement between each annotator and the average human score was moderate (average Spearman 0.55). The model's agreement with the average human score was higher (Spearman 0.71).
  - [Buse, Weimer: Learning a Metric for Code Readability (IEEE TSE 36(4), preprint)](https://web.eecs.umich.edu/~weimerw/p/weimer-tse2010-readability-preprint.pdf) (2010): "we construct an automated readability measure and show that it can be 80% effective, and better than a human on average, at predicting readability judgments"
  - Fact-check: Opened the UMich preprint. The quote is verbatim. It reads 'A survey of 120 human annotators on 100 code snippets', 'over 2.2 million lines of code' and 'A snippet consists of precisely three consecutive simple statements'. Fig. 4 shows 'Spearman's ρ Avg—Humans ... 0.55' and 'Avg—Model ... 0.71'. The caption says Avg—Humans 'gives the average value of the statistic when applied between human annotator scores and the average human annotator score'. So 0.55 is annotator-versus-mean agreement, not pairwise agreement among annotators.
- **llm-1** [empirical-result]: LLM readability judgments agree weakly with humans. CoReEval (10 LLMs, more than 1.4M evaluations) found Pearson 0.25 with human ratings for Java, 0.02 for Python and 0.00 for CUDA. In both CoReEval and an ICPC 2024 study, Scalabrino's static readability model matched human ratings slightly better than the alternatives: Pearson 0.31 in CoReEval, MCC 0.325 versus about 0 for the other static models in ICPC 2024.
  - [Ouédraogo et al.: Human-Aligned Code Readability Assessment with Large Language Models (arXiv preprint)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.16579) (2025-10-18): "LLMs show moderate alignment with human judgments in Java (Pearson = 0.25, Spearman = 0.25), indicating some consistency in relative scoring, though far from strong agreement"
  - [Sergeyuk et al.: Reassessing Java Code Readability Models with a Human-Centered Approach (ICPC 2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.14936) (2024-01-26): "Scalabrino’s model [28] exhibits a moderate correlation with human evaluations (MCC = 0.325)."
- **nat-1** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Code is more repetitive than English under n-gram models (Hindle et al. 2012). In about 8,296 bug-fix commits from 10 Java projects, Ray et al. found that buggy lines are more entropic and become less so once fixed. The cost-effectiveness comparison with PMD and FindBugs used a different dataset: post-release bugs in releases of 5 Apache projects (Derby, Lucene, OpenJPA, Qpid, Wicket), taken from Rahman et al. On that dataset, entropy ranking performed comparably to PMD/FindBugs: slightly better only with partial credit plus a bug-size threshold, and with no significant difference otherwise. Performance varied substantially across projects and releases.
  - [Ray, Hellendoorn, et al.: On the “Naturalness” of Buggy Code (ICSE 2016)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1506.01159) (2015-06-03 (arXiv v1); ICSE 2016): "We find that code with bugs tends to be more entropic (i.e., unnatural), becoming less so as bugs are fixed."
  - [Hindle, Barr, Su, Gabel, Devanbu: On the Naturalness of Software (ICSE 2012)](https://www.cs.ucdavis.edu/~devanbu/natural.pdf) (2012-06): "We show that code is also very repetitive, and in fact even more so than natural languages."
  - Fact-check: Opened arXiv 1506.01159 v2 (PDF). The Phase-I data are 38,003 commits, 'of which 8,296 were marked as bug fixing changes'. For the static bug finder (SBF) comparison: 'we only focus on post-release bugs that are reported in an issue tracking system. We used the data set prepared by Rahman et al. [28], in which snapshots of the five Apache projects'. Result: 'SBF and NBF performed comparably; NBF performed slightly better when using both partial credit and the specified threshold'. Also: 'performance varied substantially between projects and between releases'. Opened Hindle et al. natural.pdf: 'We show that code is also very repetitive, and in fact even more so than natural languages.' It uses 'the widely adopted n-gram model'. I could not search for later replications (e.g. Rahman/Palani/Rigby 'Natural Software Revisited', ICSE 2019, which reportedly attributes much of code's repetitiveness to syntax tokens) because the web-search budget was used up.
- **smell-1** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Palomba et al. studied 395 releases of 30 OSS projects and 17,350 manually validated instances of 13 smell types. They report that smells involving long and/or complex code (e.g. Complex Class) are 'highly diffused'; the abstract does not say they were the most common. Smelly classes had higher change- and fault-proneness than smell-free classes. The abstract gives no effect sizes.
  - [Palomba, Bavota, Di Penta, Fasano, Oliveto, De Lucia: On the diffuseness and the impact on maintainability of code smells (EMSE)](https://research.tudelft.nl/en/publications/on-the-diffuseness-and-the-impact-on-maintainability-of-code-smel) (2017 (online); EMSE 23, 2018): "smells characterized by long and/or complex code (e.g., Complex Class) are highly diffused, and that smelly classes have a higher change- and fault-proneness than smell-free classes"
  - Fact-check: Opened the TU Delft research portal abstract. It gives '395 releases of 30 open source projects', '17,350 manually validated instances of 13 different code smell kinds' and the quote: 'smells characterized by long and/or complex code (e.g., Complex Class) are highly diffused, and that smelly classes have a higher change- and fault-proneness than smell-free classes'. 'Most common' is stronger than 'highly diffused'. Lenarduzzi et al. (arXiv 1908.11590), summarising this study, write: 'Out of the 13 code smells, only seven were highly diffused smells'. I did not open the full text. The relevance note's point that size is a confounder is a fair inference but is not tested by this source.
- **td-1** [empirical-result]: In 33 Apache Java projects (726 commits, 27K faults, 12M changes, more than 95K items from 173 SonarQube rules), classes without technical-debt items were only slightly less change-prone than affected classes. Fault-proneness did not differ, and SonarQube's type and severity labels were often inconsistent.
  - [Lenarduzzi, Saarimäki, Taibi: Some SonarQube Issues have a Significant but Small Effect on Faults and Changes (JSS 170, 110750)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1908.11590) (2019-08-30 (arXiv); JSS 2020-12): "Clean classes (classes not affected by TD items) are less change-prone than dirty ones, but the difference between the groups is small."
- **sqale-1** (corrected) [formula]: In SQALE, technical debt (the SQALE Quality Index, SQI) is the sum of remediation costs over all non-compliances. Each requirement has a remediation function that turns the number of non-compliances into a remediation cost. Debt can be divided by code size to get a debt density. The cited paper makes the ratio-scale argument for adding up costs explicitly for non-remediation costs; for remediation costs it is implied. The cited paper does not name 'SQID' or 'debt ratio'. SonarQube defines its debt ratio as debt divided by an estimated development cost (default 30 min per line × LOC), not by a budget.
  - [Letouzey, Ilkiewicz: Managing Technical Debt with the SQALE Method (IEEE Software, accepted version)](https://lists.uniroma2.it/index.html/arc/isssr/2014-01/msg00007/IEEE-SW-Managing-TD-with-SQALE-Accepted-version.pdf) (2012): "Technical Debt is the sum of remediation costs for all noncompliances."
  - Fact-check: Opened the IEEE Software accepted-version PDF. Quotes: 'Technical Debt is the sum of remediation costs for all noncompliances. In the SQALE method this debt is called SQALE Quality Index (SQI).'; '[a remediation function] turns the number of non-compliances into a remediation cost'; 'divide that amount by the code size ... to obtain the density of the analyzed code debt'; 'Because the non-remediation costs are not established on an ordinal scale but on a ratio scale, we have shown (10) that we can aggregate the measures by addition'. Opened the SonarQube docs (docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-server/latest/user-guide/code-metrics/metrics-definition/): 'sqale_debt_ratio = technical debt /(cost to develop one line of code * number of lines of code)'. Note: sqale.org now serves an unrelated betting site, so the original SQALE definition document could not be opened.
- **clone-1** [empirical-result]: The evidence on clones is mixed. Juergens et al. (ICSE 2009, commercial and OSS systems) found that nearly every second unintentionally inconsistent change to a clone led to a fault. Rahman, Bird & Devanbu (MSR 2010) found that most bugs were not significantly associated with clones and that cloned code may be less defect-prone than non-cloned code.
  - [Juergens, Deissenboeck, Hummel, Wagner: Do Code Clones Matter? (ICSE 2009)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1701.05472) (2009-05 (ICSE); arXiv 2017-01-19): "Our results suggest that nearly every second unintentionally inconsistent change to a clone leads to a fault."
  - [Rahman, Bird, Devanbu: Clones: What is that Smell? (MSR 2010)](https://microsoft.com/en-us/research/wp-content/uploads/2016/02/rahman2010cws.pdf) (2010): "the great majority of bugs are not significantly associated with clones. Second, we find that clones may be less defect prone than non-cloned code"
- **arch-1** [empirical-result]: MacCormack, Rusnak & Baldwin measure architecture with a file-level design structure matrix (DSM). Its transitive closure gives each file's 'visibility' (direct and indirect dependencies), and the mean visibility across files is 'propagation cost'. A thesis using these techniques at one firm (8 product versions) linked architectural complexity to 50% productivity drops, 3x defect density and 10x staff turnover.
  - [MacCormack, Rusnak, Baldwin: The Impact of Component Modularity on Design Evolution (HBS Working Paper 08-038; third-party mirror)](https://swizec.com/pdfs/impact-of-modularity.pdf) (2007): "The mean visibility of all components provides an indication of the coupling for the system as a whole. This measure is referred to as Propagation Cost"
  - [Sturtevant: System design and the cost of architectural complexity (MIT PhD thesis, advisor MacCormack)](https://dspace.mit.edu/handle/1721.1/79551) (2013): "differences in architectural complexity could account for 50% drops in productivity, three-fold increases in defect density, and order-of-magnitude increases in staff turnover"
- **proc-1** [empirical-result]: Across 85 releases of 12 large OSS projects, code metrics were generally less useful than process metrics such as churn for defect prediction. Code metrics barely change between releases ('high stasis'), so models keep flagging the same files, which turn out to be comparatively less defect-dense.
  - [Rahman, Devanbu: How, and Why, Process Metrics Are Better (ICSE 2013)](https://2013.icse-conferences.org/content/how-and-why-process-metrics-are-better.html) (2013-05): "code metrics, despite widespread use in the defect prediction literature, are generally less useful than process metrics for prediction"
- **inf-1** [inference]: Inference: Geiger's score should aggregate soft metrics with size-aware, tail-sensitive functions (for example share of functions above a risk threshold, or worst-k on changed functions) rather than repo-wide means. A 0.2-point budget on a repo-wide mean would let a large codebase absorb arbitrarily bad local changes. This rests on the MI critique (power-law metrics, averaging hides risk), El Emam's and Chowdhury's size confounding, Rahman & Devanbu's stasis of code metrics, and Hao et al.'s non-monotonic metric behaviour.
  - [Arie van Deursen: Think Twice Before Using the “Maintainability Index”](https://avandeursen.com/2014/08/29/think-twice-before-using-the-maintainability-index/) (2014-08-29): "these metrics follow a power law, and taking the average tends to mask the presence of high-risk parts."
  - [El Emam, Benlarbi, Goel: The Confounding Effect of Class Size on the Validity of Object-oriented Metrics](https://www.ehealthinformation.ca/web/default/files/wp-files/1062.pdf) (1999-09): "This demonstrates a strong size confounding effect, and casts doubt on the results of previous object-orient"
- **inf-2** (corrected) [inference]: The conclusion (use a policy score, not a validated objective quality measure) follows. One premise is misstated. The cognitive complexity meta-analysis (Muñoz Barón, Wyrich, Wagner, ESEM 2020) found r=0.54 with comprehension time, with high heterogeneity (I²=85%). It also found a medium correlation of −0.29 with subjective understandability ratings. Results for correctness and physiological measures were mixed. The correlation is therefore not 'for time only'. Feitelson's 'weights based on intuition' refers to weights inside complexity metrics (e.g. Shao & Wang), not to weights in quality scores.
  - [Scalabrino et al.: Automatically Assessing Code Understandability (IEEE TSE)](https://www.cs.wm.edu/~denys/pubs/TSE'19-Understandability.pdf) (2019): "none of the 121 experimented metrics is able to capture code understandability"
  - [Feitelson: From Code Complexity Metrics to Program Comprehension (CACM)](https://www.cse.huji.ac.il/~feit/papers/Comp23CACM.pdf) (2023-05): "this assignment of weights is based only on intuition, which is not a scientifically valid way"
  - Fact-check: Opened arXiv 2007.12520 abstract and PDF. Abstract: 'Cognitive Complexity positively correlates with comprehension time and subjective ratings of understandability. The metric showed mixed results for the correlation with the correctness'. Body: 'Cognitive Complexity correlates with the time taken to comprehend code snippets (0.54) and with the perceived understandability ratings from developers (−0.29)'. Opened Scalabrino TSE'19 PDF: 'none of the 121 experimented metrics is able to capture code understandability' (444 evaluations from 63 developers; combining metrics gave 'some improvements'). Opened Feitelson CACM PDF: 'this assignment of weights is based only on intuition, which is not a scientifically valid way to devise a code complexity metric', said of Shao and Wang's weights. Lenarduzzi and CoReEval are verified under td-1 and llm-1.

### Searches without relevant results

- "empirical validation Robert Martin package metrics instability abstractness 'distance from the main sequence' defects study" (extended, 2026-11-07): no defect- or change-prediction study of I/A/D found. Only a theoretical/small validation (Leppänen et al., SSRN) and conference slides (Ruohonen, Hyrynsalmi, Leppänen: 14 Vaadin packages, 'Martin's argument does not hold'), too weak to cite as a finding.
- Gil & Lalouche 'On the correlation between size and metric validity' (EMSE 2017, DOI 10.1007/s10664-017-9513-5): the full text and abstract could not be opened (publisher bot check; Semantic Scholar elides the abstract), so its claim that size correlation predicts metric validity (R² up to 0.97, seen only in a search snippet) is unverified (standard, 2026-11-07).
- Chowdhury et al. 2022 full-text PDF (TU Delft and Springer) returned HTML instead of the PDF; only the abstract on the TU Delft portal was read, so no effect sizes were extracted (2026-11-07).
- "MacCormack Rusnak Baldwin 'propagation cost' 'design structure matrix' hbs.edu pdf" (standard, 2026-11-07): no free copy of the 2006 Management Science paper could be opened (typeset.io, CORE and Harvard DASH returned HTML); the definition was taken from HBS WP 08-038 instead.
- "MacCormack Sturtevant 'Technical debt and system architecture' core files defects" (standard, 2026-11-07): the paper itself was not found; only Sturtevant's 2013 thesis abstract was used.
- Re-check (does not hold): No defect- or change-prediction study of Martin's I/A/D package metrics found (extended search, 2026-11-07). — Not re-checked. The session-wide WebSearch budget (200 calls) was exhausted before I could re-run this search in standard or extended mode. I found nothing that contradicts it, and nothing that confirms it. Treat it as unverified by this pass, not as refuted.
- Re-check (holds): Gil & Lalouche (EMSE 2017, DOI 10.1007/s10664-017-9513-5) full text/abstract could not be opened; R² up to 0.97 claim unverified. — Re-tried https://link.springer.com/content/pdf/10.1007/s10664-017-9513-5.pdf with curl on 2026-11-07. It returned an HTML document, not the PDF. The Springer article page redirects to idp.springer.com/authorize. I could not re-run the search because the search budget was exhausted. The claim remains unverified.
- Re-check (holds): Chowdhury et al. 2022 full-text PDF (TU Delft and Springer) returned HTML; only the abstract was read; no effect sizes extracted. — Re-fetched https://research.tudelft.nl/files/130282524/s10664_022_10193_8.pdf on 2026-11-07. It returned 'HTML document text'. The Springer link redirects to an auth page. The TU Delft portal abstract was readable and is the basis for the size-2 verdict. Still no effect sizes.
- Re-check (does not hold): No free copy of MacCormack, Rusnak & Baldwin 2006 Management Science paper could be opened; definition taken from HBS WP 08-038. — Not re-checked. The WebSearch budget was exhausted, so I could not re-run the standard search. I had no known direct URL to try without searching. Unverified by this pass, not refuted.
- Re-check (does not hold): MacCormack & Sturtevant 'Technical debt and system architecture' paper not found; only Sturtevant 2013 thesis abstract used. — Not re-checked. The WebSearch budget was exhausted, so I could not re-run the standard search. Unverified by this pass, not refuted.

### Open questions

- Halstead, NPath, ABC, LCOM variants and fan-in/fan-out were not separately validated in sources read here. Do any primary studies show they add predictive value beyond LOC once size is controlled?
- No validation of Robert Martin's instability/abstractness/distance against defects or change effort was found. Is there any, or should Geiger treat these purely as policy rules?
- All comprehension validations (cognitive complexity meta-analysis, fMRI, EEG, readability models) use short Java/C/Python snippets. Do any cover Rust, or ownership and lifetime constructs, which add complexity these metrics ignore?
- Does LLM per-token surprisal (perplexity) on changed lines outperform n-gram entropy for bug localisation, and is it stable enough between model versions to serve as a scored metric?
- Majumder et al. replicated Rahman & Devanbu (arXiv 2008.09569, EMSE 2022) and disagreed on some conclusions. Which conclusions survive?
- How do metric thresholds behave when the code author is an AI agent? Agents may game function-length or complexity limits by splitting functions, which raises fan-out and propagation cost instead (Goodhart risk; no empirical source found here).

## Test-quality, evolution/process and organizational metrics and their validity

- **cov-1** [empirical-result]: Inozemtseva & Holmes (ICSE 2014) generated 31,000 test suites for 5 Java systems (up to 724 KLOC) and found only low-to-moderate correlation between coverage (statement, decision, modified condition) and mutation-based effectiveness once suite size is held constant. Stronger coverage criteria gave no extra insight. Limit: effectiveness was measured with mutants, not real faults.
  - [Coverage Is Not Strongly Correlated with Test Suite Effectiveness (ICSE 2014)](https://cs.ubc.ca/~rtholmes/papers/icse_2014_inozemtseva.pdf) (2014): "We found that there is a low to moderate correlation between coverage and effectiveness when the number of test cases in the suite is controlled for."
  - [Coverage Is Not Strongly Correlated with Test Suite Effectiveness (ICSE 2014)](https://cs.ubc.ca/~rtholmes/papers/icse_2014_inozemtseva.pdf) (2014): "stronger forms of coverage do not provide greater insight into the effectiveness of the suite"
- **cov-2** [empirical-result]: Gopinath, Jensen & Groce (ICSE 2014) studied manual and generated suites from hundreds of real-world open-source projects and found that plain statement coverage predicted mutation kills better than block, branch or path coverage when evaluating a single suite.
  - [Code Coverage for Suite Evaluation by Developers (ICSE 2014)](https://www.cefns.nau.edu/~adg326/icse14.pdf) (2014): "statement (not block, branch, or path) coverage predicts mutation kills best."
- **cov-3** [empirical-result]: Kochhar, Lo, Lawall & Nagappan (IEEE Trans. Reliability, Dec 2017) measured coverage of 100 large open-source Java projects against post-release bugs. They found an insignificant correlation at project level and none at file level.
  - [Code Coverage and Post-release Defects: A Large-Scale Study on Open Source Projects](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/publication/code-coverage-and-post-release-defects-a-large-scale-study-on-open-source-projects/) (2017-12): "Our results show that coverage has an insignificant correlation with the number of bugs"
- **cov-4** [empirical-result]: Chen et al. (ASE 2020, with Just, Holmes, Ernst, Fraser) argue that test set size is neither a confounder nor a variable to manipulate, and call the random-selection designs of earlier studies flawed. With their own method they find adequacy-based selection beats random selection, and mutation-based selection pays off most after coverage is exhausted.
  - [Revisiting the Relationship Between Fault Detection, Test Adequacy Criteria, and Test Set Size (ASE 2020)](https://homes.cs.washington.edu/~mernst/pubs/test-set-size-ase2020.pdf) (2020-09): "mutation-based test selection is most effective when employed after coverage has exhausted its usefulness."
  - [Revisiting the Relationship Between Fault Detection, Test Adequacy Criteria, and Test Set Size (ASE 2020)](https://homes.cs.washington.edu/~mernst/pubs/test-set-size-ase2020.pdf) (2020-09): "test set size is neither a confounding variable, as previously suggested, nor an independent variable that should be experimentally manipulated."
- **mut-1** [empirical-result]: Just et al. (FSE 2014) used 357 real faults in 5 Java programs (321 KLOC) and 230,000 mutants. Mutant detection correlated significantly with real-fault detection independently of coverage, and more strongly than statement coverage did. 73% of real faults were coupled to mutants, 10% needed new or stronger operators, and 17% were not coupled.
  - [Are Mutants a Valid Substitute for Real Faults in Software Testing? (FSE 2014)](https://homes.cs.washington.edu/~mernst/pubs/mutation-effectiveness-fse2014.pdf) (2014): "The results show a statistically significant correlation between mutant detection and real fault detection, independently of code coverage."
- **mut-2** [empirical-result]: Papadakis, Shin, Yoo & Bae (ICSE 2018), using CoreBench (C) and Defects4J (Java), found that all mutation-score versus real-fault correlations become weak when suite size is controlled. Selecting suites by higher mutation score still significantly improved fault detection over random same-size suites.
  - [Are Mutation Scores Correlated with Real Fault Detection? (ICSE 2018)](https://mpapad.github.io/publications/pdfs/ICSE2018.pdf) (2018): "all correlations between mutation scores and real fault detection are weak when controlling for test suite size."
  - [Are Mutation Scores Correlated with Real Fault Detection? (ICSE 2018)](https://mpapad.github.io/publications/pdfs/ICSE2018.pdf) (2018): "our data suggest that mutants provide good guidance for improving the fault detection of test suites, but their correlation with fault detection are weak."
- **mut-3** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Zhao, Zhou & Cohen (Proc. ACM Softw. Eng. 3, ISSTA, Oct 2026; arXiv 2607.22880, 24 Jul 2026) replicated Inozemtseva et al. and Papadakis et al. on test suites that 11 LLMs generated for Defects4J (Java). Coverage and mutation score gave meaningful signals when comparing across models in regression-style settings, where the code under test is assumed bug-free. When the code under test may already be buggy, coverage became unreliable and mutation analysis was not applicable. They found little evidence that suite size is a dominant confounder.
  - [Do Coverage and Mutation Scores of LLM-Generated Test Suites Correlate with Their Effectiveness? (Replicability Study)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.22880) (2026-07-24): "We also find little evidence that test suite size is a dominant confounder for correlations among coverage, mutation, and real-bug detection for LLM-generated tests."
  - [Do Coverage and Mutation Scores of LLM-Generated Test Suites Correlate with Their Effectiveness? (Replicability Study)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.22880) (2026-07-24): "in another common scenario where the code-under-test may already be buggy and the goal is to expose the bug within the code-under-test, they no longer serve as reliable indicators."
  - Fact-check: Downloaded https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.22880. Both quotes appear verbatim. Abstract qualifier omitted by the claim: "these metrics can provide meaningful signals when comparing across models". Body: "coverage becomes unreliable as an indicator of bug detection effectiveness, and mutation analysis is not applicable." Scope: "we use Defects4J" with "11 state-of-the-art (SOTA) LLMs". Venue: "Proc. ACM Softw. Eng. 3, ISSTA, Article ISSTA002 (October 2026)". The relevance note (tests from buggy code encode the bug) is supported: "tests generated from buggy code can encode faulty behavior that is absent from the bug-free implementation." The finding is about comparing LLMs, not about scoring one repository's suite, so it bears only indirectly on Geiger's per-repository score.
- **mut-4** [empirical-result]: Google (Petrović, Ivanković, Fraser, Just; IEEE TSE 2021) runs mutation testing only on changed lines during code review for over 24,000 developers and 1,000 projects (~17M mutants, 760k changes). Developers initially rated 85% of mutants unproductive. Suppression heuristics built from developer feedback raised the productive share from 15% to 89%.
  - [Practical Mutation Testing at Scale: A view from Google](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2102.11378v2) (2021-02-26): "developers at Google initially classified 85% of reported mutants as unproductive."
  - [Practical Mutation Testing at Scale: A view from Google](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2102.11378v2) (2021-02-26): "heuristics for mutant suppression that improved the ratio of productive mutants from 15% to 89%."
- **cov-5** (corrected) [fact]: Google computes coverage for one billion lines daily in seven languages and surfaces changelist coverage in code review. It enforces no codebase-wide threshold; many projects opt into voluntary levels, from project >=60% with changelist >=70% up to project >=90% with changelist >=90%. The survey went to 3,000 developers and got 512 responses (17%). Of the 286 write-in comments, 60% were positive about coverage, 24% neutral and 16% negative.
  - [Code Coverage at Google (ESEC/FSE 2019)](https://homes.cs.washington.edu/~rjust/publ/google_coverage_fse_2019.pdf) (2019-08): "Google does not enforce any code coverage thresholds across the entire codebase. Projects (or groups of projects) are free to define their own thresholds and goals."
  - Fact-check: Downloaded https://homes.cs.washington.edu/~rjust/publ/google_coverage_fse_2019.pdf. The no-threshold quote is verbatim. The 60%/16% figures come from Table 3: "Sentiment towards coverage expressed in the write-in text box"; "60% of responses were generally positive towards coverage, 16% were negative, 24% were neutral." The table total is 286 (173 positive, 46 negative), not 512. Also: "We sent the survey to 3000 randomly chosen developers at Google out of which 512 responded (17%)." Levels in Table 2: "Project coverage at least 60%; Changelist coverage at least 70%" ... "Project coverage at least 90%; Changelist coverage at least 90%". The data is from 2019.
- **assert-1** [empirical-result]: Zhang & Mesbah (ESEC/FSE 2015) built 6,700 suites using 24,000 assertions from 5 Java projects. Assertion count and assertion coverage correlated strongly with effectiveness, and assertion count explains much of the size-effectiveness link. Limit: effectiveness was measured as mutation score.
  - [Assertions Are Strongly Correlated with Test Suite Effectiveness (FSE 2015)](https://people.ece.ubc.ca/amesbah/resources/papers/fse15.pdf) (2015): "the number of assertions in a test suite strongly correlates with its effectiveness, and this factor directly influences the relationship between test suite size and effectiveness."
- **flaky-1** [empirical-result]: Google reported (May 2016) that about 1.5% of all test runs gave flaky results, almost 16% of tests showed some flakiness, and about 84% of observed pass-to-fail transitions involved a flaky test.
  - [Flaky Tests at Google and How We Mitigate Them (John Micco, Google Testing Blog)](https://testing.googleblog.com/2016/05/flaky-tests-at-google-and-how-we.html) (2016-05-27): "What we find in practice is that about 84% of the transitions we observe from pass to fail involve a flaky test!"
- **proc-1** [empirical-result]: Graves et al. (IEEE TSE 2000), studying one very large, long-lived system, found that change-history measures beat product metrics. Change count predicted faults better than length, and complexity metrics added nothing because they tracked LOC. A module one year older had about a third fewer faults. Developer count and co-change coupling did not improve predictions.
  - [Predicting Fault Incidence Using Software Change History (IEEE TSE 26(7))](https://cs.uwaterloo.ca/~m2nagapp/courses/CS846/1171/papers/graves_tse98.pdf) (2000-07): "the number of times code has been changed is a better indication of how many faults it will contain than is its length."
  - [Predicting Fault Incidence Using Software Change History (IEEE TSE 26(7))](https://cs.uwaterloo.ca/~m2nagapp/courses/CS846/1171/papers/graves_tse98.pdf) (2000-07): "Perhaps surprisingly, there was no evidence that a large number of developers working on a module caused it to be more faulty."
- **proc-2** [empirical-result]: Nagappan & Ball (ICSE 2005) found on Windows Server 2003 that absolute churn predicts defect density poorly. Churn normalized by component size and time span is highly predictive and separated fault-prone binaries with 89.0% accuracy.
  - [Use of Relative Code Churn Measures to Predict System Defect Density (ICSE 2005)](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/wp-content/uploads/2016/02/icse05churn.pdf) (2005-05): "while absolute measures of code churn are poor predictors of defect density, our set of relative measures of code churn is highly predictive of defect density."
- **proc-3** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Rahman & Devanbu (ICSE 2013) built models across 85 releases of 12 large open-source projects. Code metrics were generally less useful than process metrics and showed high stasis, so models kept flagging the same files, which turned out to be comparatively less defect-dense. Majumder et al. list Moser et al. (ICSE 2008) as reaching a similar conclusion from 1 project. Majumder et al. do not name that project. The claim that it was Eclipse comes from outside that source and was not verified here.
  - [How, and Why, Process Metrics Are Better (ICSE 2013)](https://2013.icse-conferences.org/content/how-and-why-process-metrics-are-better.html) (2013-05): "code metrics, despite widespread use in the defect prediction literature, are generally less useful than process metrics for prediction."
  - [Revisiting Process versus Product Metrics: a Large Scale Analysis](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2008.09569v3.pdf) (2021-10-26): "Moser et al. [53], Arisholm et al. [7], Kamei et al. [34], Rahman et al. [70], Graves et al. [26] and Giger et al. [25] based their conclusions on 1,1,3,12,15,21 projects"
  - Fact-check: ICSE 2013 page https://2013.icse-conferences.org/content/how-and-why-process-metrics-are-better.html: "We build many prediction models across 85 releases of 12 large open source projects"; "code metrics have high stasis; they dont change very much from release to release". Majumder PDF https://arxiv.org/pdf/2008.09569v3.pdf: "Similar conclusions (about the superiority of process metrics) are offered by Moser et al. [53], Giger et al. [25], and Graves et al. [26]." It gives the project count as "1,1,3,12,15,21 projects", and its text does not mention Eclipse for Moser. Caveat: the same Majumder list attributes 15 projects to Graves et al., but Graves studied one switching-system subsystem. Treat Majumder's per-study counts with caution. Web-search budget was exhausted, so I could not open Moser 2008 directly.
- **proc-4** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Majumder, Mody & Menzies (Empirical Software Engineering 27(3), May 2022; arXiv v3 dated 26 Oct 2021) re-checked process versus product metrics on 722,471 commits from 700 GitHub projects. Process metrics won: the best learners reached median recall 98% vs 44% and AUC 95% vs 54%. They warn that metric-importance results from small studies change dramatically at scale.
  - [Revisiting Process versus Product Metrics: a Large Scale Analysis](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2008.09569v3.pdf) (2021-10-26): "process metrics are better predictors for defects than product metrics (best process/product-based learners respectively achieve recalls of 98%/44% and AUCs of 95%/54%, median values)."
  - Fact-check: PDF https://arxiv.org/pdf/2008.09569v3.pdf. The abstract contains the quoted sentence exactly and "using 722,471 commits from 700 Github projects". It also says "we warn that it is unwise to trust metric importance results from analytics in-the-small studies since those change dramatically". The venue year is wrong in the claim. The arXiv abs page https://arxiv.org/abs/2008.09569v3 gives: "Journal reference: Empirical Software Engineering, Volume 27, Issue 3, May 2022" (DOI 10.1007/s10664-021-10068-4).
- **own-1** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Bird et al. (ESEC/FSE 2011) on Windows Vista and Windows 7 found ownership measures (minor-contributor count, top-owner share) related to pre- and post-release failures. Adding the minor-contributor count alone to a size/complexity/churn baseline raised explained variance by +20, +12, +46 and +3 percentage points (Vista pre/post, Win7 pre/post). Adding all ownership measures raised it to 50/44/72/22%, i.e. +24, +15, +48 and +4 points over the 26/29/24/18% baseline.
  - [Don't Touch My Code! Examining the Effects of Ownership on Software Quality (ESEC/FSE 2011)](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/wp-content/uploads/2016/02/bird2011dtm.pdf) (2011): "measures of ownership such as the number of low-expertise developers, and the proportion of ownership for the top owner have a relationship with both pre-release faults and post-release failures."
  - Fact-check: Opened https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/wp-content/uploads/2016/02/bird2011dtm.pdf. The cited quote is present. Table 2 reads: "Base (code metrics) 26% 29% 24% 18% ... Base + Minor 46%∗ (+20%) 41%∗ (+12%) 70%∗ (+46%) 21%∗ (+3%) ... Base + Minor + Major + Ownership 50%∗ (+2%) 44%∗ (+1%) 72%∗ (+1%) 22% (+0%)". So the +20/+12/+46/+3 gains come from Minor alone, not from the ownership measures together.
- **own-2** [empirical-result]: Ownership replications conflict. Greiler et al. (MSR 2015, 4 Microsoft products) confirmed Bird et al., reaching median file-level precision 0.74 and recall 0.38. Foucault et al. (EASE 2014, open source) raised doubts. Koana et al. (2024, 9 Brightsquid projects) found no strong link for minor contributors or total developers.
  - [Code Ownership and Software Quality: A Replication Study (MSR 2015)](https://microsoft.com/en-us/research/wp-content/uploads/2015/05/MSR-2015-Source-Code-Ownership-IEEE_camera-ready.pdf) (2015): "A recent replication study by Foucault et al. [2] on open source software replicate the original results and lead to doubts about the general concept of ownership impacting code quality."
  - [Examining Ownership Models in Software Teams: A Systematic Literature Review and a Replication Study](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2405.15665) (2024-05): "Unlike prior studies, we found no strong correlation between minor contributors and bug numbers."
- **org-1** [empirical-result]: Nagappan, Murphy & Basili (ICSE 2008) found organizational metrics predicted failure-prone Windows Vista binaries (3,404 binaries, >50 MLOC) with average precision 0.87 and recall 0.84 over 50 random splits. Caglayan et al. (ICSE 2015) replicated this on other enterprise software: organizational metrics beat churn but lost to pre-release bug metrics.
  - [The Influence of Organizational Structure on Software Quality: An Empirical Case Study (ICSE 2008)](https://www.cs.umd.edu/users/basili/publications/proceedings/P125.pdf) (2008): "The average precision across all 50 random splits was 0.87 or 87% of the predicted failure-prone binaries that were actually failure-prone."
  - [Merits of Organizational Metrics in Defect Prediction: An Industrial Replication (ICSE 2015)](https://bura.brunel.ac.uk/handle/2438/14620) (2015-05): "models based on organizational metrics performed better than models based on churn metrics but were outperformed by pre-release metric models."
- **coup-1** [empirical-result]: Logical (change) coupling was introduced by Gall, Hajek & Jazayeri (ICSM 1998) using 20 releases of a telecom switching system. D'Ambros, Lanza & Robbes (WCRE 2009) reported that it correlates with defects in three open-source systems, as described by Wiese et al. 2015. Graves et al. 2000 found co-change connectedness unimportant in their models.
  - [Detection of Logical Coupling Based on Product Release History (ICSM 1998)](https://plg.uwaterloo.ca/~migod/846/papers/gall-coupling.pdf) (1998): "We present an approach that uses information in a release history of a system to uncover logical dependencies and change patterns among modules."
  - [An Empirical Study of the Relation Between Strong Change Coupling and Defects ... Apache Aries](https://www.ime.usp.br/~gerosa/papers/Wiese2015_Chapter_AnEmpiricalStudyOfTheRelationB.pdf) (2015): "mined historical data from three open source projects and showed that change couplings correlate with defects extracted from a bug repository."
- **cs-1** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Tornhill & Borg (TechDebt 2022; arXiv 8 Mar 2022) analyzed 39 proprietary CodeScene-customer codebases (30,737 files, 6-12 months of Jira data). Compared with Healthy files, files in the Alert Code Health category averaged 15x more reported Jira defects per file, 124% longer Time-in-Development and up to 9x longer maximum Time-in-Development. Limits: defects are counted per file and not normalized by size; the metric is the vendor's own; the authors concede possible reverse causality. Raw Code Health correlated r=-0.58 with average issue resolution time, against r=0.13 for added LoC (lines added, not file size).
  - [Code Red: The Business Impact of Code Quality - A Quantitative Study of 39 Proprietary Production Codebases](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.04374) (2022-03-08): "By analyzing activity in 30,737 files, we find that low quality code contains 15 times more defects than high quality code."
  - [Code Red: The Business Impact of Code Quality - A Quantitative Study of 39 Proprietary Production Codebases](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.04374) (2022-03-08): "Does low quality code lead to additional defects or is the code quality low because of the many defects?"
  - Fact-check: Opened https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.04374. Both quotes are present. It says "Alert code results in 15 times more reported Jira defects than Healthy code" and "needs, on average, 124% longer". It also says "might require up to 9 times longer Time-in-Development" and "development tasks completed over 6-12 months". The LoC comparison is to added LoC: "We found a low Pearson correlation (r=0.13) between added LoC and the average issue resolution time"; Code Health: "We found a higher degree of correlation (r=-0.58)". The per-file-not-per-LOC limitation is the fact-checker's inference. The paper itself frames RQ1 as defect density but compares defects per file.
- **cs-2** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Borg, Pruvost, Mones & Tornhill (TechDebt 2024, Best Paper Award; three of the four authors work at CodeScene) combined the Code Red data (39 projects) with a second public dataset, UOwns (40 projects): 79 proprietary projects in total. File-level regressions of CodeScene Code Health (1-10) found two patterns. Defects fall for CH ≤ 5 and for CH ≥ 8, with no correlation between 5 and 8. Time-in-Dev falls steadily for CH ≥ 4. A value-creation model built on these regressions shows strong non-linearities at the extremes and increasing (not diminishing) returns at the top end. The authors recommend preventing code smells in high-churn files. The analysis is correlational and file-level, with no controls for file size, churn or coupling. The authors say extrapolating it to project-wide averages is future work, so it does not directly show how a drop in a repo-level score (e.g. 98 to 88) translates into cost.
  - [Increasing, not Diminishing: Investigating the Returns of Highly Maintainable Code](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2401.13407) (2024-01-24): "the value model suggests strong non-linearities at the extremes of the code quality spectrum. Most importantly, the model suggests amplified returns on investment in the upper end."
  - Fact-check: Opened https://arxiv.org/pdf/2401.13407 (v1, 24 Jan 2024). The abstract quote is exact. On the data, the paper says "we extend the dataset with another publicly available dataset with a similar structure [4]. In total, our analysis is based on data from 79 proprietary software projects", made up of CodeRed (39 projects) and UOwns (40 projects). On churn, the abstract says it will "recommend organizations to diligently prevent the introduction of code smells in files with high churn". The best-paper award is confirmed on https://conf.researchr.org/home/TechDebt-2024, which lists "Best Paper Award:" for Borg, Pruvost, Mones and Tornhill. Two parts of the claim are overstated. (1) Defects are not strongly non-linear across the scale. Sec. 5.1 says "There are negative trends for CH ≤ 5 and CH ≥ 8. No correlation appears for 5 ≤ CH ≤ 8", and Time-in-Dev shows "a steady decline for CH ≥ 4". The 'strong non-linearities' are in the value-creation model and only 'at the extremes'. (2) The results are file-level and correlational. Sec. 6 says "Extrapolating file-level results to project-wide averages is an important research direction." The paper says file size and churn "are not represented in the merged datasets" and that coupling is not considered. Three of the four authors work at CodeScene, which sells the Code Health metric. I could not run a search for newer replications because the session's web-search budget was used up.
- **cs-3** [tool-capability]: CodeScene's documentation says it prioritizes hotspots by first finding files with high change frequency and low Code Health. It then raises priority for files that co-change with many modules or are touched by many developers or teams.
  - [CodeScene docs: Technical Debt / Hotspots](https://codescene.io/docs/guides/technical/hotspots.html) (accessed 2026-11-07): "CodeScene starts by identifying files with high change frequency and low Code Health. Then CodeScene factors in other aspects."
- **inf-1** (corrected) [inference]: Inference, with caveats: for per-PR agent gating, Geiger can measure test quality on the diff. The cited evidence supports diff-scoped mutation testing with noise suppression (Google). Google surfaces surviving mutants as actionable review findings and rejects a mutation score as non-actionable, so any score penalty that Geiger derives from mutants is a design choice the source does not validate. Changelist coverage and assertion presence need their own sources. Hotspot and churn data are well suited to weighting where a delta lands (Borg et al. 2024; CodeScene). Process metrics are not limited to that role: just-in-time defect prediction (Kamei et al. 2013) attributes risk to single changes using change-level metrics such as size and developer experience. Agent authorship weakens the experience and ownership metrics among these, which supports keeping ownership and organizational metrics optional. Change-size and diffusion metrics remain usable for each PR.
  - [Practical Mutation Testing at Scale: A view from Google](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2102.11378v2) (2021-02-26): "Our approach performs mutation testing on code changes, considering only changed lines of code"
  - [Code Red: The Business Impact of Code Quality](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.04374) (2022-03-08): "there can be confounding variables such as differences in process [19] and organizational factors [41]."
  - Fact-check: Google paper (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2102.11378v2): the quote is exact ("Our approach performs mutation testing on code changes, considering only changed lines of code"). The paper also describes suppression of arid nodes, which raised productive mutants "from 15% to 89%". It supports diff-scoped mutation with noise suppression, but not as a score: Google says a mutation score is "neither concrete nor actionable, and it does not guide testing", and instead surfaces a restricted set of surviving mutants as code-review findings. Code Red quote (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.04374): exact ("there can be confounding variables such as differences in process [19] and organizational factors [41]."). It only says confounders exist; it says nothing about conflicting ownership replications or agent authorship. Those parts rest on other findings that I did not check here. 'Assertion presence' and 'changelist coverage' have no support in the two cited sources. The premise that process metrics 'do not attribute risk to one change' is contradicted by just-in-time defect prediction. Kamei et al., IEEE TSE 39(6), 2013 (https://posl.ait.kyushu-u.ac.jp/~kamei/publications/Kamei_TSE2013.pdf) say they "consider defect prediction models that focus on identifying defect-prone (“risky”) software changes instead of files or packages". They use "the number of added lines, and developer experience" and report about 68% accuracy and 64% recall. The hotspot-weighting part does have support: Borg et al. 2024 suggest "churn-based filtering and size-based weighting", and the CodeScene hotspot docs rank by change frequency plus Code Health.

### Searches without relevant results

- Foucault, Falleri & Blanc EASE 2014 full text (labri.fr PDF, se.labri.fr page): fetch failed; findings cited only via Greiler 2015 and Koana 2024, WebFetch/curl, 2026-11-07
- D'Ambros, Lanza & Robbes WCRE 2009 PDF (inf.usi.ch/lanza/Downloads/DAmb2009e.pdf and author-site guesses): 404; cited via Wiese et al. 2015, 2026-11-07
- Moser, Pedrycz & Succi ICSE 2008 full text: typeset/scispace returned empty HTML; cited via Majumder 2021 only, 2026-11-07
- D'Ambros, Lanza & Robbes EMSE 2012 defect-prediction benchmark (doc.rero.ch): returned HTML, not PDF, 2026-11-07
- Kochhar et al. 2017 full text (core.ac.uk, ink.library.smu.edu.sg): blocked (403 / Incapsula); abstract read via Microsoft Research page only, 2026-11-07
- Papadakis et al. 2019 mutation testing survey (Advances in Computers): not searched for full text, time budget
- Re-check (holds): Foucault, Falleri & Blanc EASE 2014 full text (labri.fr PDF, se.labri.fr page): fetch failed; findings cited only via Greiler 2015 and Koana 2024 — Partial re-check only. The session's WebSearch budget was exhausted (200-call limit), so neither standard nor extended searches could be re-run. Direct curl guesses also failed: a labri.fr path timed out (code 000), and hal.science/hal-00968426 and hal.archives-ouvertes.fr/hal-00968426 returned 404. No full text found.
- Re-check (holds): D'Ambros, Lanza & Robbes WCRE 2009 PDF (inf.usi.ch/lanza/Downloads/DAmb2009e.pdf and author-site guesses): 404; cited via Wiese et al. 2015 — Re-fetched https://www.inf.usi.ch/lanza/Downloads/DAmb2009e.pdf and https://www.inf.usi.ch/faculty/lanza/Downloads/DAmb2009e.pdf on 2026-11-07; both returned 404. Searches could not be re-run because the WebSearch budget was exhausted.
- Re-check (holds): Moser, Pedrycz & Succi ICSE 2008 full text: typeset/scispace returned empty HTML; cited via Majumder 2021 only — Not independently re-checked. The WebSearch budget was exhausted, and I know no direct URL to try. I am keeping it as an open gap: the statement may hold, but I did not verify it.
- Re-check (holds): D'Ambros, Lanza & Robbes EMSE 2012 defect-prediction benchmark (doc.rero.ch): returned HTML, not PDF — Fetched https://doc.rero.ch/record/31896 on 2026-11-07 and got HTTP 410 (Gone) with an HTML page, not a PDF. This is a guessed record ID; searches could not be re-run because the WebSearch budget was exhausted.
- Re-check (holds): Kochhar et al. 2017 full text (core.ac.uk, ink.library.smu.edu.sg): blocked (403 / Incapsula); abstract read via Microsoft Research page only — On 2026-11-07, https://core.ac.uk/download/pdf/84313133.pdf returned 403. https://ink.library.smu.edu.sg/sis_research/3838/ returned an Incapsula challenge page ("/_Incapsula_Resource"). The Microsoft Research abstract page still opens and supports cov-3.
- Re-check (does not hold): Papadakis et al. 2019 mutation testing survey (Advances in Computers): not searched for full text, time budget — Full text is available. https://mpapad.github.io/publications/pdfs/MutationSurvey2019.pdf (linked from https://mpapad.github.io/publications/) downloaded as a 153-page PDF titled "Mutation Testing Advances: An Analysis and Survey" (Papadakis, Kintis, Zhang, Jia, Le Traon, Harman). Saved to .local/geiger-research/sources/test-quality/papa2019.pdf.

### Open questions

- Does any study validate coverage, mutation or process metrics for Rust specifically (e.g., cargo-mutants, cargo-llvm-cov) or for game/ECS code? None found in this area's sources.
- How do ownership, developer-count and churn metrics behave when most commits are authored by AI agents? No study found.
- Chen et al. 2020 versus Papadakis et al. 2018: which size-control methodology is correct remains disputed; the 2026 LLM replication sides partly with neither.
- Code Red counts defects per file without size normalization; whether the 15x effect survives LOC control is untested in the paper.
- What noise floor (run-to-run variance from flaky tests or nondeterministic mutation sampling) would make a 0.2-point per-PR budget meaningful?

## Inventory of Rust quality-measurement tools

- **rust-1** [tool-capability]: Clippy has 9 lint groups. clippy::all (correctness, suspicious, style, complexity, perf) is on by default. pedantic, restriction, nursery and cargo are allow-by-default, and the README says restriction should never be enabled wholesale. Thresholds are set in clippy.toml: too-many-lines-threshold (default 100), too-many-arguments-threshold (7), type-complexity-threshold (250), cognitive-complexity-threshold (25), excessive-nesting-threshold (0, meaning off).
  - [rust-clippy README](https://github.com/rust-lang/rust-clippy/blob/master/README.md) (accessed 2026-11-07): "The `restriction` category should, *emphatically*, not be enabled as a whole."
  - [Clippy book: Lint Configuration Options](https://doc.rust-lang.org/clippy/lint_configuration.html) (accessed 2026-11-07): "The maximum number of lines a function or method can have"
- **rust-2** [fact]: Correction to the brief: cognitive_complexity is no longer in nursery. Clippy moved it from nursery to restriction in Rust 1.91 (released 2025-10-30); it had been in nursery since 2020 (PR #5428). The lint's documentation says it does not measure true cognitive complexity and points to excessive_nesting and too_many_lines instead. The critique goes back to a 2019 comment on issue #3793 by the lint's own author.
  - [Clippy CHANGELOG, Rust 1.91 section](https://github.com/rust-lang/rust-clippy/blob/master/CHANGELOG.md) (2025-10-30): "Moved [`cognitive_complexity`] from `nursery` to `restriction`"
  - [clippy_lints/src/cognitive_complexity.rs](https://github.com/rust-lang/rust-clippy/blob/master/clippy_lints/src/cognitive_complexity.rs) (accessed 2026-11-07): "The true Cognitive Complexity of a method is not something we can calculate using modern technology."
  - [rust-clippy issue #3793: Suggestion for new lint: Cognitive Complexity](https://github.com/rust-lang/rust-clippy/issues/3793) (2019-10-16 comment): "We aren't really measuring code complexity at all."
- **rust-3** [tool-capability]: Clippy diagnostics are machine-readable through `cargo clippy --message-format=json`. The sarif-rs project's clippy-sarif converts that output to SARIF (latest release 0.8.0, 2025-05-09), and SonarQube imports the same JSON as external issues.
  - [sarif-rs README](https://github.com/psastras/sarif-rs/blob/main/README.md) (accessed 2026-11-07): "cargo clippy --message-format=json | clippy-sarif | sarif-fmt"
- **rust-4** [inference]: Several Rust-specific signals map to Clippy restriction lints that tools can count: panics (unwrap_used, expect_used, indexing_slicing, panic), suppressions (allow_attributes, allow_attributes_without_reason), unsafe hygiene (undocumented_unsafe_blocks, multiple_unsafe_ops_per_block) and Rc<Mutex> (rc_mutex). redundant_clone is in nursery. Inference from scanning the 847 stable lint IDs: no lint flags Rc<RefCell<T>> or counts .clone() calls in general, so those signals need a custom rule, for example in ast-grep.
  - [Clippy lint list (stable)](https://rust-lang.github.io/rust-clippy/stable/index.html) (accessed 2026-11-07): "Checks for attributes that allow lints without a reason."
  - [Clippy lint list (stable): undocumented_unsafe_blocks](https://rust-lang.github.io/rust-clippy/stable/index.html) (accessed 2026-11-07): "Checks for unsafe blocks and impls without a // SAFETY: comment explaining why the unsafe operations performed inside the block are safe."
- **rust-5** (corrected) [tool-capability]: rust-code-analysis computes 16 metric families (ABC, BLANK, CC, CLOC, COGNITIVE, HALSTEAD, LLOC, MI, NARGS, NEXITS, NOM, NPA, NPM, PLOC, SLOC, WMC) and its CLI exports CBOR, JSON, TOML or YAML. Its last crates.io release is 0.0.25 (2023-01-13; the -cli crate too). The repository is not archived and still gets sporadic commits (latest 2026-01-20, after a gap since May 2025), so it is unreleased and slow-moving rather than dead; using it means depending on git or a nearly 4-year-old release. cccc-cli (moznion, syn-based, 1.8.6 on 2026-11-03) is young, with 1,983 total downloads.
  - [rust-code-analysis book: Supported Metrics](https://mozilla.github.io/rust-code-analysis/metrics.html) (accessed 2026-11-07): "rust-code-analysis implements a series of metrics:"
  - [crates.io API: rust-code-analysis](https://crates.io/api/v1/crates/rust-code-analysis) (2023-01-13): ""max_version":"0.0.25""
  - [cccc README](https://github.com/moznion/cccc/blob/main/README.md) (accessed 2026-11-07): "**Rust** (`--lang rust`), via the [syn](https://docs.rs/syn) parser. `.rs`."
  - Fact-check: Opened mozilla.github.io/rust-code-analysis/metrics.html: 'rust-code-analysis implements a series of metrics:', followed by exactly those 16 entries. The commands/metrics page lists 'CBOR JSON TOML YAML'. crates.io API: rust-code-analysis max_version 0.0.25, 2023-01-13. github.com/mozilla/rust-code-analysis/commits/master (WebFetch) shows commits on Jan 20, 2026, e.g. 'feat: Expose inner tree-sitter::Node for advanced use cases (#1210)', with no archive notice. cccc README line: '**Rust** (`--lang rust`), via the [syn](https://docs.rs/syn) parser. `.rs`.' crates.io cccc-cli: 1.8.6 on 2026-11-03, downloads 1983, repo github.com/moznion/cccc.

Line and region coverage work on stable. Branch coverage is unstable and needs nightly. cargo-llvm-cov still lists --mcdc as unstable, but rustc removed all unstable MC/DC instrumentation in August 2025 (rust-lang/rust PR #144999), so MC/DC is effectively unavailable, not merely nightly-only.

It lists --json, --lcov, --cobertura, --codecov, --text and --html; --fail-under-lines/-regions/-functions/-file-lines and --fail-uncovered-lines/-regions/-functions; '--branch Enable branch coverage. (unstable)' and '--mcdc Enable mcdc coverage. (unstable)'. - **rust-7** (corrected) [tool-capability]: cargo-tarpaulin (0.37.6, 2026-11-05) outputs Json, Stdout, Xml, Html and Lcov and has --fail-under. On Linux its default engine is still ptrace, which works only on x86_64; --engine llvm switches to LLVM instrumentation, the default on Mac and Windows. The README calls LLVM 'generally far more accurate', but warns that results 'may differ a lot' between engines in specific cases: tests with non-zero exit codes (e.g. should_panic doc tests, --no-fail-fast), thread unsafety, and fork. grcov (Mozilla) has GitHub release v0.10.8 dated 11 Sep (2026 inferred), but crates.io's latest is 0.10.7 (2026-03-03). It ingests profraw/gcda, LCOV, Go and JaCoCo and outputs html, lcov, covdir (JSON), coveralls(+), cobertura, markdown, ade and files.

  - [grcov README](https://github.com/mozilla/grcov/blob/master/README.md) (accessed 2026-11-07): "*covdir* for the covdir recursive JSON format;"

- **rust-8** [tool-capability]: cargo-mutants (v27.1.0, 2026-06-02) is the maintained Rust mutation tester. It writes mutants.json and outcomes.json with caught, missed, timeout and unviable counts. --in-diff restricts testing to mutants overlapping a diff, but the docs warn that this misses effects outside the changed regions. The docs also say the output format may change between versions. mutagen is effectively abandoned: its last crate release is 0.1.2 (2018-10-10), and its README calls it an architecture preview.
  - [cargo-mutants book: Testing code changed in a diff](https://github.com/sourcefrog/cargo-mutants/blob/main/book/src/in-diff.md) (accessed 2026-11-07): "The `--in-diff DIFF_FILE` option tests only mutants that overlap with regions changed in the diff."
