- **rust-9** [tool-capability]: cargo-nextest (0.9.151, 2026-11-03) detects flaky tests through retries. A test that passes on retry is reported as flaky and, by default, counts as a success. The JUnit report marks such tests with <flakyFailure> or <flakyError> tags. `--flaky-result fail` (or flaky-result = "fail" in config) makes them fail the run.
  - [nextest docs: Retries and flaky tests](https://nexte.st/docs/features/retries/) (accessed 2026-11-07): "Flaky tests are marked with <flakyFailure> or <flakyError> tags."
  - [nextest docs: Retries and flaky tests](https://nexte.st/docs/features/retries/) (accessed 2026-11-07): "By default, flaky tests are treated as ultimately successful."

Its README lists known misses: some unused crates go undetected, and doc-test-only use cannot be checked. cargo-machete (v0.9.2, 2026-04-15) describes itself as fast but imprecise (regex-based). cargo-shear (v1.14.0, 2026-09-22) parses the source without compiling. It also finds misplaced dev/build dependencies (only for integration tests, benches and examples, not #[cfg(test)] unit tests) and unlinked source files, and it offers --format=json for CI.

Both depend on rustdoc JSON, which is unstable and changes often.

- **rust-16** [fact]: SonarQube added Rust on SonarQube Cloud on 2025-04-17; at launch it ran 85 Clippy rules as first-party rules and invoked Clippy automatically. The Server 2026.5 LTA docs describe a different model: Sonar computes cognitive and cyclomatic complexity and duplication, imports LCOV/Cobertura coverage, and imports Clippy JSON as external rules without running Clippy itself.
  - [Introducing Rust in SonarQube](https://www.sonarsource.com/blog/introducing-rust-in-sonarqube/) (2025-04-17): "The Rust analyzer is available today on SonarQube Cloud."

- **rust-17** [fact]: CodeQL Rust support went to public preview in mid-2025 and became generally available on 2025-10-14 (github.com, CodeQL CLI 2.23.3, GHES 3.20+). Semgrep lists Rust as generally available for Code (cross-function dataflow, 40+ Pro rules) and Supply Chain (reachability). Both are security/SAST tools, not maintainability metrics.
  - [GitHub Changelog: CodeQL scanning Rust and C/C++ without builds is now generally available](https://github.blog/changelog/2025-10-14-codeql-scanning-rust-and-c-c-without-builds-is-now-generally-available) (2025-10-14): "Support for Rust and scanning C/C++ without builds is now available on github.com, CodeQL CLI 2.23.3, and on GitHub Enterprise Server starting with version 3.20."
  - [Semgrep: Supported languages](https://docs.semgrep.dev/supported-languages) (accessed 2026-11-07): "Rust Generally available • Cross-function dataflow analysis • 40+ Pro rules"
- **rust-18** [fact]: CodeScene lists Rust with full support (Code Health) and X-Ray (function-level hotspots), and supports separate Code Health rules for tests embedded in Rust files. Qlty's Rust page marks maintainability (complexity, duplication, code smells), linting, security and coverage as supported. DeepSource lists its Rust analyzer as GA (stable Rust 1.25+, up to 1.93) with a configurable cyclomatic_complexity_threshold.
  - [CodeScene: Language support](https://codescene.io/docs/usage/language-support.html) (accessed 2026-11-07): "CodeScene lets you apply custom rules to tests, even when embedded in a file with application code. (Rust only)."
  - [Qlty: Rust Language Support](https://docs.qlty.sh/languages/rust) (accessed 2026-11-07): "Complexity, duplication, code smells"
  - [DeepSource: Core analyzers](https://docs.deepsource.com/docs/analyzers) (accessed 2026-11-07): "Rust 1.25.0 and above on the stable channel is supported. Nightly and beta channels are not supported."
- **rust-19** (corrected) [tool-capability]: ast-grep (0.51.1, 2026-11-02; about 16.5k GitHub stars) runs YAML structural rules through `ast-grep scan`. It outputs JSON (pretty, stream or compact) or, with --format, GitHub annotations or SARIF, and --error/--warning override per-rule severity. Inference, qualified: it is the cheapest engine for syntactic team rules, such as banned patterns or counts of Rc<RefCell<>> or .clone() call sites. It has no type information, scope analysis or data-flow analysis, so it cannot express rules that depend on types or name resolution, for example which type .clone() is called on or semantic ECS invariants. Those need rustc-based lints or CodeQL/Semgrep.
  - [ast-grep CLI reference: scan](https://ast-grep.github.io/reference/cli/scan.html) (accessed 2026-11-07): "Supported formats: GitHub Action, SARIF (Static Analysis Results Interchange Format)."
  - Fact-check: ast-grep scan reference: '--format <FORMAT> Output warning/error messages in different formats. Supported formats: GitHub Action, SARIF (Static Analysis Results Interchange Format). [possible values: github, sarif]'. It lists the JSON values pretty, stream and compact, plus '--error[=<RULE_ID>...] Set rule severity to error' and '--warning[=<RULE_ID>...]'. crates.io: ast-grep 0.51.1, 2026-11-02. github.com/ast-grep/ast-grep page: '16.5k stars'. Independent check, ast-grep FAQ (https://ast-grep.github.io/advanced/faq.html): 'ast-grep at the moment does not support the following information: scope analysis type information control flow analysis data flow analysis'. That limits the 'cheapest engine' inference to syntactic rules.
- **rust-20** [tool-capability]: Uncle Bob's crapper (repository last pushed 2026-10-21; Python-based, installs into .venv) supports Rust. It scores each function as CRAP = CC² × (1 − coverage)³ + CC. For Rust, cyclomatic complexity counts if, loops, match arms, ? and &&/||, and coverage comes from LCOV produced by cargo llvm-cov or tarpaulin. It has --changed (git additions and edits) and --threshold (exit 2), and writes .metrics/crap.edn (EDN, not JSON). The README says renamed or moved functions become new entries.
  - [crapper README](https://github.com/unclebob/crapper/blob/master/README.md) (accessed 2026-11-07): "Rust counts `if`, loops, each `match` arm, `?`, and `&&` / `||`. `mod tests` is skipped. Coverage is LCOV from `cargo llvm-cov` or `cargo tarpaulin`"
  - [crapper README](https://github.com/unclebob/crapper/blob/master/README.md) (accessed 2026-11-07): "Rename or move is a new entry. There is no identity matching across runs."

## Pluggable custom rules and architecture conformance (hard gates), with Rust focus

- **ff-1** (corrected) [term-usage]: Ford's workshop slides (PDF metadata: created 2018-09-13, so it reflects the 1st-edition material) define an architectural fitness function as 'an objective integrity assessment of some architectural characteristic(s)'. They list atomic vs holistic, triggered vs continuous, and also static vs dynamic, automated vs manual, temporal, domain-specific, and intentional vs emergent. So temporal and intentional/emergent are not 2nd-edition additions: the same slides already include them. The 2nd-edition (2023) chapter 2 is still unverified (O'Reilly returns 403). Calling Geiger's aggregate score 'holistic' stretches the term: in the slides, holistic means a check run 'against a shared context' that exercises combined aspects such as security and scalability. It does not mean a weighted sum of atomic results.
  - [Building Evolutionary Architectures Workshop (Neal Ford; slides credit Ford, Parsons, Kua)](https://nealford.com/downloads/Evolutionary_Architectures_workshop_by_Neal_Ford.pdf) (undated (downloaded 2026-11-07)): "An architectural fitness function provides an objective integrity assessment of some architectural characteristic(s)."
  - [Building Evolutionary Architectures Workshop (Neal Ford)](https://nealford.com/downloads/Evolutionary_Architectures_workshop_by_Neal_Ford.pdf) (undated): "run against a singular context and exercise one particular aspect of the architecture."
  - Fact-check: I downloaded https://nealford.com/downloads/Evolutionary_Architectures_workshop_by_Neal_Ford.pdf (131 pages; pdfinfo CreationDate 2018-09-13, title 'Building Evolutionary Architectures Workshop—1 day.key'), extracted the text and found both quotes verbatim (slides 48/73/74). The same 'Categories of Fitness Functions' sequence (slides 79-91) also contains 'temporal: architects may want to build a time component into assessing fitness', 'intentional ... emergent: …some fitness functions will emerge during development of the system', 'static: have a fixed result, such as the binary pass/fail of a unit test', 'dynamic', 'automated', 'manual' and 'domain-specific'. Holistic: 'run against a shared context and exercise a combination of architectural aspects such as security and scalability'. The O'Reilly ch02 page returned 403 to both WebFetch and curl.
- **ff-2** [fact]: The same slides treat metric thresholds and structural checks as fitness functions alike. Examples include a 'Cyclic Dependency Function' built on JDepend and 'Cyclomatic complexity < 50 for all projects' as a maintainability check. Unit tests, metrics, monitors and chaos engineering are all presented as kinds of fitness function.
  - [Building Evolutionary Architectures Workshop (Neal Ford)](https://nealford.com/downloads/Evolutionary_Architectures_workshop_by_Neal_Ford.pdf) (undated): "Cyclomatic complexity < 50 for all projects"
- **rust-1** (corrected) [inference]: The core inference holds. A workspace crate cannot name another workspace crate unless its Cargo.toml declares it. The Book: 'we can't use it in other crates in the workspace unless we add `rand` to their Cargo.toml files as well'. So per-layer crates give compiler-enforced layering at crate granularity. Two caveats are missing. (a) An allowed dependency can `pub use` re-export a forbidden crate, so the manifest gate must check the transitive closure, not only direct edges. (b) Source inclusion bypasses the crate boundary entirely: `#[path = "..."] mod x;` or `include!` can pull another crate's files into domain. The Reference places no limit on the path, but I did not test this. The Reference also notes that sysroot crates (`alloc`, `test`) can be added with `extern crate`, which is harmless for layering.
  - [The Rust Programming Language, ch. 14.3 Cargo Workspaces](https://doc.rust-lang.org/book/ch14-03-cargo-workspaces.html): "Cargo doesn't assume that crates in a workspace will depend on each other, so we need to be explicit about the dependency relationships."
  - [The Rust Reference: Preludes](https://doc.rust-lang.org/reference/names/preludes.html): "provided to the compiler (as with the `--extern` flag with `rustc`) are added to the _extern prelude_."
  - Fact-check: https://doc.rust-lang.org/book/ch14-03-cargo-workspaces.html: the quoted sentence is verbatim; also 'even though `rand` is used somewhere in the workspace, we can't use it in other crates in the workspace unless we add `rand` to their Cargo.toml files as well.' https://doc.rust-lang.org/reference/names/preludes.html: the quote is verbatim ('External crates imported with `extern crate` in the root module or provided to the compiler (as with the `--extern` flag with `rustc`) are added to the _extern prelude_'). https://doc.rust-lang.org/reference/items/modules.html: 'the file path is relative to the directory the source file is located', with no restriction stated, so #[path] can reach files outside the crate (inference, not tested).
- **rust-2** [tool-capability]: `cargo metadata --format-version=1` outputs the resolved workspace dependency graph as JSON. The output includes workspace_members and resolve.nodes[].deps[].dep_kinds, which distinguish dev, build and normal dependencies. Cargo keeps compatibility within a format version, apart from additions such as new fields. A forbidden-edge check, such as 'domain may depend only on these crates', is a small script over this JSON.
  - [cargo metadata (The Cargo Book)](https://doc.rust-lang.org/cargo/commands/cargo-metadata.html): "The resolved dependency graph for the entire workspace."
  - [cargo metadata (The Cargo Book)](https://doc.rust-lang.org/cargo/commands/cargo-metadata.html): ""dev", "build", or null for a normal dependency."
- **rust-3** (corrected) [tool-capability]: The `wrappers` quote is verbatim, and the untested inference ('only infrastructure may depend directly on sqlx/godot') is plausible. Two details are overstated. (1) The docs do not describe phasing out a crate as the use case. That idea appears only as the example reason string 'we want to get rid of this crate but there is still one user of it'. (2) The quote 'Workspace members take precedence over explicit deny entries' applies only when `allow-workspace = true`, which targets deny-by-default policies (`deny = [{ name = "*" }]`). It suggests, but does not state, that workspace members can otherwise be denied. Also, `wrappers` limits only direct dependents of the banned crate. It does not stop domain from depending on infrastructure itself.
  - [cargo-deny: bans config](https://embarkstudios.github.io/cargo-deny/checks/bans/cfg.html): "This field allows specific crates to have a direct dependency on the banned crate but denies all transitive dependencies on it."
  - [cargo-deny: bans config](https://embarkstudios.github.io/cargo-deny/checks/bans/cfg.html): "Workspace members take precedence over explicit deny entries"
  - Fact-check: I fetched https://embarkstudios.github.io/cargo-deny/checks/bans/cfg.html with curl. It contains 'This field allows specific crates to have a direct dependency on the banned crate but denies all transitive dependencies on it.' and the example `{ name = "all-versionsd", wrappers = [...], reason = "we want to get rid of this crate but there is still one user of it" }`. allow-workspace: 'If true, automatically allows all workspace members even when using a deny-by-default policy (i.e., when deny = [{ name = "*" }] is specified)', with default false. cargo-deny is current at 0.20.2 (crates.io, 2026-07-09).
- **rust-4** (corrected) [tool-capability]: The facts are right, but 'together these cover module-level hard gates' is overstated. Rust visibility is ancestor-based: `pub(in path)` requires that `path` 'resolves to an ancestor module'. So it cannot express directional rules between sibling layer modules. An item that adapters need via `pub(super)`/`pub(crate)` is equally visible to a sibling domain module. `cargo modules dependencies --acyclic` detects only cycles, not wrong-direction edges. Directional module-layer rules still need a dependency-rule tool (cargo-bylaw, layered-crate, cargo-pup, rust_arkitect) or a custom lint. cargo-modules is active (0.28.0, 2026-10-18).
  - [The Rust Reference: Visibility and privacy](https://doc.rust-lang.org/reference/visibility-and-privacy.html): "`pub(in path)` makes an item visible within the provided `path`."
  - [cargo-modules README](https://github.com/regexident/cargo-modules): "If found it will search for cycles in the directed graph and return an error for any cycles it found."
  - Fact-check: https://doc.rust-lang.org/reference/visibility-and-privacy.html: '`pub(in path)` makes an item visible within the provided `path`.' and '`path` must be a simple path which resolves to an ancestor module of the item whose visibility is being declared.' The cargo-modules README (raw from github.com/regexident/cargo-modules main) has the verbatim quote 'If found it will search for cycles in the directed graph and return an error for any cycles it found.' The crates.io API gives cargo-modules 0.28.0 on 2026-10-18.
- **rust-5** (corrected) [tool-capability]: cargo-bylaw/bylaw 0.1.0 (2026-08-14; 369/111 downloads) is described as 'an architecture enforcement framework for Rust' with a #[test] API and a `cargo bylaw` command for module/crate/external-crate rules, layer directions and cycles; that part is correct. layered-crate's latest version is 0.4.6 (2026-07-12), not 0.4.3. The cited limitations (pub(crate)/impl, macros, library targets only) still appear in the 0.4.6 README. The ecosystem is also larger than these two tools: Datadog's cargo_pup 0.1.9 (2026-10-28; rustc-integrated ArchUnit-style linting that requires a pinned nightly, nightly-2026-01-22), rust_arkitect 0.3.7 (38.7k downloads, last release 2025-01-25), arch_validation_core/cargo-archtest-cli 0.2.6 (2026-09-22), archaven 1.1.0 and archunit 0.0.2. All are small or young, so 'immature' stands.
  - [cargo-bylaw 0.1.0 (docs.rs)](https://docs.rs/cargo-bylaw) (2026-08-14): "an architecture enforcement framework for Rust"
  - [layered-crate 0.4.3 README](https://docs.rs/crate/layered-crate/0.4.3/source/README.md): "you will get an error since the layer and its dependencies are split into different crates during layer checking."
  - Fact-check: From the crates.io API: cargo-bylaw 0.1.0 created 2026-08-14; layered-crate max_version 0.4.6, released 2026-07-12 (0.4.3 was 2026-01-28). https://docs.rs/cargo-bylaw: 'Cargo Bylaw is an architecture enforcement framework for Rust.' The https://docs.rs/crate/layered-crate/0.4.6/source/README.md quote is verbatim: 'you will get an error since the layer and its dependencies are split into different crates during layer checking.' The DataDog/cargo-pup README: 'Inspired by ArchUnit and ArchUnitNet' and 'you must use this nightly toolchain, as pup depends on compiler internals'.
- **rust-6** [tool-capability]: Clippy's `disallowed-methods`, `disallowed-types` and `disallowed-macros` options ban fully qualified paths, with optional `reason` and `replacement`, without writing lint code. Clippy looks for clippy.toml starting from CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR, so each workspace crate can have its own ban list, for example a domain crate banning godot types or std::time. Clippy marks the configuration file itself as unstable.
  - [Clippy Lint Configuration](https://doc.rust-lang.org/clippy/lint_configuration.html): "The list of disallowed types, written as fully qualified paths."
  - [Configuring Clippy](https://doc.rust-lang.org/clippy/configuration.html): "Note: The configuration file is unstable and may be deprecated in the future."
- **rust-7** (corrected) [tool-capability]: The thresholds and defaults are right (cognitive-complexity 25, too-many-lines 100, too-many-arguments 7), but two points are wrong or missing. (1) The lints do report the raw value: the messages read 'the function has a cognitive complexity of ({cc}/{threshold})', 'this function has too many lines ({line_count}/{threshold})' and 'too many arguments ({args}/{threshold})'. Setting a very low threshold and parsing one run yields per-function values, so no sweep over thresholds is needed. Geiger still parses diagnostics, and values below the threshold are never reported. (2) Clippy itself disowns cognitive_complexity as a metric. It sits in the off-by-default `restriction` group 'so as to not mislead users into using this lint as a measurement tool', and its docs say 'We used to think it measured how hard a method is to understand.' Geiger should not use it as a complexity measure.
  - [Clippy Lint Configuration](https://doc.rust-lang.org/clippy/lint_configuration.html): "The maximum cognitive complexity a function can have"
  - [Clippy Lint Configuration](https://doc.rust-lang.org/clippy/lint_configuration.html): "The maximum number of lines a function or method can have"
  - Fact-check: https://doc.rust-lang.org/clippy/lint_configuration.html: 'The maximum cognitive complexity a function can have' (Default 25); 'The maximum number of lines a function or method can have' (Default 100); too-many-arguments-threshold Default 7. Clippy source, rust-lang/rust-clippy master: clippy_lints/src/cognitive_complexity.rs declares COGNITIVE_COMPLEXITY in group `restriction`, with doc 'This lint has been left in `restriction` so as to not mislead users into using this lint as a measurement tool'. Message formats were checked in cognitive_complexity.rs, functions/too_many_lines.rs and functions/too_many_arguments.rs.
- **rust-8** (corrected) [tool-capability]: Essentially correct, with one detail to fix. Dylint 6.3.2 was released 2026-10-30 (6.2.0, 6.3.0 and 6.3.1 came 2026-10-16/28/29), and the repository is active. It loads lints from user-specified dynamic libraries, which a workspace names under workspace.metadata.dylint.libraries in Cargo.toml or dylint.toml. Lints are not limited to LateLintPass: dylint_linting also offers declare_early_lint! and declare_pre_expansion_lint!. Late lints are the ones with type information. Each library is compiled for a specific toolchain, which appears in its file name, and clippy_utils has no stability guarantees. Additional evidence: the accepted Rust project goal 'Establish a Spot for Safety-Critical Lints in Clippy' rejected Dylint because it 'requires managing lint-driver-compiler version coordination and lacks official Rust Project support'.
  - [trailofbits/dylint README](https://github.com/trailofbits/dylint) (repo pushed 2026-11-07; v6.3.2 2026-10-30): "All you have to do is implement the [`LateLintPass`] trait and accommodate the symbols asking to be filled in."
  - [How Dylint works](https://github.com/trailofbits/dylint/blob/master/docs/how_dylint_works.md): "Dylint tries to build the package with the same toolchain used to build the library."
  - [trailofbits/dylint README](https://github.com/trailofbits/dylint): "Note that, like `rustc`, `clippy_utils` provides no stability guarantees for its APIs."
  - Fact-check: trailofbits/dylint README (raw master): 'runs lints from user-specified, dynamic libraries'; 'All you have to do is implement the [`LateLintPass`] trait and accommodate the symbols asking to be filled in.'; 'either file can contain a TOML array under `workspace.metadata.dylint.libraries`'; 'Note that, like `rustc`, `clippy_utils` provides no stability guarantees for its APIs.' docs/how_dylint_works.md: 'Dylint tries to build the package with the same toolchain used to build the library.' utils/linting/README.md lists 'declare_late_lint!, declare_early_lint!, declare_pre_expansion_lint!'. crates.io gives dylint 6.3.2 on 2026-10-30. Goal text from https://goals.rust-lang.org/print.html.
- **rust-9** [fact]: Marker (rust-marker), which aimed at a stable custom-lint API, appears dormant. Its last release is v0.5.0 (2023-12-28), its last commit is from 2023-12-31, and its README still says the API is unstable. A 2026 search found no Rust project goal for a stable user-lint API.
  - [rust-marker/marker README](https://github.com/rust-marker/marker) (last commit 2023-12-31 (GitHub API, checked 2026-11-07)): "The project is in the early stages of development, some things are still missing and the API is still unstable."
- **rust-10** [tool-capability]: bevy_lint (Bevy CLI working group, unofficial; lint-v0.6.0 released 2026-02-01) is a standalone Clippy-like driver. It needs a pinned nightly (nightly-2026-01-22) with rustc-dev because it imports internal rustc crates via rustc_private. Each lint is a LateLintPass built on clippy_utils.
  - [bevy_lint README](https://github.com/TheBevyFlock/bevy_cli/blob/main/bevy_lint/README.md) (main branch, fetched 2026-11-07): "`bevy_lint` uses [internal `rustc` crates](https://doc.rust-lang.org/nightly/nightly-rustc/) that can only be imported with the permanently-unstable [`rustc_private` feature]"
  - [bevy_lint README](https://github.com/TheBevyFlock/bevy_cli/blob/main/bevy_lint/README.md): "This is an unofficial community project, hacked upon by the Bevy CLI working group"
- **rust-11** (corrected) [fact]: bevy_lint's ECS-specific lints on main (2026-11-07) include: update_schedule and fixed_update_schedule, two restriction lints defined in restriction/schedule.rs that deny the Update or FixedUpdate schedule per module (there is no single lint named 'schedule'); style::unconventional_naming (Plugin types end in 'Plugin', SystemSet types end in 'Systems'); suspicious::unit_in_bundle; suspicious::insert_message_resource; suspicious::iter_current_update_messages; nursery::zst_query (documented false positives on Has<T>/AnyOf<T>); nursery::camera_modification_in_fixed_update; restriction::panicking_methods (panicking World methods, e.g. World::entity vs World::get_entity); restriction::missing_reflect.
  - [bevy_lint schedule lint source](https://github.com/TheBevyFlock/bevy_cli/blob/main/bevy_lint/src/lints/restriction/schedule.rs): "These two lints are useful for denying an unwanted schedule throughout entire modules."
  - [bevy_lint unconventional_naming lint source](https://github.com/TheBevyFlock/bevy_cli/blob/main/bevy_lint/src/lints/style/unconventional_naming.rs): "Checks for types that implement certain Bevy traits but do not follow that trait's naming convention."
  - [bevy_lint zst_query lint source](https://github.com/TheBevyFlock/bevy_cli/blob/main/bevy_lint/src/lints/nursery/zst_query.rs): "This lint raises false positives on queries like `Has<T>` and `AnyOf<T>` because they are ZSTs"
  - Fact-check: Downloaded the TheBevyFlock/bevy_cli main tarball. restriction/schedule.rs declares UPDATE_SCHEDULE and FIXED_UPDATE_SCHEDULE, and its doc says "`fixed_update_schedule`: Disallows using the `FixedUpdate` schedule" and "These two lints are useful for denying an unwanted schedule throughout entire modules." unconventional_naming.rs has a table: Plugin -> "Name ends in \"Plugin\"", SystemSet -> "Name ends in \"Systems\"". zst_query.rs: "This lint raises false positives on queries like `Has<T>` and `AnyOf<T>` because they are ZSTs". panicking_methods.rs: "this will lint against `World::entity()`, recommending that `World::get_entity()` should be used instead." UNIT_IN_BUNDLE, INSERT_MESSAGE_RESOURCE, ITER_CURRENT_UPDATE_MESSAGES, CAMERA_MODIFICATION_IN_FIXED_UPDATE and MISSING_REFLECT are all declared in the stated groups.
- **syn-1** [tool-capability]: ast-grep YAML rules combine atomic, relational (inside, has, follows, precedes) and composite (all, any, not) matchers. They can be scoped with `files` and `ignores` globs and set severity from hint to error. ast-grep has no scope, type, control-flow or dataflow information, so 'type X used in crate Y' cannot be expressed reliably.
  - [ast-grep YAML rule reference](https://ast-grep.github.io/reference/yaml.html): "Glob patterns to specify that the rule only applies to matching files."
  - [ast-grep FAQ](https://ast-grep.github.io/advanced/faq.html): "ast-grep at the moment does not support the following information:"
- **syn-2** [tool-capability]: Semgrep lists Rust as generally available for Semgrep Code, with cross-function (not cross-file) dataflow analysis and 40+ Pro rules. CodeQL Rust support became generally available on 2025-10-14 with CodeQL CLI 2.23.3. The new Rust queries named in the changelogs are security queries.
  - [Semgrep supported languages](https://docs.semgrep.dev/supported-languages) (fetched 2026-11-07): "Cross-function dataflow analysis"
  - [GitHub changelog: CodeQL scanning Rust ... generally available](https://github.blog/changelog/2025-10-14-codeql-scanning-rust-and-c-c-without-builds-is-now-generally-available) (2025-10-14): "Rust joins the list of generally available languages"
- **other-1** [tool-capability]: Architecture-conformance tools in other ecosystems share one model: declare layers or groups, then assert allowed or forbidden dependency directions. Examples are ArchUnit (Java; layeredArchitecture, onionArchitecture), import-linter (Python; forbidden, protected, layers, independence and acyclic-siblings contracts, plus custom contract types), Deptrac (PHP), Konsist (Kotlin; assertArchitecture) and jQAssistant (rules as Cypher queries over a Neo4j graph of the code).
  - [ArchUnit User Guide](https://www.archunit.org/userguide/html/000_Index.html): "No adapter may depend on another adapter."
  - [Import Linter: Contract types](https://import-linter.readthedocs.io/en/stable/contract_types/): "you can define a custom contract type"
  - [jQAssistant user manual](https://jqassistant.github.io/jqassistant/current/): "A Constraint is a query which detects violations"
- **base-1** (corrected) [fact]: ArchUnit's FreezingArchRule stores known violations, reports only new ones, ignores line numbers by default, and automatically shrinks the store when violations are fixed. ESLint bulk suppressions (announced 2025-04-04) store a count per file per rule in eslint-suppressions.json. When the count grows, ESLint reports all violations of that rule in that file. Per the current ESLint docs (not the 2025 blog, which calls it a warning), leftover unused suppressions cause exit code 2 unless --pass-on-unpruned-suppressions is used. ESLint does not shrink the file automatically; the user must run --prune-suppressions. Only ArchUnit ratchets down automatically.
  - [ArchUnit User Guide: Freezing Arch Rules](https://www.archunit.org/userguide/html/000_Index.html): "If violations are fixed, `FreezingArchRule` will automatically reduce the known stored violations to prevent any regression."
  - [Introducing bulk suppressions (ESLint blog)](https://eslint.org/blog/2025/04/introducing-bulk-suppressions/) (2025-04-04): "if more violations are found for the same rule in the same file, ESLint will report **all** of them."
  - Fact-check: ArchUnit guide 8.6: "If violations are fixed, FreezingArchRule will automatically reduce the known stored violations to prevent any regression." and "By default FreezingArchRule will ignore line numbers". ESLint blog (Published 04 Apr, 2025): "if more violations are found for the same rule in the same file, ESLint will report all of them." The blog describes unused suppressions only as a warning: "ESLint outputs a warning when it finds suppressed violations that no longer occur". https://eslint.org/docs/latest/use/command-line-interface: "By default, ESLint exits with exit code 2 and displays an error message if there are unused suppressions in the suppressions file." https://eslint.org/docs/latest/use/suppressions: "To remove the suppressions that are no longer needed, you can use the --prune-suppressions flag." This contradicts the relevance note's "Both ratchet down automatically".
- **base-2** [tool-capability]: Further ratchet and baseline implementations exist. PHPStan's --generate-baseline writes current errors to phpstan-baseline.neon so stricter levels apply to new code. Betterer stores test results in .betterer.results and fails when a value gets worse. dependency-cruiser's --ignore-known lowers the severity of known violations to 'ignore'. jQAssistant has baseline management for existing violations.
  - [PHPStan: The Baseline](https://phpstan.org/user-guide/baseline): "declare the currently reported list of errors as "the baseline""
  - [Betterer: Introduction](https://phenomnomnominal.github.io/betterer/docs/introduction): "If it gets worse, your test will fail and Betterer will throw an error."
  - [dependency-cruiser CLI docs](https://github.com/sverweij/dependency-cruiser/blob/main/doc/cli.md): "For all violations dependency-cruiser finds in the known violations file it will lower the severity to `ignore`."
- **base-3** (corrected) [fact]: SonarQube's built-in 'Sonar way' quality gate applies conditions to new code only: no new issues, all new Security Hotspots reviewed, new-code coverage >= 80.0%, and new-code duplication <= 3.0%. A default fudge factor ignores the duplication conditions until there are at least 20 new lines, and the coverage conditions until there are at least 20 new lines to cover. Project administrators can override it.
  - [SonarQube Server: Introduction to quality gates](https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-server/latest/quality-standards-administration/managing-quality-gates/introduction-to-quality-gates/): "New code test coverage is greater than or equal to 80.0%"
  - [SonarQube Server: About new code](https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-server/latest/user-guide/about-new-code/): "the built-in Sonar way quality gate defines conditions applying to new code only."
  - Fact-check: https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-server/latest/quality-standards-administration/managing-quality-gates/introduction-to-quality-gates/: "The Sonar way quality gate has four conditions: No new issues are introduced All new Security Hotspots are reviewed New code test coverage is greater than or equal to 80.0%" and "Duplication in the new code is less than or equal to 3.0%". On the fudge factor: "The conditions on coverage are ignored until the number of new lines to cover is at least 20." The duplication threshold uses "new lines". about-new-code page: "the built-in Sonar way quality gate defines conditions applying to new code only." The only correction is the coverage threshold: it counts lines to cover, not all new lines.
- **pac-1** [inference]: OPA is a general-purpose policy engine that separates policy decisions from enforcement and takes structured data such as JSON as input. Conftest applies Rego policies to structured files, including TOML and JSON, through deny, violation and warn rules. Inference: Geiger could express gate policies in Rego over Cargo.toml, cargo metadata JSON or its own measurement JSON (deny = hard gate, warn = soft). Teams would have to learn Rego.
  - [Open Policy Agent documentation](https://www.openpolicyagent.org/docs): "OPA decouples policy decision-making from policy enforcement."
  - [Conftest](https://www.conftest.dev/): "Conftest relies on the Rego language from Open Policy Agent for writing policies."
- **synth-1** [inference]: Inference on fit by mechanism. (a) Hard gates: the Cargo dependency graph checked via cargo metadata or cargo-deny wrappers, Clippy disallowed_* lints and cargo-modules --acyclic. These are deterministic and stable-toolchain, with few false positives. (b) Soft scored rules: tools that produce counts or continuous values, such as metric extractors, Clippy threshold lints and ast-grep match counts, combined with a ratchet (Betterer or Sonar new-code style). (c) User-defined domain rules such as ECS conventions: dylint/rustc_private lints when they need types; ast-grep or tree-sitter when syntax is enough. A rule with known false positives, like zst_query, should start as soft or nursery before becoming a gate. This rests on findings rust-1 to rust-11, syn-1, base-1 and base-3.
  - [How Dylint works](https://github.com/trailofbits/dylint/blob/master/docs/how_dylint_works.md): "if two libraries use different toolchains, they may not be applicable to each other."
  - [bevy_lint zst_query lint source](https://github.com/TheBevyFlock/bevy_cli/blob/main/bevy_lint/src/lints/nursery/zst_query.rs): "This lint raises false positives on queries like `Has<T>` and `AnyOf<T>` because they are ZSTs"

## Aggregating measurements into one score, and scoring local/changed code

- **agg-1** [formula]: SIG model (Heitlager, Kuipers & Visser, QUATIC 2007): units are binned by McCabe CC into risk levels (1-10 low, 11-20 moderate, 21-50 high, >50 very high). The share of LOC in each bin forms a 'risk profile', and a lookup table maps it to a rating: ++ ≤25% moderate/0% high/0% very high; + ≤30/5/0; o ≤40/10/0; - ≤50/15/5; else --. Sub-characteristic scores are weighted averages of the relevant property scores.
  - [A Practical Model for Measuring Maintainability (Heitlager, Kuipers, Visser)](https://www.di.uminho.pt/~joost/publications/HeitlagerKuipersVisser-Quatic2007.pdf) (2007): "Thus, to be rated as ++, a system can have no more than 25% of code with moderate risk, no code at all with high or very high risk."
- **agg-2** [fact]: Heitlager et al. reject plain averaging because metrics like complexity follow power-law distributions. Most units are trivial (getters/setters with CC 1), so the mean stays low while the risk sits in a few outliers.
  - [A Practical Model for Measuring Maintainability](https://www.di.uminho.pt/~joost/publications/HeitlagerKuipersVisser-Quatic2007.pdf) (2007): "In general, the use of averaging to aggregate measures on individual system parts tends to mask the presence of high-risk parts."
- **agg-3** [formula]: Alves, Ypma & Visser (ICSM 2010) derive thresholds from a benchmark of 100 Java/C# systems (~12M LOC). They weight each entity by its LOC share in its own system so every system counts equally, pool the systems, and take the metric values at the 70th/80th/90th percentile of code. For McCabe this gives 6/8/14 (low ≤6, moderate 6-8, high 8-14, very high >14). The paper also states '6, 8 and 15' in its motivating example, an internal inconsistency.
  - [Deriving Metric Thresholds from Benchmark Data (Alves, Ypma, Visser)](https://list.inf.unibe.ch/hyperkitty/list/moose-dev@list.inf.unibe.ch/message/5JKRBNHFQI3AFFUZJELPGASSYK4HWQPO/attachment/4/Alves101.pdf) (2010): "the SIG uses thresholds derived by choosing 70%, 80% and 90% of the overall code, which derive thresholds 6, 8 and 14, respectively."
- **agg-4** [fact]: SIG/TÜViT star ratings are relative to a benchmark. Thresholds are calibrated (at least yearly) so systems fall 5%/30%/30%/30%/5% over 1-5 stars; internally the scale runs 0.5-5.5. TÜViT certification adds non-compensatory floors: at least 2 stars on every sub-characteristic and at least 3 stars overall.
  - [Standardized Code Quality Benchmarking for Improving Software Maintainability (Baggen et al., SQM 2010 workshop version; journal version SQJ 2012 not opened)](https://webarchive.di.uminho.pt/wiki.di.uminho.pt/twiki/pub/Personal/Joost/PublicationList/SQM2010-StandardizedCodeQuality.pdf) (2010): "To achieve a certificate, a system must score at least with 2 stars on all sub-characteristics and at least 3 stars on the overall maintainability score."
  - [Meet Sigrid (SIG)](https://www.softwareimprovementgroup.com/sigrid-software-quality-platform/) (undated, accessed 2026-11-07): "It follows a 5%-30%-30%-30%-5% distribution."
- **agg-5** [formula]: Squale (Mordal-Manet et al., 2009) gives each method, class or package an individual mark IM in [0,3]. Marks combine into a practice mark with mark = -log_λ( (1/n) Σ λ^(-IM_i) ), with λ chosen for hard, medium or soft weighting (the comment-rate practice uses λ=20). Example: marks 0.5, 1.5 and 3 have a mean of 1.67 but a weighted mark of 0.93.
  - [The Squale Model – A Practice-based Industrial Quality Model (tech report)](https://inria.hal.science/inria-00637363v1/file/Mord09b-TechReport-Squale.pdf) (2009): "translates individual marks into a new space where low marks have significantly more weight than others."
- **agg-6** (corrected) [formula]: SQALE (Letouzey & Ilkiewicz, IEEE Software 2012 accepted version) computes technical debt as the sum of remediation costs for all non-compliances, called the SQALE Quality Index (SQI). Dividing by size (function points or KLOC) gives debt density. In this paper the explicit ratio-scale argument for adding values is stated for non-remediation costs (the business-impact index). It refers to Letouzey & Coq (2010) for the proof, not to remediation costs.
  - [Managing Technical Debt with the SQALE Method (Letouzey & Ilkiewicz, IEEE Software accepted version)](https://lists.uniroma2.it/index.html/arc/isssr/2014-01/msg00007/IEEE-SW-Managing-TD-with-SQALE-Accepted-version.pdf) (2012): "Technical Debt is the sum of remediation costs for all non-compliances."
  - Fact-check: Opened the lists.uniroma2.it PDF. Verbatim: 'Technical Debt is the sum of remediation costs for all noncompliances. In the SQALE method this debt is called SQALE Quality Index (SQI).' Also: 'divide that amount by the code size (expressed for example in function points or thousands lines of code) to obtain the density'. The ratio-scale sentence concerns non-remediation costs: 'Because the non-remediation costs are not established on an ordinal scale but on a ratio scale, we have shown (10) that we can aggregate the measures by addition'. I could not check whether the SQALE definition document makes the same argument for remediation costs (web search budget exhausted).
- **agg-7** [formula]: SonarQube Server 2026.5 uses two kinds of aggregation. The maintainability rating is compensatory: debt ratio = technical debt / (cost per line × LOC), A ≤5%, B 5-<10%, C 10-<20%, D 20-<50%, E ≥50%. The reliability and security ratings are worst-case: the grade is set by the most severe open issue (A = 0 bugs … E = at least one blocker).
  - [SonarQube Server metrics definition (2026.5)](https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-server/latest/user-guide/code-metrics/metrics-definition/) (2026 (version 2026.5)): "E = at least one blocker bug"
- **agg-8** [tool-capability]: SonarQube scores locality through 'new code'. A project defines new code as changes since the previous version, in the last X days, since a specific analysis, or versus a reference branch; for a PR it is the diff against the target branch. The built-in 'Sonar way' gate requires no new issues, all new hotspots reviewed, new-code coverage ≥80% and new duplication ≤3%. Coverage and duplication conditions are skipped below 20 new lines. Sonar's 2020 'Clean as You Code' post argues that legacy code improves as a side effect.
  - [Introduction to quality gates (SonarQube Server 2026.5)](https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-server/latest/quality-standards-administration/managing-quality-gates/introduction-to-quality-gates/) (2026): "The conditions on coverage are ignored until the number of new lines to cover is at least 20."
  - [Clean as You Code (G. Ann Campbell, Sonar blog)](https://www.sonarsource.com/blog/clean-as-you-code/) (2020-01-20): "By always requiring new code to be issue-free, teams gradually improve the overall codebase quality with each sprint"
- **agg-9** (corrected) [formula]: CodeScene Code Health is per file, from 10 (healthy) to 1, built from 25+ factors. The KPIs are LOC-weighted: Hotspot Code Health over hotspots, Average Code Health over all files, and Worst Performer, the lowest file score. In the docs example (5,000 LOC at 2.0; 100 and 10 LOC at 10.0) the plain mean is 7.33. The weighted mean is printed as 2.18, but the arithmetic gives 2.17. Current official docs and help center give no numeric band cut-offs. Only the Code Red paper (Tornhill of CodeScene and Borg, 2022) puts them at 8.0 (healthy) and 4.0 (alert); it does not say whether the boundaries are inclusive. Per the paper, CodeScene's internal team set them using hand-scored examples.
  - [Code Health – CodeScene docs 6.3.3](https://docs.enterprise.codescene.io/versions/6.3.3/guides/technical/code-health.html) (v6.3.3): "Calculating a pure average gives a code health of 7.33 which is high and non-representative."
  - [Code Red: The Business Impact of Code Quality (Tornhill & Borg, TechDebt 2022)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.04374) (2022-03-08): "The cut-off point for healthy code is 8.0, for alert 4.0, and the range in between represents the warning space."
  - Fact-check: Opened docs 6.3.3 and latest 7.5.16 code-health pages. 6.3.3 has the verbatim quote 'Calculating a pure average gives a code health of 7.33 which is high and non-representative. However,a weighted average gives a code health of 2.18' and '25+ factors'. (2*5000+10*100+10*10)/5110 = 2.172. The 10-1 scale is not on the docs page. It is in the help center https://helpcenter.codescene.com/articles/8038123-what-are-the-different-code-health-scores: 'The maximum score for the 3 KPIs above is 10 (healthy, green code), and the lowest score is 1.0'. The same article says 'The worst performer score represents the code health of one or more files with the lowest code health score'. Code Red arXiv 2203.04374: 'The cut-off point for healthy code is 8.0, for alert 4.0' and 'cut-off points were decided by their internal team via a baseline library of hand-scored code examples.'
- **agg-10** [tool-capability]: CodeScene delta analysis on pull requests (docs v7.5.16) gates on change direction, not absolute level. The 'Code Health Decline' gate fails if any code declines; another gate fails when a new module starts below a threshold; hotspot goals are a third gate.
  - [CI/CD Integration with CodeScene's Delta Analysis (v7.5.16)](https://docs.enterprise.codescene.io/latest/guides/delta/automated-delta-analyses.html) (v7.5.16, accessed 2026-11-07): "This quality gate is triggered if any code declines in code health."
- **agg-11** [formula]: Qlty (successor to Code Climate Quality; docs.codeclimate.com now redirects to docs.qlty.sh) grades projects by technical debt ratio = remediation time / COCOMO-estimated rewrite effort: A <5%, B 5-10%, C 10-20%, D 20-50%, F ≥50%. File and directory grades use a logarithmic function of total debt instead.
  - [Qlty docs – Maintainability metrics](https://docs.qlty.sh/cloud/maintainability/metrics) (undated, accessed 2026-11-07): "At a file and directory level, the grades are calculated using a logorithmic function based on the total Technical Debt."
- **agg-12** (corrected) [formula]: Quamoco (Wagner et al., IST 2015) maps each measure to a utility in [0,1] with a linear increasing or decreasing function. Most measures are normalised, for example by LOC or number of classes; system-level measures such as clone coverage are not. The min/max thresholds come from a benchmark: 110 Java systems from SDS (100 sampled by size class plus 10 consortium systems over 250 kLoC) and 23 C# systems. Values beyond Q1/Q3 ± 1.5×IQR are trimmed as outliers. If fewer than 5 benchmark systems have a non-zero value, the function becomes a jump at 0. Utilities are combined by weighted sum up the hierarchy. Weights come from relevance rankings via Rank-Order Centroid, e.g. 0.25×0.74 + 0.75×0.89 = 0.85. Utility maps to German school grades 1-6, one grade per 2%. Missing data are scored at best and worst case and reported as a range.
  - [Operationalised Product Quality Models and Assessment: The Quamoco Approach (arXiv version of IST 2015)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1611.09230) (2015 (arXiv 2016)): "We use a weighted sum operator as an easily understandable and relatively robust aggregation approach."
  - Fact-check: Read arXiv 1611.09230 in full via pdftotext. Verbatim: 'We use a weighted sum operator as an easily understandable and relatively robust aggregation approach.' Also 'Rank-Order Centroid method [8] to automatically calculate the weights', '0.25 × 0.74 + 0.75 × 0.89 = 0.85', 'decreasing the quality grades by one every 2% until the best possible grade of 1 is reached' and 'using best-case and worst-case values for the missing data and express the uncertainty using the resulting range.' The paper has no 'at least 10 systems' rule. The only size rule is in Appendix A: 'IF |{si=1...n : si > 0}| < 5 THEN min = 0, max = 0.00000001'. On the benchmark: '39 systems were larger than 100 kLoC, 42 ... between 10 and 100 kLoC, and 19 ... between 5 and 10 kLoC ... we included 10 systems of this size class'. C#: 'calibrated the C# part of the base model with 23 open source systems'.
- **agg-13** (corrected) [fact]: ISO/IEC 25023:2016 says most of its quality measures use a measurement function that normalises the result to 0.0-1.0, closer to 1.0 being better; exceptions are noted per measure, and some measures are normalised against a target value from requirements. The free preview does not show whether the standard defines an overall score, so 'no single overall score' is unverified. The Quamoco authors call ISO/IEC 25040's procedures too abstract to be operational.
  - [ISO/IEC 25023:2016 (preview sample)](https://cdn.standards.iteh.ai/samples/35747/34b91bc957f647ce8bbb2093907d7bc0/ISO-IEC-25023-2016.pdf) (2016): "Most quality measures use a measurement function, which normalizes the result value within a range of 0,0 to 1,0. Closer to 1,0 is better."
  - [The Quamoco Approach](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1611.09230) (2015): "provide procedures that are too abstract to be operational (e. g. ISO/IEC 25040 [37])"
  - Fact-check: Opened the iteh.ai preview PDF (15 pages). It says: 'Most quality measures use a measurement function, which normalizes the result value within a range of 0,0 to 1,0. Closer to 1,0 is better. When this is not true, the interpretation is described in a NOTE.' NOTE 2: 'Some measurements are normalized against the target value specified in a requirement specification'. The preview has no text on aggregating measures into one overall score, so the negative claim cannot be checked from it. Quamoco (arXiv 1611.09230) line 78: 'procedures that are too abstract to be operational (e. g. ISO/IEC 25040 [37])'. My web-search budget ran out before I could look for newer 25023/25040 editions.
- **agg-14** [empirical-result]: Vasilescu, Serebrenik & van den Brand (ICSM 2011) compared mean, median and sum with econometric inequality indices on class-to-package SLOC across 106 Qualitas Corpus systems. The Gini, Theil, Atkinson and Hoover indices were highly correlated, so they convey largely the same information. Serebrenik & van den Brand (ICSM 2010) chose Theil because it can be split by groups (e.g. teams or technologies).
  - [You can't control the unfamiliar: A study on the relations between aggregation techniques for software metrics (abstract)](https://search.isc.ac/inventory/49/2333020.htm) (2011): "high and statistically significant correlation between the Gini, Theil, Atkinson, and Hoover indices"
  - [Seeing the Forest for the Trees with New Econometric Aggregation Techniques (ERCIM News 88)](https://ercim-news.ercim.eu/en88/special/seeing-the-forest-for-the-trees-with-new-econometric-aggregation-techniques) (2012-01-02): "the mean is known to become unreliable in the presence of highly skewed distributions"
- **agg-15** (corrected) [fact]: In MCDA, the additive value function V = Σ k_i v_i(x_i) trades criteria off against each other, so it is compensatory. Outranking methods such as ELECTRE I instead accept 'x is at least as good as y' only if a weighted concordance coalition reaches a threshold s and, on no criterion, y beats x by more than a veto threshold v_j. The veto is pairwise: it blocks the outranking assertion between two alternatives. It is not an absolute pass/fail threshold. Geiger's hard gates are absolute limits, closer to a conjunctive or veto rule against a fixed reference profile than to ELECTRE I itself.
  - [Outranking Methods (D. Bouyssou, encyclopedia entry)](https://www.lamsade.dauphine.fr/~bouyssou/OutrankingEoO.pdf) (undated): "It mainly uses ordinal considerations and has a strong non compensatory flavour"
  - Fact-check: Read Bouyssou's 'Outranking Methods' PDF (lamsade.dauphine.fr). Verbatim: 'The most common form for V is an additive value function'. On the concordance-discordance principle: 'It mainly uses ordinal considerations and has a strong non compensatory flavour'. On vetoes: 'non negative veto thresholds vj ... a preference difference is declared "large" as soon as gj(yj) − gj(xj) > vj', which serves to 'reject the proposition xSy'. The source frames the veto as rejecting xSy between two alternatives. It does not use the wording 'fully compensatory'.
- **agg-16** (corrected) [formula]: The Delta Maintainability Model (di Biase, Rastogi, Bruntink, van Deursen; SIG/TU Delft, TechDebt 2019) addresses dilution directly. It classifies each changed LOC as low or high risk using SIG-MM thresholds. LOC increases in the low-risk profile and decreases in the medium, high and very-high profiles count as good. Delta Score per property = low-risk delta / total delta. The DMM score is the mean over 5 properties, in [0,1]. It matched expert judgement on 67 of 100 commits. PyDriller ships an open implementation that covers only 3 of the 5 properties (unit size, unit complexity, unit interfacing; no duplication or module coupling) and is computed with Lizard, so its values can differ from SIG's.
  - [The Delta Maintainability Model: Measuring Maintainability of Fine-Grained Code Changes](https://zenodo.org/records/2606632/files/deltamaintainability.pdf) (2019-03-26): "this leads to small changes to large systems typically having a very minor impact on the maintainability rating."
  - Fact-check: Read the Zenodo PDF. Verbatim: 'this leads to small changes to large systems typically having a very minor impact on the maintainability rating.' Also: 'the Delta Score for each Code Property is the fraction of highly maintainable Low Risk Profile Delta out of the total', 'When the five Code Properties are aggregated (here using mean) ... DMM Score', 'Both the Delta Score and the DMM Score range between 0 and 1', and 'In a manual inspection process for 100 commits, 67 cases matched the expert judgment.' PyDriller docs (https://pydriller.readthedocs.io/en/latest/deltamaintainability.html) list only unit size, unit complexity and unit interfacing. They say coupling and cloned code 'are not easily computed per commit with the Lizard infrastructure.'
- **agg-17** [tool-capability]: Sigrid CI (SIG) rates a pull request by the full contents of the files it touches, not only the changed lines. It tolerates a small number of violations as long as quality stays above an objective, and SIG recommends a warning rather than a build failure.
  - [Sigrid FAQ](https://docs.sigrid-says.com/capabilities/faq.html) (undated, accessed 2026-11-07): "entire contents of those files, not only the lines that you personally wrote."
- **agg-18** [tool-capability]: Lightweight locality tools: diff-cover compares a coverage XML with `git diff` against a compare branch (default origin/main) and fails under a threshold (--fail-under); diff-quality does the same for linter violations. Betterer stores a metric snapshot in `.betterer.results`, fails when it gets worse and tightens the baseline when it improves (a ratchet).
  - [diff_cover README](https://github.com/Bachmann1234/diff_cover) (accessed 2026-11-07): "compares an XML coverage report with the output of `git diff`"
  - [Betterer – Introduction](https://phenomnomnominal.github.io/betterer/docs/introduction) (accessed 2026-11-07): "If it gets worse, your test will fail and Betterer will throw an error."
- **agg-19** [empirical-result]: Evidence for debt-based scores is weak. SonarQube's remediation times were generally overestimated against actual fix times (15 Java projects, 65 novice developers; SEAA 2019). Across 33 Apache Java projects, classes with SonarQube TD items were only slightly more change-prone than clean classes, with no difference in fault-proneness (JSS 2020). For Code Health, the Code Red study reports 15× more defects in Alert files than Healthy files across 39 codebases, but it is co-authored by CodeScene's founder.
  - [On the Accuracy of SonarQube Technical Debt Remediation Time (Saarimäki et al., SEAA 2019)](https://researchportal.tuni.fi/en/publications/on-the-accuracy-of-sonarqube-technical-debt-remediation-time/) (2019): "SonarQube remediation time, compared to the actual time for reducing technical debt, is generally overestimated."
  - [Some SonarQube Issues have a Significant but Small Effect on Faults and Changes (Lenarduzzi et al.)](https://arxiv.org/abs/1908.11590) (2019-08-30 (JSS 2020)): "As for fault-proneness, there is no difference between clean and dirty classes."
- **agg-20** (corrected) [inference]: Inference holds with one caveat. A fixed 0.2-point cap on a LOC-weighted global average loosens as the repo grows, because each change moves the average less. DMM shows this for SIG-MM: a 200-LOC Rhino change moved the rating by -0.007. The proposed design is consistent with the sources: vetoes for hard gates, a per-PR delta score on changed units with fixed, versioned thresholds, and the global score reported separately. Caveat: DMM's delta score is a size-independent proportion in [0,1]. It shows how good a change is, not how much it costs, so it cannot by itself enforce a cumulative budget like '50 PRs cost at most 10 points'. Geiger needs to define how per-PR scores combine with change size or count into the budget. DMM agreed with experts on only 67/100 commits, and PyDriller implements 3 of 5 properties. The claims attributed to agg-3 through agg-9 were not checked in this batch.
  - [The Delta Maintainability Model](https://zenodo.org/records/2606632/files/deltamaintainability.pdf) (2019): "uses the proportion of low risk change to calculate a delta score."
  - Fact-check: DMM PDF: 'uses the proportion of low risk change to calculate a delta score' and 'The impact on the SIG-MM maintainability rating is just −0.007, a negligible delta on a scale of -5 to 5 due to the weighting against Rhino's entire code base.' Also 'changes are always weighted towards the entire system's code base; this was in fact a design goal of the SIG-MM.' The ratio form ('fraction of highly maintainable Low Risk Profile Delta out of the total') makes the score independent of change size. Ratio scores do not add up across PRs, so a budget needs an extra rule.
