  - [Hilton, Bell, Marinov, A Large-Scale Study of Test Coverage Evolution, ASE 2018](https://mir.cs.illinois.edu/marinov/publications/HiltonETAL18TestCoverageEvolution.pdf) (2018-09): "every project had at least one commit where there were occluded changes, indicating that “steady” coverage does not imply “no change” to code covered."
  - [Hilton, Bell, Marinov, A Large-Scale Study of Test Coverage Evolution, ASE 2018](https://mir.cs.illinois.edu/marinov/publications/HiltonETAL18TestCoverageEvolution.pdf) (2018-09): "We see lines which only flip once, all the way up to a single line which flips coverage 128 times."
- **mut-1** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Shi, Bell & Marinov (ISSTA 2019) ran PIT on 30 Java projects that were chosen from projects already studied for flaky tests. They found that about 4% of mutants have an unknown status because coverage is nondeterministic, so the overall mutation score can lie anywhere from 77.7% to 82.0%. That is a bound of about 4 percentage points: the lowest value assumes every unknown mutant survived, the highest that every one was killed. It is not a measured average difference between reruns. 9% of mutant-test pairs had an unknown status. The submitted abstract on the ISSTA program page gives 5 points instead.
  - [Shi, Bell, Marinov, Mitigating the Effects of Flaky Tests on Mutation Testing, ISSTA 2019](https://mir.cs.illinois.edu/marinov/publications/ShiETAL19FlakyMutation.pdf) (2019-07): "mutation scores vary by four percentage points on average between repeated executions, and 9% of mutant-test pairs have an unknown status."
  - Fact-check: The abstract of https://mir.cs.illinois.edu/marinov/publications/ShiETAL19FlakyMutation.pdf contains the quoted sentence verbatim. The body shows how the number was derived: 'The lowest possible mutation score is if all the unknown mutants actually survived, and the highest possible mutation score is if all the unknown mutants are actually killed. As such, the overall mutation score ranges from 77.7% to 82.0%.' The conclusion says these factors 'can account for (on average) a four percentage point variation'. On sample selection: '30 open-source projects selected from projects recently studied in the context of flaky tests'. The ISSTA 2019 program page (conf.researchr.org) has a different abstract: 'mutation scores vary by 5 percentage points on average'. So the 4w-points noise estimate in the relevance note is a worst-case bound on a flaky-test-heavy sample, not a typical spread between reruns.
- **mut-2** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Google's production mutation testing (Petrović et al., arXiv 2102.11378v2, 2021) creates mutants only on changed lines and shows surviving mutants in code review as findings. Like other analyzer findings, they do not need to be resolved before submission 'unless a human reviewer marks them as mandatory'. Developers initially classified 85% of reported mutants as unproductive. Suppression heuristics based on developer feedback raised the productive share from 15% to 89%.
  - [Petrović, Ivanković, Fraser, Just, Practical Mutation Testing at Scale: A view from Google, arXiv 2102.11378v2](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2102.11378) (2021-02-26): "developers at Google initially classified 85% of reported mutants as unproductive."
  - [Petrović, Ivanković, Fraser, Just, Practical Mutation Testing at Scale: A view from Google, arXiv 2102.11378v2](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2102.11378) (2021-02-26): "considering only changed lines of code (Section 2, based on our prior work [12]), and surfacing mutants during code review."
  - Fact-check: Opened https://arxiv.org/pdf/2102.11378 (v2, 26 Feb 2021). The quotes appear verbatim, including 'improved the ratio of productive mutants from 15% to 89%'. On blocking, Section 'Surfacing Mutants in the Code Review Process' says: 'findings do not need to be resolved by the author before submission, unless a human reviewer marks them as mandatory.' So 'does not block merges' is the default, but a reviewer can make a finding blocking.
- **cov-gate-1** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Projects add Codecov tolerances to absorb coverage noise. AnkiDroid uses a project threshold of 0.15 because 0.12 was the largest variability it observed. It still makes both the project and patch statuses informational, so its coverage check does not block merges. CorfuDB added a 0.2% threshold 'to account for flaky tests' (2023-03-20). Other projects use larger tolerances: PostgREST 1% for 'random coverage fluctuations' (2021), and kanonproxy 2% after dependency-only upgrades moved coverage by 0.15 to 1.02 points (2026-03-15). Codecov's recipe page suggests a 10% threshold 'based on how flaky your tests are'. Observed tolerances therefore range from about 0.15 to 2 points or more, and the cited evidence does not show a typical value of 0.1 to 0.2.
  - [AnkiDroid .codecov.yml (main branch, read 2026-11-07)](https://github.com/ankidroid/Anki-Android/blob/main/.codecov.yml) (2026-11-07): ".12 was observed as the largest variability owing to timing forcing different test branch choice"
  - [CorfuDB commit: Codecov allow 0.2% coverage threshold (#3553)](https://github.com/CorfuDB/CorfuDB/commit/b84ef873227b2616d2e36666ef06f4fe8c1254de) (2023-03-20): "Add 0.2% code coverage threshold to account for flaky tests."
  - [Codecov docs: Commit Status](https://docs.codecov.com/docs/commit-status) (2026-11-07): "Allow the coverage to drop by <number>% , and posting a success status."
  - Fact-check: Opened the raw AnkiDroid .codecov.yml: '.12 was observed as the largest variability owing to timing forcing different test branch choice', 'threshold: 0.15', 'informational: true' on both project and patch. Opened the CorfuDB patch, dated Mon, 20 Mar 2023: 'Add 0.2% code coverage threshold to account for flaky tests.' https://docs.codecov.com/docs/commit-status: 'Allow the coverage to drop by <number>%, and posting a success status.' https://docs.codecov.com/docs/common-recipe-list: '# adjust accordingly based on how flaky your tests are # this allows a 10% drop from the previous base commit coverage threshold: 10%'. https://github.com/PostgREST/postgrest/issues/1800: 'We introduced a 1% threshold to silence the random coverage fluctuations'. https://github.com/compscidr/kanonproxy/pull/227: 'failing codecov/project due to minor coverage fluctuations (0.15-1.02%) caused by dependency version changes, not source code changes'.
- **cov-gate-2** [tool-capability]: SonarQube's default 'fudge factor' ignores quality-gate conditions on coverage and duplication until new code has at least 20 lines. Sonar says the aim is to avoid overly strict enforcement on small changes.
  - [SonarQube Server docs: Introduction to quality gates](https://docs.sonarsource.com/sonarqube-server/latest/quality-standards-administration/managing-quality-gates/introduction-to-quality-gates/) (2026-11-07): "The conditions on coverage are ignored until the number of new lines to cover is at least 20."
- **tool-tarpaulin** [tool-capability]: The tarpaulin README warns that ptrace and LLVM engines can give very different coverage results. It says LLVM coverage returns no data when a test exits non-zero and that fork-like syscalls overwrite profraw files.
  - [cargo-tarpaulin README (read 2026-11-07)](https://github.com/xd009642/tarpaulin) (2026-11-07): "In these cases coverage results may differ a lot between ptrace and llvm and llvm coverage may be a worse choice."
- **tool-mutants** [tool-capability]: cargo-mutants sets test timeouts relative to the baseline: by default 5× baseline test time, minimum 20 s. Build timeouts are off by default because build time varies and timeouts would make builds flaky. Unviable mutants are 'inconclusive'. Exit code 3 reports timeouts. The docs warn that --in-diff can miss regressions in code outside the diff and ignores test-only diffs.
  - [cargo-mutants book: Hangs and timeouts](https://mutants.rs/timeouts.html) (2026-11-07): "The default test timeout is 5 times the baseline test time, with a minimum of 20 seconds."
  - [cargo-mutants book: Hangs and timeouts](https://mutants.rs/timeouts.html) (2026-11-07): "because build time can be quite variable there’s some risk of this causing builds to be flaky, and so it’s off by default."
  - [cargo-mutants book: Testing code changed in a diff](https://mutants.rs/in-diff.html) (2026-11-07): "it’s certainly possible that edits in one region cause code in a different region or a different file to no longer be well tested."
- **tool-nextest** [tool-capability]: cargo-nextest marks a test that passes on retry as flaky and by default treats it as successful. Since nextest 0.9.131 it can fail the run on flaky tests with --flaky-result fail or flaky-result = "fail".
  - [cargo-nextest docs: Retries and flaky tests](https://nexte.st/docs/features/retries/) (2026-11-07): "By default, flaky tests are treated as ultimately successful."
  - [cargo-nextest docs: Retries and flaky tests](https://nexte.st/docs/features/retries/) (2026-11-07): "To fail a test run on flaky tests, pass in --flaky-result fail or set NEXTEST_FLAKY_RESULT=fail in the environment."
- **clippy-1** [fact]: Every 6-week stable Rust release changes Clippy. Rust 1.92–1.99 added 1–8 new lints each, with 11–25 enhancements and 4–11 false-positive fixes per release; Rust 1.90 alone had 43 false-positive fixes. Rust 1.99 has been the current stable since 2026-10-01.
  - [rust-clippy CHANGELOG.md (read 2026-11-07)](https://github.com/rust-lang/rust-clippy/blob/master/CHANGELOG.md) (2026-11-07): "Current stable, released 2026-10-01"
  - [rust-clippy CHANGELOG.md, Rust 1.95 section](https://github.com/rust-lang/rust-clippy/blob/master/CHANGELOG.md) (2026-04-16): "Added [`unnecessary_trailing_comma`] to `pedantic`"
- **clippy-2** [empirical-result]: In Li et al.'s 2023 survey of 31 Rust developers, 42% complained that Clippy produces many and frequent false positives. 68% configure lints as 'warn' and 16% as 'deny'.
  - [Li et al., Unleashing the Power of Clippy in Real-World Rust Projects, arXiv 2310.11738](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.11738) (2023-10-18): "a lot of (42%) participants complained that Clippy produce relatively many and frequent false positives."
- **llm-1** [empirical-result]: LLM outputs are not repeatable. Ouyang et al. found that ChatGPT gave no two requests with equal test output on 75.76%, 51.00% and 47.56% of tasks in three benchmarks, and that temperature 0 does not guarantee determinism. Haldar & Hockenmaier (EMNLP Findings 2025) found that LLM judges have low intra-rater reliability across runs. That study covered natural-language generation, not code.
  - [Ouyang, Zhang, Harman, Wang, An Empirical Study of the Non-determinism of ChatGPT in Code Generation, arXiv 2308.02828v2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.02828) (2024-10-17): "setting the temperature to 0 does not guarantee determinism in code generation"
  - [Haldar & Hockenmaier, Rating Roulette: Self-Inconsistency in LLM-As-A-Judge Frameworks, Findings of EMNLP 2025](https://aclanthology.org/2025.findings-emnlp.1361.pdf) (2025): "we show that LLM judges have low intra-rater reliability in their assigned scores across different runs."
- **llm-2** (corrected) [tool-capability]: Qlty SlopOne (free research preview, blog post 2026-09-27) combines static-analysis signals with decisions from Jev. Its CLI has --offline ("Use existing cached analyses; make no API calls") and also a built-in per-file gate, --max-drop, default 1 point. Qlty publishes no run-to-run stability figures and says it will publish its methodology and measurements "soon". Issue #2863, closed 2026-09-25 by PR #2865, is about a test that compared SlopOne trend-report explanation values bit-exactly against a Python fixture. Two values differed in about the 17th significant digit on x86_64 macOS and musl targets. The libm `exp` cause was suspected but not reproduced. The fix loosened the test to a 1e-12 tolerance, so cross-platform last-bit differences can remain, but they are far too small to matter for a 0.2-point budget. desloppify scores subjective dimensions with an LLM, and its `--profile ci` skips slow and subjective phases to collect a mechanical snapshot.
  - [Qlty blog: SlopOne: Winning the code quality fight with Jev](https://qlty.sh/blog/introducing-slopone) (2026-09-27): "combines signals from traditional static analysis with decisions from Jev."
  - [Qlty docs: slop-one command](https://docs.qlty.sh/cli/commands/slop-one) (2026-11-07): "Use existing cached analyses; make no API calls"
  - [qlty issue #2863: explanation parity test fails on musl and x86_64 macOS](https://github.com/qltysh/qlty/issues/2863) (2026): "The platform's libm returns a different last bit for `exp`"
  - [desloppify README (read 2026-11-07)](https://github.com/peteromallet/desloppify) (2026-11-07): "uses an LLM to assess subjective quality dimensions"
  - Fact-check: Opened https://qlty.sh/blog/introducing-slopone: "combines signals from traditional static analysis with decisions from Jev." The post also says "releasing SlopOne as a free research preview" and "We'll publish the methodology and our measurements soon." Opened https://docs.qlty.sh/cli/commands/slop-one: --offline "Use existing cached analyses; make no API calls" and --max-drop "Largest score drop allowed before a changed file counts as declined (default: 1)". Fetched issue #2863 via the GitHub API. The failing test is `fixture_export_matches_python` in qlty-slop-one-trends, with "0.24937869046968375 != 0.24937869046968372". The issue says "The platform's libm returns a different last bit for `exp`" and "This cause has not been reproduced yet." PR #2865: "compares numbers within 1e-12 relative or 1e-12 absolute" and "The root cause... was not reproduced." desloppify README: "`--profile ci` skips slow and subjective phases" and "Review uses an LLM to assess subjective quality dimensions".

### Searches without relevant results

- WebSearch 'Qlty SlopOne LLM code quality score docs' (standard, 2026-11-07): no SlopOne page found; the extended query 'qlty "SlopOne"' found it
- No source states SlopOne or desloppify run-to-run variance: Qlty docs, the SlopOne blog and the desloppify README were read on 2026-11-07 and are silent on stability
- WebSearch 'rust coverage percentage changed after upgrading rustc version llvm-cov regions different "instrument-coverage" issue' (extended, 2026-11-07): no source measured coverage drift across rustc releases; only format mismatches and region-count bugs (results not opened or cited)
- WebSearch 'codecov threshold "flaky" OR "fluctuate" coverage noise project status' (standard, 2026-11-07): Codecov's own docs give no rationale or default for threshold; only project configs (AnkiDroid, CorfuDB) document noise-based thresholds
- WebFetch of the Google Testing Blog flaky-tests post (2026-11-07) returned only comments; the figures came from a curl fetch of the same URL
- WebFetch of the cacm.acm.org Sadowski article returned 403 (2026-11-07); the MIT-hosted PDF was used instead
- WebSearch 'Shi Bell Marinov "Mitigating the effects of flaky tests on mutation testing"' (standard, 2026-11-07) failed because the session's web-search budget was exhausted; the PDF was taken from Marinov's publication page
- Not run: Dagger (CLI not installed), cargo-tarpaulin (not installed; its ptrace engine is Linux x86_64 only), coverage on Rust 1.92 (llvm-tools component missing), consecutive releases 1.93/1.94 (not installed); installing them would have modified the user's toolchain setup
- Re-check (holds): WebSearch 'Qlty SlopOne LLM code quality score docs' (standard): no SlopOne page found; the extended query 'qlty "SlopOne"' found it — Re-ran both on 2026-11-07. The standard search returned Qlty metrics/changelog pages and unrelated tools, with no SlopOne page. The extended search returned qlty.sh/blog/introducing-slopone, docs.qlty.sh/cli/commands/slop-one and qltysh/qlty PRs #2855, #2862 and #2930.
- Re-check (holds): No source states SlopOne or desloppify run-to-run variance (Qlty docs, SlopOne blog, desloppify README silent on stability) — Re-read the SlopOne blog and the raw desloppify README (peteromallet/desloppify, main branch). Neither contains determinism, variance, reproducibility or rerun wording. Related finding: qltysh/qlty issue #2863 (2026-09-25) shows SlopOne trend values differing only in the last float bit across platforms ('0.24937869046968375 != 0.24937869046968372'; musl/x86_64 macOS libm 'exp' rounding). That is a cross-platform difference of negligible size, not a statement about run-to-run variance.
- Re-check (holds): Extended WebSearch on rustc upgrade coverage drift: no source measured coverage drift across rustc releases; only format mismatches and region-count bugs — Re-ran the extended query. Results covered profraw/format version mismatches (hermeticbuild/rules_rs #340, LLVM 23/rustc 1.95), rustc book docs and tool switches, and no measured drift across rustc releases. Adjacent non-Rust evidence: kanonproxy PR #227 (Kotlin/Gradle, 2026-03-15) reports coverage shifts of 0.15 to 1.02 points from dependency/toolchain upgrades alone.
- Re-check (does not hold): Codecov's own docs give no rationale or default for threshold; only project configs (AnkiDroid, CorfuDB) document noise-based thresholds — Codecov's docs give no default, but they do give a rationale. https://docs.codecov.com/docs/common-recipe-list, under 'Relax my coverage target, because I have flaky tests', says: '# adjust accordingly based on how flaky your tests are # this allows a 10% drop'. More project configs document noise-based thresholds: PostgREST issue #1800 (1%, 'random coverage fluctuations', 2021) and kanonproxy PR #227 (2%, 2026).
- Re-check (holds): WebFetch of the Google Testing Blog flaky-tests post returned only comments; figures came from a curl fetch — Re-ran WebFetch on 2026-11-07. It again reported that the article body was missing (title, date, comments only). A curl fetch contains both the 1.5% and the 84% sentences.
- Re-check (holds): WebFetch of the cacm.acm.org Sadowski article returned 403; the MIT-hosted PDF was used instead — Re-ran WebFetch on https://cacm.acm.org/research/lessons-from-building-static-analysis-tools-at-google/ and got HTTP 403 Forbidden. The MIT PDF opened and contains the quotes.
- Re-check (holds): WebSearch for Shi/Bell/Marinov paper failed due to exhausted search budget; PDF taken from Marinov's publication page — The re-run search now succeeds and returns par.nsf.gov, the ISSTA program page, figshare and the ACM DOI, so the earlier failure was transient. The ISSTA program page abstract gives '5 percentage points' while the published PDF gives 'four percentage points'. Cite the published version.

### Open questions

- How large is the noise on Linux CI runners inside Dagger, with cache volumes for target/ and the cargo registry, compared with native macOS runs?
- How much does coverage (lines and regions) drift between consecutive stable releases (e.g., 1.98 to 1.99) on the same commit? This needs llvm-tools on both toolchains; the local default stable is 1.92 while current stable is 1.99.
- What run-to-run variance do SlopOne (uncached) and desloppify's subjective review show on an unchanged Rust file? No vendor figures exist; measure with 10 or more runs.
- What share of cargo-mutants outcomes become timeouts under heavy CI load for crates with timing-sensitive tests?
- Gate design: should the 0.2 budget be compared against the paired delta minus k×(measured SD) per component, and how many reruns per PR are affordable?

## Which existing products already deliver per-change quality scores, regression gates or bundled agent-loop checks that overlap Geiger, and how far does each go? Cover at least aislop, DRS-OSS, GitHub Copilot cloud agent's built-in validation, Cursor Bugbot, Claude Code Code Review and OpenAI Codex review. For each: deterministic or LLM-judged; whether teams can add architecture or domain rules; Rust depth; whether a non-zero per-PR budget on an aggregate score can be set; pricing; adoption.

- **aislop-1** [tool-capability]: aislop (MIT CLI, v0.20.0 released 2026-11-01) gives a deterministic 0-100 score per scan with no LLM in the scoring path. The score aggregates findings from six engines (formatting, linting, code quality, AI slop, security, architecture) with configurable engine weights, rule impact tiers and per-rule caps.
  - [aislop README (fetched 2026-11-07)](https://github.com/scanaislop/aislop/blob/main/README.md) (2026-11-07): "Scores every change 0–100. Sub-second. Deterministic: no LLM in the runtime path, same code in, same score out."
  - [aislop docs/scoring.md](https://github.com/scanaislop/aislop/blob/main/docs/scoring.md) (2026-11-07): "Penalties are multiplied by the engine weight (configurable in `.aislop/config.yml`)."
- **aislop-2** [tool-capability]: aislop scopes both scan and CI to the files a PR changes (`ci --changes --base origin/main`), and the score gate applies to that changed-file scan. The score is normalised by file count, so on a full scan a few findings in a large repository move it little.
  - [aislop docs/ci.md, PR-scoped gating](https://github.com/scanaislop/aislop/blob/main/docs/ci.md) (2026-11-07): "Both `scan` and `ci` accept `--changes` and `--base`, so you can gate a PR on only the files it touches"
  - [aislop docs/scoring.md, Density normalization](https://github.com/scanaislop/aislop/blob/main/docs/scoring.md) (2026-11-07): "Penalties are measured relative to the number of production and test files in the project"
- **aislop-3** (corrected) [tool-capability]: aislop has no configurable non-zero per-change budget. CiSchema allows only failBelow, format, failOnMissingTools and baseline. The quality-gate hook marks any score drop as a regression (`delta < 0`). It blocks only the Claude Code Stop hook, and only once per turn: it returns early when `stop_hook_active` is set. `aislop ci` fails when the score is below the absolute `failBelow` or when any error-severity diagnostic is present. With a baseline, it also fails on any new finding, whatever its severity. A hard-coded REGRESSION_FLAG_THRESHOLD = 5 exists, but it only changes the suggested-action text, not the gate.
  - [aislop src/hooks/feedback.ts](https://github.com/scanaislop/aislop/blob/main/src/hooks/feedback.ts) (2026-11-07): "const regressed = typeof delta === "number" ? delta < 0 : false;"
  - [scanaislop docs: hooks, Quality-gate mode](https://scanaislop.com/docs/hooks) (2026-11-07): "Non-Claude agents still receive the regressed flag in feedback; only the Claude Stop hook blocks."
  - [aislop docs/ci.md, Baseline](https://github.com/scanaislop/aislop/blob/main/docs/ci.md) (2026-11-07): "CI exits 1 when any finding is not in the baseline, whatever its severity, or when the score drops below `failBelow`."
  - Fact-check: src/hooks/feedback.ts line 236: 'const regressed = typeof delta === "number" ? delta < 0 : false;'. Line 78: 'const REGRESSION_FLAG_THRESHOLD = 5;', used only to build suggested actions. src/hooks/adapters/claude.ts: 'if (input.stop_hook_active) return 0;', then it blocks if feedback.regressed. src/config/schema.ts CiSchema: 'failBelow: z.number().default(70), format..., failOnMissingTools..., baseline: z.string().min(1).optional()'. docs/ci.md (https://github.com/scanaislop/aislop/blob/main/docs/ci.md): 'The CI command exits 1 when the score drops below `failBelow`, or when any error-severity diagnostic is present.' scanaislop.com/docs/hooks contains the quoted 'Non-Claude agents still receive the regressed flag in feedback; only the Claude Stop hook blocks.' The core claim (no budget) holds. The claim omitted the error-severity failure condition.
- **aislop-4** [tool-capability]: aislop's opt-in architecture rules (forbid_import, forbid_import_from_path) extract imports only from JS/TS, Python and Go files. On Rust files the import list is empty, so layer bans never fire. require_pattern is a whole-file substring check, not a regex or AST check.
  - [aislop src/engines/architecture/matchers.ts (main, last changed 2026-06-29)](https://github.com/scanaislop/aislop/blob/main/src/engines/architecture/matchers.ts) (2026-11-07): "if ([".ts", ".tsx", ".js", ".jsx", ".mjs", ".cjs"].includes(ext)) {"
  - [aislop src/engines/architecture/matchers.ts, applyRequirePattern](https://github.com/scanaislop/aislop/blob/main/src/engines/architecture/matchers.ts) (2026-11-07): "if (content.includes(rule.pattern)) return [];"
- **aislop-5** [inference]: Inference from reading the source: aislop's shipped example rules file uses keys `module` and `path`, but the rule loader's type defines only `match`, `from`, `forbid`, `pattern` and `where` and passes the parsed YAML through unchanged. The example rules would therefore silently produce no findings. This rests on code reading only; I did not run aislop.
  - [aislop examples/architecture-rules.yml](https://github.com/scanaislop/aislop/blob/main/examples/architecture-rules.yml) (2026-11-07): "- type: forbid_import_from_path
    from: "src/controllers/**"
    module: "src/database/**""
  - [aislop src/engines/architecture/rule-loader.ts](https://github.com/scanaislop/aislop/blob/main/src/engines/architecture/rule-loader.ts) (2026-11-07): "match?: string;
	from?: string;
	forbid?: string;
	pattern?: string;
	where?: string;"
- **aislop-6** (corrected) [tool-capability]: aislop's Rust coverage is shallow: cargo fmt, clippy, a complexity check, comment-based AI-slop rules plus `ai-slop/rust-non-test-unwrap` and `ai-slop/rust-todo-stub`, secrets, and audit. Its Rust function-length measure counts braces without masking Rust strings and comments, because main's masker has no family for `.rs`. An open pull request (#412, opened 2026-09-29, unmerged) reproduces a 4-line function measured as 167 lines. The same PR states that generic Rust functions (`fn f<'a>(...)`) are not length-checked at all.
  - [aislop docs/rules.md, Supported Languages table](https://github.com/scanaislop/aislop/blob/main/docs/rules.md) (2026-11-07): "| Rust | cargo fmt | clippy | complexity | Comments | Secrets, audit |"
  - [aislop #412 fix(masker): mask Rust strings and comments (open)](https://github.com/scanaislop/aislop/issues/412) (2026-09-29): "`.rs` has no family in `familyForExt`, so `maskStringsAndComments` returns Rust source unmasked."
  - Fact-check: docs/rules.md: '| Rust | cargo fmt | clippy | complexity | Comments | Secrets, audit |'; it also lists ai-slop/rust-non-test-unwrap and ai-slop/rust-todo-stub. The GitHub API shows #412 is a pull request (state open, merged_at null, opened 2026-09-29T08:44:01Z), not an issue. Body: '`.rs` has no family in `familyForExt`, so `maskStringsAndComments` returns Rust source unmasked.' and 'The Rust fn regex doesn't match generic functions (`fn f<'a>(...)`), so their length isn't checked at all.' Current main src/utils/source-masker.ts familyForExt has no .rs entry (C_STYLE_COMMENT_EXTS = ['.go']).
- **aislop-7** [market]: aislop adoption and pricing: the MIT CLI is free. The hosted scanaislop tiers are Solo $29, Studio $99 (custom thresholds and PR comments) and Business $249 per month, and the CLI-vs-platform table lists blocking merges below a threshold as a platform feature. The repository was created 2026-03-06 and had 731 stars on 2026-11-07. npm counted 82,052 downloads from 2026-10-08 to 2026-11-06 and PyPI 9,299 in the last month. npm counts include `npx aislop@latest` runs in CI, so they overstate the number of users.
  - [aislop pricing: free CLI, hosted plans from $29](https://scanaislop.com/pricing) (2026-11-07): "Custom thresholds + PR comments"
  - [npm downloads API, aislop last month](https://api.npmjs.org/downloads/point/last-month/aislop) (2026-11-07): "{"downloads":82052,"start":"2026-10-08","end":"2026-11-06","package":"aislop"}"
- **drs-1** [empirical-result]: DRS-OSS (arXiv 2511.21964 v3, 2026-06-07) is an ML-judged diff-risk tool. A fine-tuned Llama 3.1 8B classifier, trained on ApacheJIT (14 Apache projects, 106,674 commits), returns a risky/safe label and a confidence through a GitHub App comment. It takes no team-written rules. Its code repository had 3 stars on 2026-11-07. It is unlikely to know Rust, since its training data comes from Apache projects (inference).
  - [Sayedsalehi, Rigby, Mockus: DRS-OSS: A Diff-Risk Scoring Tool for Continuous Integration Workflows (v3)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.21964) (2026-06-07): "The bot posts a compact card: commit/PR identifiers, derived label (risky/safe), and confidence score."
  - [DRS-OSS paper, Section on dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.21964) (2026-06-07): "spanning 14 Apache projects with 106,674 commits (28,239 bug-inducing and 78,435 clean commits)"
- **copilot-1** (corrected) [tool-capability]: By default, Copilot cloud agent runs CodeQL, Advisory Database checks on new dependencies, secret scanning and Copilot code review (an LLM review), and tries to fix the issues before it completes the PR. No GHAS license is needed. Repository admins can toggle each tool off. Per GitHub's 2026-03-18 changelog, the agent also automatically runs the project's own tests and linter. Team rules written as tests or lints (for example clippy, or architecture tests) therefore already run in the loop. The docs describe no score, no threshold and no per-PR score budget.
  - [Risks and mitigations for GitHub Copilot cloud agent (read 2026-11-07, undated)](https://docs.github.com/en/copilot/concepts/security-governance-and-network-settings/risks-and-mitigations) (2026-11-07): "does not require a GitHub Secret Protection, GitHub Code Security, or GitHub Advanced Security license."
  - [Risks and mitigations for GitHub Copilot cloud agent](https://docs.github.com/en/copilot/concepts/security-governance-and-network-settings/risks-and-mitigations) (2026-11-07): "It attempts to resolve issues identified prior to completing the pull request."
  - Fact-check: The risks-and-mitigations page contains both quoted sentences: 'does not require a GitHub Secret Protection, GitHub Code Security, or GitHub Advanced Security license.' and 'It attempts to resolve issues identified prior to completing the pull request.' configuring-agent-settings: 'You must be a repository administrator to configure these settings.' https://github.blog/changelog/2026-03-18-configure-copilot-coding-agents-validation-tools/ : 'When Copilot coding agent writes code, it automatically runs your project’s tests and linter.' The claim's 'no team-defined rule gate' is therefore misleading. Team-defined tests and linters do run, but not as a configurable validation tool or score gate.
- **copilot-2** (corrected) [tool-capability]: Copilot cloud agent runs repository-defined hooks from `.github/hooks/*.json` and supports a subset of events, including sessionStart, preToolUse, postToolUse, agentStop and subagentStop. A `preToolUse` hook can approve or deny tool executions. Per the hooks reference, an `agentStop` hook under cloud agent can return `decision: "block"`, which forces another agent turn using `reason` as the prompt. The extra turn counts against the job timeout, and after 8 consecutive blocks the runtime ends the turn anyway. Geiger could therefore block completion inside Copilot's loop, the way aislop does with the Claude Stop hook.
  - [About hooks (GitHub Copilot cloud agent)](https://docs.github.com/en/copilot/concepts/agents/cloud-agent/about-hooks) (2026-11-07): "Hooks are a way of executing custom shell commands at strategic points in an agent's workflow"
  - Fact-check: The cited about-hooks URL redirects (301) to /en/copilot/concepts/customizations/hooks, which contains the quote and lists agentStop in the cloud-agent subset. https://docs.github.com/en/copilot/reference/hooks-reference : 'agentStop The main agent finishes a turn. Yes — can block and force continuation. Fires. decision: "block" forces another turn, which still counts against the job's timeout.' Also: 'After 8 consecutive block continuations, the CLI overrides the hook and ends the turn anyway'. The claim's open question is answered: blocking is supported.
- **ghcq-1** (corrected) [tool-capability]: GitHub Code Quality (GA 2026-07-20; $10 per active committer per month, plus usage-based billing for AI detection and Autofix, plus Actions compute for CodeQL scans) shows repository reliability and maintainability scores and gates PRs through rulesets. Each score is one of four categories set by the worst rule-based CodeQL finding (Excellent, Good, Fair, Poor). Gates take a CodeQL severity level, an absolute minimum line-coverage percentage, or a maximum line-coverage drop in percentage points relative to the default branch. AI findings cannot be thresholds. The coverage-drop rule is a non-zero per-PR budget on one metric; the docs describe no budget on an aggregate quality score, and the scores are categorical, not numeric.
  - [GitHub Code Quality is now generally available](https://github.blog/changelog/2026-07-20-github-code-quality-is-now-generally-available/) (2026-07-20): "Quality gates through GitHub rulesets, including coverage thresholds, with an evaluate mode for gradual rollout."
  - [Metrics and scores reference (GitHub Code Quality)](https://docs.github.com/en/code-security/reference/code-quality/metrics-and-ratings) (2026-11-07): "Codebase has high-severity issues, including bugs or major maintainability risks.
≥1 "Error" level finding"
  - [Setting code quality thresholds for pull requests](https://docs.github.com/en/code-security/how-tos/maintain-quality-code/set-pr-thresholds) (2026-11-07): "Code Quality AI findings cannot be set as a threshold."
  - Fact-check: I opened all three cited pages. The quotes are present. Blog: '$10 per active committer, per month.' and 'Quality gates through GitHub rulesets, including coverage thresholds, with an evaluate mode for gradual rollout.' Metrics page: 'Codebase has high-severity issues, including bugs or major maintainability risks. ≥1 "Error" level finding'. Thresholds page: 'Code Quality AI findings cannot be set as a threshold.' The error is in the coverage gate. The linked page https://docs.github.com/en/code-security/how-tos/maintain-quality-code/restrict-code-coverage also offers a delta gate: 'Maximum line coverage drop : enter a value to block pull requests where line coverage drops by more than this many percentage points relative to the default branch.' So gates are not limited to an absolute percentage. On Actions billing, https://docs.github.com/en/code-security/concepts/code-quality/code-quality says 'Actions minutes for deterministic CodeQL scans, if you don't use self-hosted runners.'
- **ghcq-2** [fact]: GitHub Code Quality's per-language CodeQL query lists cover C#, Go, Java, JavaScript, Python and Ruby. Rust appears only under code-scanning (security) built-in queries, not under Code Quality, so Rust repositories would get no rule-based quality findings or scores.
  - [GitHub Code Quality: CodeQL detection reference](https://docs.github.com/en/code-security/code-quality/reference/codeql-detection) (2026-11-07): "C# CodeQL queries for Code Quality"
- **bugbot-1** (corrected) [tool-capability]: Cursor Bugbot is an LLM reviewer. Teams steer it with nested .cursor/BUGBOT.md files, Team Rules in Bugbot Automations, manual repository rules, learned rules and '@cursor remember', and can enable a PR-summary riskScore. Its GitHub check concludes neutral on findings by default. It fails only when fail-on-unresolved-issues is enabled, where that option is available to the organization. On 2026-05-11 Cursor announced a switch from $40 per seat per month to usage-based billing (about $1.00-$1.50 per average run) for Teams and Individual plans. Existing customers switch at their first renewal after 2026-06-08, so some annual seat plans run until 2027.
  - [Cursor Docs: Bugbot](https://cursor.com/docs/bugbot) (2026-11-07): "Requiring the status alone does not block merges on findings because findings default to neutral ."
  - [May 2026 Bugbot changes](https://cursor.com/blog/may-2026-bugbot-changes) (2026-05-11): "The average Bugbot run costs $1.00-$1.50, depending on PR size and complexity."
  - Fact-check: I opened https://cursor.com/docs/bugbot. It contains: 'Requiring the status alone does not block merges on findings because findings default to neutral . If fail-on-unresolved-issues behavior is available for your organization, enable it to make unresolved findings produce a failing status.' Its config example includes 'riskScore: true', and it describes Team Rules in Bugbot Automations, nested .cursor/BUGBOT.md files, learned rules and '@cursor remember [fact]'. I opened https://cursor.com/blog/may-2026-bugbot-changes (May 11, 2026). It says 'The average Bugbot run costs $1.00-$1.50, depending on PR size and complexity.' It also says 'For existing customers, this change will start at your next billing renewal after June 8th, 2026.' That makes 'since 2026-05-11' an overstatement for existing customers.
- **claude-cr-1** (corrected) [tool-capability]: Claude Code Code Review (Team and Enterprise plans) runs several agents over each PR, verifies their findings, and posts inline comments ranked by severity plus a check run. It never approves or blocks, and its check run never concludes as a failure. Teams tune it with REVIEW.md, read from the PR's base branch or the default branch and never from the PR's own changes, and with CLAUDE.md files read as project context. The docs do not say which branch CLAUDE.md is read from. It is billed by tokens through usage credits, with no flat per-review price.
  - [Claude Code Docs: Code Review](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/code-review) (2026-11-07): "A review never approves or blocks the pull request, so the decision to merge stays with your team."
  - [Claude Code Docs: Code Review costs and usage](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/code-review-costs) (2026-11-07): "Code Review is billed on token usage through usage credits, separately from your plan's included usage."
  - Fact-check: I opened https://code.claude.com/docs/en/code-review. It says: 'A review never approves or blocks the pull request, so the decision to merge stays with your team.' It also says 'The check run doesn’t conclude as a failure.' and 'Find : several agents examine the diff and the surrounding code in parallel.' It lists 'Plans : Team and Enterprise'. I opened https://code.claude.com/docs/en/code-review-costs: 'Code Review is billed on token usage through usage credits, separately from your plan’s included usage.' It gives no flat price. The branch detail needs a correction. https://code.claude.com/docs/en/code-review-customize says 'Code Review reads REVIEW.md from the pull request’s base branch or from the default branch, never from the pull request’s own changes.' For CLAUDE.md it says only 'Code Review reads your repository’s CLAUDE.md files as project context' and names no branch.
- **codex-1** [tool-capability]: OpenAI Codex GitHub review flags only P0 and P1 issues and follows `## Code Review Rules` sections in AGENTS.md. OpenAI tells teams to keep formatting, lint and other deterministic checks in CI, and says review rules do not replace tests or branch protections. It is included in ChatGPT plans (Plus $20/month, Business $20-25 per user per month), and GitHub reviews count as separate Code Review usage.
  - [Review GitHub pull requests with Codex (redirected from developers.openai.com/codex/integrations/github)](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/third-party/github) (2026-11-07): "Leave mechanical checks in CI. Keep formatting, lint, and other deterministic checks out of review rules."
  - [Codex pricing](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/pricing) (2026-11-07): "Code Review usage applies only when Codex runs reviews through GitHub"
- **pomona-1** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Bloomberg's Pomona (arXiv 2606.06752 v3, 2026-08-25) is an internal tool built from agent skills. Its Repair skill aims for about 10-line quality PRs, but in the evaluated deployment the median was 37 changed lines (range 5-168, 9 PRs over 100). Changes are validated with the team's own test and lint commands. The 10-line target was only an instruction to the agent; the adopting team later added a deterministic check that rejects PRs over 100 lines. Pomona computes no quality score. Code and data are not released, but the paper says the implementation is three Markdown files that can be reproduced.
  - [Williams et al.: Pomona: Continuous Code Quality Improvement via Small, Agentic Pull Requests at Bloomberg](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06752) (2026-08-25): "enforced it with a deterministic check that automatically rejects PRs exceeding that limit."
  - [Pomona paper, threats/limitations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06752) (2026-08-25): "we cannot release the data and source code due to non-disclosure agreement"
  - Fact-check: I opened https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06752: 'Submitted on 4 Jun 2026 ... last revised 25 Aug 2026 (this version, v3)'. I opened the full text at https://arxiv.org/html/2606.06752v3, saved under .local/geiger-research/sources/competitors/pomona_v3.txt. It says: 'the adopting team has since increased Pomona ’s PR size limit to 100 lines and enforced it with a deterministic check that automatically rejects PRs exceeding that limit.' It also says: 'While our original implementation of the Repair skill explicitly specified 10-line diffs, the agent sometimes failed to adhere to this limit' and 'The median was 37, the mean was 57.7, and values ranged from 5 to 168.' On validation: 'any changes should be validated with the project’s testing and linting commands'. On release: 'we cannot release the data and source code due to non-disclosure agreement policies.' The word 'score' does not appear in the text.
- **inf-1** [inference]: Inference from aislop-1 to aislop-6, ghcq-1, ghcq-2 and the reviewer findings: land-21 is wrong if read as 'no tool scores changes deterministically with team rules and regression blocking', because aislop does all of that. It holds in a narrower form: no verified tool combines a configurable non-zero per-PR budget on an aggregate score, team-written deterministic architecture gates that work on Rust, and Rust-aware metrics. Q7 defensibility should rest on that narrower gap. It should also note that aislop or GitHub Code Quality could close it with modest work: a delta budget is a small feature, while Rust import or crate-graph parsing is more work.
  - [aislop README](https://github.com/scanaislop/aislop/blob/main/README.md) (2026-11-07): "| **Architecture** | Structural rules (opt-in) | Custom import bans, layering rules, required patterns |"
  - [aislop src/hooks/feedback.ts](https://github.com/scanaislop/aislop/blob/main/src/hooks/feedback.ts) (2026-11-07): "const regressed = typeof delta === "number" ? delta < 0 : false;"
- **inf-2** (corrected) [inference]: Reviewers from Copilot, Cursor, Claude Code and Codex judge code quality with LLMs and do not block merges by default. Copilot cloud agent's validation also runs deterministic security checks (CodeQL, Advisory Database, secret scanning), but its quality check is the LLM Copilot code review. Bugbot can fail its check when fail-on-unresolved is enabled. Only Codex explicitly says deterministic checks belong in CI. Claude Code's docs only imply it, by advising REVIEW.md to skip 'Anything CI already enforces'. Copilot, Claude Code and Cursor all expose agent hooks, and aislop installs into each (it blocks only via the Claude Stop hook in quality-gate mode). The platforms look more like distribution channels for a deterministic scorer than substitutes. The exception is GitHub, which already ships deterministic categorical scores and ruleset gates, including a coverage-drop budget, in Code Quality.
  - [Review GitHub pull requests with Codex](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/third-party/github) (2026-11-07): "Code review rules guide Codex; they don’t replace tests, branch protections, or required approvals."
  - [scanaislop docs: hooks](https://scanaislop.com/docs/hooks) (2026-11-07): "If score < baseline , the hook returns {"decision":"block","reason":"..."} ."
  - Fact-check: Codex, from https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/third-party/github: 'Code review rules guide Codex; they don’t replace tests, branch protections, or required approvals.' aislop, from https://scanaislop.com/docs/hooks: 'If score < baseline , the hook returns {"decision":"block","reason":"..."} .' and 'only the Claude Stop hook blocks'. The same page lists 'Cursor afterFileEdit' and 'Gemini CLI AfterTool' adapters. Copilot, from https://github.blog/changelog/2025-10-28-copilot-coding-agent-now-automatically-validates-code-security-and-quality/: 'analyzes it for potential security vulnerabilities using CodeQL , checks any newly-introduced dependencies against the GitHub Advisory Database , and uses secret scanning'. Copilot hooks, from https://docs.github.com/en/copilot/concepts/agents/cloud-agent/about-hooks: 'preToolUse ... can approve or deny tool executions'. Claude Code: https://code.claude.com/docs/en/code-review-customize gives an example REVIEW.md with 'Do not report - Anything CI already enforces: lint, formatting, type errors'. I found no explicit statement that gating belongs elsewhere. I did not see the copilot-1 and copilot-2 findings this inference cites.

### Searches without relevant results

- 'GitHub Code Quality supported languages Rust pricing generally available 2026' (standard, 2026-11-07): no official page confirms Rust support for Code Quality. The Code Quality query lists cover C#, Go, Java, JavaScript, Python and Ruby only.
- 'aislop scanaislop review OR criticism OR false positives' (standard, 2026-11-07): only anecdotal false-positive complaints (a Hacker News mirror and a blog deep dive). No independent benchmark of aislop's score validity found.
- DRS-OSS arXiv abstract page (2026-11-07) lists no code URL. The repository (github.com/Ali-Sayed-Salehi/drs-llm) comes from the paper's references; it had 3 stars, last pushed 2026-01-15.
- Codex GitHub review docs (learn.chatgpt.com/docs/third-party/github, 2026-11-07) state no per-review price. The pricing page gives plan prices only.
- Copilot cloud agent configuring-agent-settings page (2026-11-07) does not say whether custom linters or tests can be added as validation tools.
- Re-check (holds): 'GitHub Code Quality supported languages Rust pricing generally available 2026': no official page confirms Rust support for Code Quality. The Code Quality query lists cover C#, Go, Java, JavaScript, Python and Ruby only. — I re-ran the search in standard and extended mode and fetched the official https://docs.github.com/en/code-security/concepts/about-code-quality : 'Code Quality performs rule-based analysis of the following languages using CodeQL: C# Go Java JavaScript Python Ruby TypeScript'. Rust is absent. Nuances: TypeScript is also listed, and the page adds 'Code Quality also performs AI-powered analysis on your repository's recently changed code, including languages beyond those supported by rule-based queries', so Rust may get LLM findings. GA was 2026-07-20 on Team and Enterprise Cloud. CodeQL code scanning, a separate product, does support Rust.
- Re-check (holds): 'aislop scanaislop review OR criticism OR false positives': only anecdotal false-positive complaints (a Hacker News mirror and a blog deep dive). No independent benchmark of aislop's score validity found. — Standard search returned the aiindigo.com deep-dive (narrative-comment false positives) and an everydev.ai listing that mentions a false-positive fix release. I saw no HN mirror this time. An extended search for an independent accuracy evaluation found only the vendor's own scanaislop.com/benchmark. No independent validity benchmark was found.
- Re-check (holds): DRS-OSS arXiv abstract page lists no code URL. The repository (github.com/Ali-Sayed-Salehi/drs-llm) comes from the paper's references; it had 3 stars, last pushed 2026-01-15. — The abstract HTML links only http://worldofcode.org/drs (demo) and a YouTube video. The v3 PDF reference [2], 'Drs oss source code', gives github.com/Ali-Sayed-Salehi/drs-llm. GitHub API: stargazers_count 3, pushed_at 2026-01-15T23:21:30Z.
- Re-check (holds): Codex GitHub review docs (learn.chatgpt.com/docs/third-party/github) state no per-review price. The pricing page gives plan prices only. — The fetched GitHub docs page covers setup, @codex review, AGENTS.md '## Code Review Rules' and Security Review, with no price. learn.chatgpt.com/codex/pricing lists plans (Free $0, Go $8, Plus $20 with 'automatic code review', Pro from $100, Business $20/user annual) and says API-key use has 'No cloud-based features (GitHub code review, Slack, etc.)'. Nuance: the page also points to token rates for credit-based plans, so it is not strictly plan prices only. No per-review price exists. An extended search agrees that reviews are metered by tokens and credits.
- Re-check (holds): Copilot cloud agent configuring-agent-settings page does not say whether custom linters or tests can be added as validation tools. — The page's 'Enabling or disabling built-in code quality and security validation tools' section only describes toggling built-in tools in the 'Validation tools' section. Caveat: the 2026-03-18 GitHub changelog states the agent 'automatically runs your project’s tests and linter', so project tests and linters do run, outside the toggleable validation-tool list.

### Open questions

- Does the hosted scanaislop PR gate support a delta or budget threshold, or only an absolute score? The public docs show only failBelow.
- Which hook events does Copilot cloud agent support, and can any hook (e.g. agentStop) block task completion the way the Claude Code Stop hook can?
- Does GitHub plan Rust queries for Code Quality? Rust code-scanning queries exist, so it may be a short step.
- How widely is Bugbot's fail-on-unresolved-issues option available, and do teams enable it?
- No public adoption numbers were found for Copilot cloud agent validation, Bugbot, Claude Code Code Review or Codex review. Vendor-reported usage would help size the platform channel.
- Does aislop's `--changes` score compute over changed files only (my reading of the docs), or over changed hunks? This affects how local its per-PR score really is.

## What do 2026 studies of real autonomous-agent pull requests (AIDev dataset, MSR 2026 Mining Challenge) show about (a) how agent PRs change static-quality metrics once normalized for size or churn, (b) which quality signals agents degrade or game in practice (lint suppressions, threshold or config edits, function splitting, duplication, over-mocked or deleted tests), and (c) how quality decays across a sequence of agent tasks on one codebase?

- **a-1** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Staggered DiD on AIDev v3 (401 agent-first repos vs 606 matched controls; 117 IDE-first repos vs 73 controls; SonarQube; outcomes at the repository-month level with LOC as a time-varying covariate). Cognitive complexity rises significantly in both groups: +34.9% agent-first, +42.9% IDE-first. The abstract's '~39%' is roughly the average of the two. Static-analysis warnings rise +17.7% (p<0.001) in agent-first repos. The IDE-first estimate (+19.0%) is not statistically significant. The authors say warning growth is 'somewhat noisier'. They flag isolated significant pre-treatment coefficients as a limitation. The outcomes are not per-PR per-line rates.
  - [Agarwal, He, Vasilescu: AI IDEs or Autonomous Agents? Measuring the Impact of Coding Agents on Software Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.13597) (2026-01-20 (v2 2026-01-27)): "quality risks are persistent across settings, with static-analysis warnings and cognitive complexity rising by roughly 18% and 39%, indicating sustained agent-induced technical debt even when velocity advantages fade."
  - [Agarwal et al., full text section 4](https://arxiv.org/html/2601.13597) (2026-01): "We also observe isolated significant pre-treatment coefficients in static-analysis warnings and code complexity, suggesting that untreated potential outcomes are not fully captured by additive fixed effects"
  - Fact-check: Opened https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.13597 (v1 2026-01-20, v2 2026-01-27); the abstract quote matches exactly. Full text at https://arxiv.org/html/2601.13597. Table 2: 'Static Analysis Warnings 0.163∗∗∗ /0.174 (0.048)/(0.114) 17.73 / 19.00 Code Complexity 0.299∗∗∗ /0.357∗∗ (0.059)/(0.114) 34.85 / 42.87'. Methods: 'All outcomes are aggregated at the repository–month level. We control for time-varying covariates: lines of code'. Results: 'Warning growth is somewhat noisier'. The pre-treatment quote matches. An arXiv API search ('abs:difference-in-differences AND abs:agents AND abs:SonarQube') found no replication.
- **a-2** [empirical-result]: A SonarQube base-vs-merge diff of 1,210 merged agent bug-fix PRs (206 Python repos, AIDev) finds that differences between agents in raw new-issue counts largely disappear per KLOC of churn (Kruskal-Wallis not significant; Cursor partial exception). The study has no human baseline. The most violated code-smell rules are S1192 duplicated string literals, S3776 cognitive complexity and S1172 unused parameters.
  - [Cynthia, Muttakin, Roy: Beyond Bug Fixes (MSR 2026 Mining Challenge)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.20109) (2026-01-27): "apparent differences in raw issue counts across agents largely disappear after normalizing by code churn, indicating that higher issue counts are primarily driven by larger PRs."
  - [Beyond Bug Fixes, full text (threats)](https://arxiv.org/html/2601.20109) (2026-01-27): "Our study lacks a matched human baseline and focuses on static post-merge quality signals, excluding downstream runtime and maintenance effects."
- **a-3** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Kraishan (single-author arXiv preprint v1, 2026-09-12) studies 37,623 PRs: 33,596 from five agents across 2,807 repositories, and 4,027 human PRs restricted to the 810 repositories that also contain agent PRs (Dec 2024 to Jul 2025). After normalizing by PR size, no agent has higher 90-day churn per changed line than humans. Churn is measured only on each PR's most-changed file. Revert rates (detected from commit messages) differ by vendor: Codex 6.1%, Devin 14.5%, human 11.5%. The structural measures are regex counts on added diff lines, and static coverage reaches only 23.7% of PRs. Agents and tasks self-select. Claude Code PRs are about 8x larger, which confounds its results.
  - [Kraishan: Not All Agents Are Equal: Code Quality and Post-Merge Maintenance Across Five Autonomous Coding Agents in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/html/2609.17598) (2026-09-12): "none of the five agents imposes a measurably higher per-line maintenance burden than human contributors in the same repositories, and two of them differ from humans in opposite directions"
  - Fact-check: Opened https://arxiv.org/html/2609.17598. Abstract: 'drawn from 2,807 GitHub repositories'. Corpus section: 'we kept human PRs only from repositories that also contain agent PRs (810 repositories)'. Threats: 'Churn on the most-changed file is a proxy for whole-PR churn'. 'Static coverage (23.7% of PRs)'. 'Agents are not randomly assigned to tasks.' The cited quote ('none of the five agents imposes a measurably higher per-line maintenance burden...') matches exactly. The claim wrongly implies that all PRs come from the same 810 repositories.
- **a-4** [empirical-result]: In 403 readability-motivated agent commits (Python, AIDev), radon's Maintainability Index fell in 56.1% and cyclomatic complexity rose in 42.7%, while LOC rose in 71.5% (large effect sizes for LOC and cyclomatic complexity). The paper does not normalize for size, and MI is partly size-driven, so part of the decline may be volume.
  - [Horikawa et al.: Do AI Agents Really Improve Code Readability? (MSR 2026 Mining Challenge)](https://arxiv.org/html/2603.13723) (2026-03-14): "Maintainability Index deteriorated in 56.1% of the commits, indicating a tendency toward reduced maintainability. Similarly, Lines of Code increased in 71.5% of the cases"
- **a-5** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Only the semantic-redundancy analysis is limited to 617 PRs from crewAI. Agent PRs there show Average Max Redundancy of 0.2867 vs 0.1532 for humans (about 1.87x, Mann-Whitney significant). The radon LOC and cyclomatic-complexity comparison (small differences) and the reviewer-sentiment analysis (more neutral or positive toward agent PRs) use a broader dataset of 3,858 Python PRs. The redundancy result still rests on a single repository.
  - [Huang et al.: More Code, Less Reuse (MSR 2026 Mining Challenge)](https://arxiv.org/html/2601.21276) (2026-01-29): "While traditional metrics show minimal differences between agentic-PRs and human-PRs, redundancy metric analysis shows code in agentic-PRs contain significantly more redundancy"
  - Fact-check: Opened https://arxiv.org/html/2601.21276. 'Dataset A (Full Python Dataset): This dataset comprises 3,858 PRs across all filtered Python repositories. We use this broad dataset to analyze traditional code metrics (RQ1) and reviewer sentiment (RQ2).' 'Dataset B (Core Case Study): This dataset consists of 617 PRs from the crewAI repository'. 'the Average Max Redundancy (AMR) for AI agents is 0.2867, compared to only 0.1532 for humans'. Threats: 'we only analyzed the crewAI repository for RQ1 due to computational costs'. The cited quote matches.
- **a-6** (corrected) [empirical-result]: NiCad found 28,425 clone instances (about 94% precision) in 497 of 7,851 merged agent PRs that add code (Python, C#, Java, Ruby only; AIDev). In 320 PRs clones recur across commits. The sample was filtered to merged PRs, so 'merged' is partly by construction. The clone merge rate depends on the lifecycle: 11 to 42.5% when clones appear in a single commit, and 63.8 to 81.4% when they recur. The authors say agent clone lifecycles resemble those reported for humans. The genealogy study (350 lineages in 6 projects, 516 human vs 473 agent merged PRs) attributes 85.71% of lineages to human-merged PRs and 14.29% to agent-merged PRs, with survival of 80% vs 76%. In that small sample, agents originated fewer clone lineages per PR than humans.
  - [Uchoa et al.: A Study on Code Clone Lifecycles in Pull Requests Created by AI Agents (MSR '26)](https://mhepaixao.github.io/homepage/files/msr26lifecycle.pdf) (2026 (MSR 2026)): "the findings indicate that clones tend to persist once introduced, progressing through the PR lifecycle and ultimately being merged into the codebase."
  - [Sousa et al.: An Empirical Study of Code Clone Genealogies in Human-AI Collaborative Development (MSR '26)](https://mhepaixao.github.io/homepage/files/msr26genealogy.pdf) (2026 (MSR 2026)): "humans introduce 85.71% of code clones, whereas agents contribute only 14.29%. Despite similar clone survival rates for both humans (80%) and agents (76%)"
  - Fact-check: Opened https://mhepaixao.github.io/homepage/files/msr26lifecycle.pdf. 'Filter only merged PRs that add code'. 'when PRs are classified as Single, the merge rates of clones are 42.5% for Python, 39.5% for C#, and only 11.0% for Ruby'. Recurring: '77.8% of Python PRs, 81.4% of C# PRs, and 63.8% of Ruby PRs'. 'AI-generated clones show lifecycle and type distributions comparable to those reported for human developers'. Opened https://mhepaixao.github.io/homepage/files/msr26genealogy.pdf; the quote matches. Method: '989 merged PRs in total, of which 516 (54%) were merged by humans, and 473 (46%) were merged by agents'.
- **b-1** (corrected) [inference]: I re-ran the counts on AIDev v3 pr_commit_details. The arithmetic in the JSON reproduces: 8.7% add, 6.6% net-positive, 6.1% net-positive among merged PRs, 1.68 markers per 1k added lines. But the method inflates these numbers. pr_commit_details holds one row per commit and file (12,026 of 33,580 PRs have several commits). About 41% of added markers (6,545 of about 16,055) come from commits whose message starts with 'Merge ', which most likely bring in base-branch code written by others. Excluding those commits gives: 6.7% of 24,196 code PRs add at least one marker; 5.1% are net-positive; 9,510 markers added vs 5,777 removed. By agent: Claude Code 17.4% (net 14.7%, n=333), Devin 8.4%, Copilot 8.0%, Cursor 7.5%, Codex 5.8%. Rust #[allow/#[expect: 419 added vs 308 removed, in 109 PRs. Further limits: v3 truncates commits at 30 per PR, and large patches are missing. The lint-config regex skips pyproject.toml, setup.cfg, Cargo.toml, package.json and tox.ini, so the 2.6% config and 0.08% threshold rates are lower bounds; the threshold regex also matches line-length. Totals are concentrated in a few PRs (for example, Claude Code's 1,470 eslint-disable additions come from 17 PRs). There is no human baseline, so the counts cannot show gaming. The practical conclusion still holds: roughly 1 in 15 to 20 agent PRs, and 1 in 6 to 7 Claude Code PRs, adds suppressions.
  - [AIDev dataset card (v5; v3 tag used for the count), CC-BY-4.0](https://huggingface.co/datasets/hao-li/AIDev) (v3 tag; card read 2026-11-07): "Note that the `patch` data does not include large patches since the GitHub API does not provide them."
  - Fact-check: Read .local/geiger-research/sources/gap-agent-pr-quality-and-gaming/aidev_v3_suppression_counts.py and .json and recomputed the ratios. Ran scratchpad/insp2.py and insp3.py: 'Counter({'normal': 9510, 'merge': 6545})'; 'ALL 24196 add% 6.7 net% 5.1 A 9510 D 5777 rustA 419 rustD 308 rustPRs 109'. Dataset card https://huggingface.co/datasets/hao-li/AIDev (raw README): the large-patch quote matches, and 'file-level `pr_commit_details` may be missing for commits beyond the first 30 of very large PRs.' v3 is listed as having the 30-item truncation issue.
- **b-2** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Across about 1.25M commits from 2025 in 2,168 TS/JS/Python repositories that contain agent config files (not AIDev), 23% of coding-agent commits add or change test files, vs 13% of non-agent commits. Among test commits, 36% of agent test commits add mocks, vs 26% of non-agent test commits. The 36% vs 26% comparison is not over all commits. The study measures how often mocks are added, not whether tests are over-mocked.
  - [Hora, Robbes: Are Coding Agents Generating Over-Mocked Tests? An Empirical Study (MSR 2026 technical track)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00409) (2026-01-30): "36% of commits made by coding agents add mocks to tests, compared with 26% by non-agents"
  - Fact-check: Opened https://arxiv.org/html/2602.00409 (v1 30 Jan 2026, MSR '26). The abstract wording matches the cited quote, but Observation 3 in the body says: '36% of test commits made by coding agents add mocks to test code, compared with 26% of test commits made by non-agents.' Arithmetic check: there are 11,035 agent test commits among 48,563 agent commits, so 36% of all agent commits (about 17.5k) cannot be mock-adding test commits. Test share: '23% of commits made by coding agents involve the addition or modification of test files, compared with 13%'.
- **b-3** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Of 86,156 cumulative test-file patches (per PR and file) from AIDev-pop agent PRs, 80.2% have weak or no explicit oracle signal in their added lines. This includes edits to existing test files. On newly created test files, strong-oracle rates range from 18% (Codex) to 67% (Claude Code). Raw merge rates are lower for strong-oracle PRs. After adjusting for agent, size, popularity, task type and language, the multi-signal strong-oracle group (S3) has higher merge odds (OR 1.28). This odds ratio does not apply to all strong-oracle PRs. The paper has no human baseline.
  - [Banik, Chowdhury, Shamim: All Smoke, No Alarm: Oracle Signals in Agent-Authored Test Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18168) (2026-06-16): "80.2% of test patches contain weak or no explicit oracle signals."
  - Fact-check: Opened https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18168 and https://arxiv.org/html/2606.18168. The quote matches. 'While raw merge rates are lower for strong-oracle PRs, a regression analysis...'. 'S3 oracles associate with significantly higher merge rates after adjustment (OR = 1.28'. 'has_multi_strong (best patch is S3)'. 'On newly added test files, strong-oracle rates range from 18% to 67% across agents.' 'we concatenate all patches for each unique (PR, filename) pair in commit order'.
- **b-4** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Of 4,882 agent PRs (532 Java, 4,350 Python), coverage could be measured only for 213 Java and 1,664 Python merged PRs in 10 Java and 34 Python buildable repositories. In those PRs, existing tests cover 61.5% (Java) and 27.0% (Python) of changed executable lines. Agent tests add coverage in 35.9% (23/64) of Java and 22.5% (136/605) of Python Code+Tests PRs. Among non-improving Java PRs that did not already reach 100% diff coverage (a few dozen PRs), agents deleted 82 tests and added 31.
  - [Dipongkor et al.: Test Coverage Analysis of Agentic Pull Requests](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.18057) (2026-07-20): "Java agents sometimes delete more tests than they add (a 2.6 deletion-to-addition ratio in non-improving Java PRs), while Python agents add tests that exercise some other code"
  - Fact-check: Opened https://arxiv.org/html/2607.18057 (v1, July 2026). The quote matches. 'yielding coverage results for 213 of the 532 Java PRs and 1664 of the 4350 Python PRs'. 'Only 35.9% of Java (23/64) and 22.5% of Python (136/605) Code + Tests PRs actually show any improvement.' 'In Java, 42.2% already reach 100% diff coverage from the existing tests. Among the rest, agents delete more tests than they add (82 deleted vs. 31 added, a 2.6 ratio)'.
- **b-5** [empirical-result]: Conflicting evidence on test deletion. In 114,134 merged Java PRs (391 repos, Jan 2025-Apr 2026), within-repository mixed-effects models that control for change size find that agent-supported PRs add tests more often and modify or delete tests less often than human-authored PRs. Coverage of production changes did not differ in the 95 repos the authors could build, and test-smell differences were borderline.
  - [Milanese et al.: Testing with Coding Agents: A Large-Scale Comparison of Agent-Supported and Human-Authored Pull Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35297) (2026-10-26): "Agent-supported pull requests add tests more often (odds ratios 1.50-1.58) and modify (0.70-0.76) and delete (0.51-0.77) them less often."
- **b-6** [empirical-result]: A practitioner audit (self-published, exploratory) labeled 358 public 2026 PRs that touched tests, using an LLM plus manual checks. Unexplained test weakening was near zero for both agents and humans. 19-39% of PRs changed checks deliberately. In a small experiment, Codex changed production code to satisfy a wrong test (3 of 3 runs without a guard) instead of weakening the test.
  - [358 pull requests that changed tests: agents rarely weakened them. They bent the code instead. (DEV Community)](https://dev.to/cherven/358-pull-requests-that-changed-tests-agents-rarely-weakened-them-they-bent-the-code-instead-3ld) (2026-10-04 (year inferred from content)): "No meaningful agent-vs-human difference"
- **b-7** [empirical-result]: In agentic Python refactoring PRs (AIDev), 24.17% of modified files gain new Pylint issues (mostly convention-level) and 4.7% gain new Bandit findings, yet 73.5% of PRs merge, including some that add findings. In 3,907 Java agentic refactoring commits, changes in design and implementation smells are statistically significant but practically negligible.
  - [Almukhtar, Ghammam, Ming: Quality and Security Signals in AI-Generated Python Refactoring Pull Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21453) (2026-05-20): "73.5% of the analyzed PRs are merged, including cases that introduce new lint or security findings, often alongside the removal of existing issues."
  - [Horikawa et al.: Agentic Refactoring: An Empirical Study of AI Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/html/2511.04824) (2025-11-06 (v2 2026-10-26)): "Changes in design and implementation smell counts are statistically significant but practically negligible."
- **b-8** [empirical-result]: Among 400 repeat reviewers of agent PRs (11,429 reviews, seven months, AIDev), approval rose from 30.1% to 36.8% while inline comments fell 22%. Human-PR approval fell over the same period, and median PR size was flat.
  - [Yu et al.: Habituation at the Gate: Rising Approval and Declining Scrutiny in Human Review of AI Agent Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22721) (2026-06-21): "inline comment volume decreases (-22%, p=0.0014), suggesting reviewers spend more time in queue but less time actively inspecting code."
- **c-1** [empirical-result]: In SlopCodeBench, 15 agents extend their own code across 36 problems and 196 checkpoints. Structural erosion rises in 77% of trajectories and verbosity in 75.5%. Agent code is 2.3x more verbose and 2.0x more eroded than code in 473 human Python repos. Explicit quality prompts lower starting levels by up to a third but do not change the rate of decay.
  - [Orlanski et al.: SlopCodeBench: Benchmarking How Coding Agents Degrade Over Long-Horizon Iterative Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.24755) (2026-03-25 (v2 2026-05-07)): "Explicit quality guidance reduces initial verbosity and erosion by up to a third, without affecting degradation rates."
- **c-2** (corrected) [empirical-result]: In CodeClash (1,680 tournaments, 25,200 rounds, 8 LMs, 6 arenas), the number of agent-created files grows almost linearly with rounds, and filename redundancy (fraction of files sharing name prefixes) is high for Qwen3 Coder (59%) and Claude Sonnet models (35%); most agent-created files are never reused. The paper contrasts this with human codebases only qualitatively (file creation 'quickly plateaus'); it does not measure human codebases.
  - [Yang et al.: CodeClash: Benchmarking Goal-Oriented Software Engineering](https://arxiv.org/html/2511.00839) (2025-11-02 (v2 2026-05-12)): "Models also struggle with long-term codebase maintenance, as repositories become progressively messy and redundant."
  - Fact-check: Opened https://arxiv.org/html/2511.00839 (v2 12 May 2026). Quote matches exactly. "We run 1680 tournaments ( 25 , 200 rounds total) to evaluate 8 LMs across 6 arenas." "the filename redundancy metric (the fraction of files sharing name prefixes with other files) which is particularly high for Qwen3 Coder (59%) and Claude Sonnet models (35%)". Human side: "In most human-managed codebases, the rate of file creation quickly plateaus once the overall structure has been established" — an unmeasured general statement. Redundancy is reported as a level, not shown to keep growing. Arenas are competitive game bots, not product repos.
- **c-3** (corrected) [empirical-result]: CodeThread (four models via the SWE-Agent scaffold: Claude 4.5 Sonnet, GPT-5, GLM 4.7, MiniMax M2.5; four benchmarks) finds downstream resolve rates are usually lower on agent-written code, with a maximum gap of 13.1 percentage points (GLM on SWE-Bench Pro, 45.2% vs 32.0%); most gaps are 3-8 points and some pairs favour agent code. Of four static metrics tested (cyclomatic complexity, cognitive complexity, Halstead volume, LLOC), only downstream LLOC change was significant; an input/error-contract indicator and task difficulty were also significant, but model fit is modest (McFadden R2 = 0.069).
  - [Patel et al.: Is Agent Code Less Maintainable Than Human Code?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21804) (2026-06-19 (v2 2026-09-29)): "Regression analysis reveals that many traditional software engineering maintainability metrics do not explain this difference."
  - Fact-check: Opened https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21804 (v2 29 Sep 2026) and the HTML version. Abstract quote matches exactly; "task resolve rate drops of up to 13.1%". Table 2: "SWE-Bench Pro ... 45.2 32.0 − 13.1" for GLM, and positive deltas such as "+ 6.2" and "+ 3.1" for some pairs. Text: "GLM shows the largest and most consistent effect ... dropping by 13.1% on SWE-Bench Pro. Claude and MiniMax also drop by around 3 - 8 %". "Most static maintainability metrics have similar distributions, except Δ LLOC at PR 2". 'Up to 13.1% fewer tasks' overstates scope: it is a single worst case in percentage points.
- **c-4** (corrected) [empirical-result]: NITR evaluates 23 configurations (GPT, Claude, Gemini, Qwen families, in both direct-inference and agent mode). Averaged over all configurations, 36.2% of probes are solved (best 57.1%); agent mode raises the average from 28.2% to 45.0% but does not remove architectural failures. Dependency control is solved in 4.3% and responsibility decomposition in 15.2%; 64/483 outcomes (13.3%) pass all functional tests but fail the structural oracle. The abstract does not break the 13.3% down by dimension.
  - [Zhu et al.: Needle in the Repo: A Benchmark for Maintainability in AI-Generated Repository Edits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.27745) (2026-03-29): "The hardest pressures are architectural rather than local edits, especially dependency control (4.3%) and responsibility decomposition (15.2%)."
  - Fact-check: Opened https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.27745 (29 Mar 2026). Quote matches exactly. "we evaluate 23 coding configurations across GPT, Claude, Gemini, and Qwen families in both direct-inference and agent-based settings"; "agent-mode configurations improve average performance from 28.2% to 45.0%, but do not eliminate these architectural failures". So '36.2%' is not an agent-only figure. The relevance note's 'tests catch least' for architecture rules is not shown by the abstract. Probes are small synthetic codebases.
- **c-5** [empirical-result]: Across 302.6k verified AI-authored commits (Copilot, Claude, Cursor, Gemini, Devin; 6,299 repos; attributed by bot logins and Co-authored-by trailers), 7.5-14.8% of commits introduce at least one static-analysis issue, 89.3% of the issues are code smells, and 22.7% survive to the latest revision.
  - [Liu et al.: Debt Behind the AI Boom: A Large-Scale Empirical Study of AI-Generated Code in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28592) (2026-03-30 (v3 2026-10-13)): "More importantly, 22.7% of tracked AI-introduced issues still survive at the latest version of the repository."
- **inf-1** (corrected) [inference]: From the findings verified here: (1) Normalize per-PR deltas by churn and compare against a same-repository human baseline. Beyond Bug Fixes shows cross-agent differences in raw issue counts mostly vanish after churn normalization, and b-5 shows pooled cross-project comparisons can hide or reverse within-repo effects. (2) Unexplained test deletion or weakening is rare in real PRs (b-5, b-6). Deliberate check changes are common (19-39% of test-touching PRs in b-6), so surface them for review instead of treating them as rare. b-6 also shows a gaming mode the list omits: agents bending production code to satisfy a wrong test, or adding silent fallbacks. Architectural dependency control is the weakest maintainability dimension in NITR (c-4). (3) In CodeThread, cyclomatic complexity, cognitive complexity and Halstead volume did not explain downstream agent difficulty; LLOC change did, and the model fit was weak. The study did not test the Maintainability Index, so the score's weights need validation against downstream outcomes. Parts that rest on a-1 to a-6 and b-1 to b-4 were not checked here.
  - [Beyond Bug Fixes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.20109) (2026-01-27): "higher issue counts are primarily driven by larger PRs."
  - [Is Agent Code Less Maintainable Than Human Code?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21804) (2026-09-29): "many traditional software engineering maintainability metrics do not explain this difference."
  - Fact-check: Opened https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.20109 (27 Jan 2026): "apparent differences in raw issue counts across agents largely disappear after normalizing by code churn, indicating that higher issue counts are primarily driven by larger PRs". This compares agents with each other, not agents with humans. Opened https://arxiv.org/html/2606.21804: the static features are "four static proxies for maintenance cost" (CC, CogC, Halstead Volume, LLOC), and "Most static maintainability metrics have similar distributions, except Δ LLOC at PR 2". No MI was tested. DEV post (b-6): "19-39% of PRs changed or removed a check on purpose"; "It changed the code to satisfy the wrong test in 3 of 3 runs without the guard". Not verifiable here: a-1 to a-6 and b-1 to b-4, the claim that 'function splitting is unmeasured', and whether threshold or config edits are rare. A web search for counter-evidence could not run because the session's search budget was exhausted.

- Re-check (holds): arXiv API search 'abs:AIDev AND abs:suppress' (2026-11-07): 0 results; no AIDev study counts lint suppressions. — Re-ran the arXiv API on 2026-11-07. 'abs:AIDev AND abs:suppress', 'abs:AIDev AND abs:suppression' and 'abs:agent AND abs:"pull requests" AND abs:suppression' all returned totalResults 0. A WebSearch (extended) for 'AIDev coding agent pull requests lint suppression noqa eslint-disable study' found no suppression study. Its nearest results were 2605.21453 (new Pylint issues in refactoring PRs, not suppressions), 2606.13468 and 2609.26847.
- Re-check (holds): WebSearch extended 'AI coding agents pull requests lint suppression noqa OR eslint-disable OR type: ignore empirical study' (2026-11-07): found tools and blogs (agent-lint, a dotfiles hook PR, a blog anecdote), no peer-reviewed study. — Partly re-checked. An extended search with a close variant query found no peer-reviewed suppression study. The standard-mode search with the exact query was refused because the session's WebSearch budget ran out. The clone-lifecycle papers were found via the authors' homepage PDFs instead. — Re-ran: 14 results, all about behaviour, tool, voice or itinerary cloning (e.g. 2403.00336, 2603.22447, 2609.07131). The full text says 'a final pool of 110 maintainability-oriented metrics' from SonarQube, Understand and CodeScene. Note: it generates agent implementations for SQuaD issues itself rather than mining real agent PRs.

'gh auth status' prints 'You are not logged into any GitHub hosts.' I did not inspect v5 myself. The GitHub MCP server was available in this session and might fetch human PR diffs, which is untested.

### Open questions

- Do agent PRs add suppressions, #[allow] attributes, or lint and coverage threshold edits more often than human PRs in the same repositories? This needs human diffs, for example 2609.17598's cached GitHub responses or an authenticated GitHub sample.
- Does the 18%/39% repo-level rise in warnings and complexity (2601.13597) survive per-LOC or per-churn normalization, given the pre-trend coefficients the authors report?
- Is function splitting or other metric-shaped refactoring by agents measurable in practice, for example as an Extract Method rise paired with flat total complexity?
- There is no AIDev quality study for Rust or Godot. Do the Python/Java/TS results carry over to Cargo workspaces and clippy?
- Which measurable code properties predict the downstream agent resolve-rate drop in CodeThread? Those would be the right weights for a Geiger score (Q7 premise).
- Could Geiger's per-PR budget (e.g. -0.2 points) cause the code-bending behaviour seen in the 358-PR audit, with agents satisfying the check rather than the intent?

## What outcome evidence supports scoring a change instead of the whole codebase?
