Which change-level features predict defect-inducing changes in just-in-time (JIT) defect prediction, how stable are those models over time, do simple models match deep ones, can risk be localized to lines for a report, do diff-time findings get fixed more often than batch reports, and what did Google and Meta observe when they deployed change-risk scores?

- **jit-1** [empirical-result]: Kamei et al. (TSE 2013; 6 open-source and 5 commercial projects, more than 250,000 changes, C/C++ and Java) predicted defect-inducing changes with 68% accuracy and 64% recall. Ranking by risk per changed line, 20% of inspection effort (measured as modified LOC) finds 35% of defect-inducing changes. In open-source projects, the number of files (NF), relative churn (LA/LT, LT/NF) and fix commits (FIX) increase risk, and the time since a file's last change (AGE) decreases it. In commercial projects, NF, relative churn and the past number of developers on a file (NDEV) increase risk.
  - [Kamei et al., A Large-Scale Empirical Study of Just-in-Time Quality Assurance, TSE 39(6) (local copy kamei.pdf)](https://doi.org/10.1109/TSE.2012.70) (2013-06): "using only 20 percent of the effort it would take to inspect all changes, we can identify 35 percent of all defect-inducing changes"
  - [Kamei et al., TSE 2013, Section on RQ3](https://doi.org/10.1109/TSE.2012.70) (2013-06): "the number of files and whether or not the change fixes a defect are riskincreasing factors, whereas the average time interval since the previous change is a risk-decreasing factor"
- **jit-2** [empirical-result]: JIT models drift. On 37,524 Qt and OpenStack changes, models trained on short periods lost 3-22 (Qt) and 3-34 (OpenStack) AUC points one year after training. The authors recommend retraining on data from the last three months and, for quality-improvement plans, training on at least six months of history.
  - [McIntosh & Kamei, Are Fix-Inducing Changes a Moving Target? TSE 44(5), doi:10.1109/TSE.2017.2693980 (preprint)](https://rebels.cs.uwaterloo.ca/papers/tse2017_mcintosh.pdf) (2018 (TSE; preprint 2017-04)): "JIT models should be retrained using recently recorded data (within three months)."
  - [McIntosh & Kamei, TSE 2018, RQ1 results](https://rebels.cs.uwaterloo.ca/papers/tse2017_mcintosh.pdf) (2018): "our short-period models lose 3–22 and 3–34 AUC percentage points one year after being trained"
- **jit-3** (corrected) [empirical-result]: In McIntosh & Kamei, Size was the most consistent top contributor, but its importance fluctuated (10%-43% of explanatory power in Qt three-month short-period models). In Qt, Size was the top contributor in all long-period periods, in 8 of 11 three-month and 5 of 6 six-month short-period periods, and statistically significant in all periods. Even in Qt, the Review family outranked Size in short-period periods 5, 6 and 9, and History outranked it in period 6. In OpenStack, Review outranked Size in many periods, including all six-month long-period models, and Size was non-significant in 2 of 9 three-month short-period models.
  - [McIntosh & Kamei, TSE 2018, RQ2 results](https://rebels.cs.uwaterloo.ca/papers/tse2017_mcintosh.pdf) (2018): "consistency of the importance of Size suggests that advice about limiting change size is sound."
  - Fact-check: https://rebels.cs.uwaterloo.ca/papers/tse2017_mcintosh.pdf RQ2: 'the Size family is the top contributor in 8 of the 11 three-month periods and 5 of the 6 six-month periods of our short-period JIT models, and all of the three- and six-month periods for our long-period JIT models.' Also: 'in QT, the Review family achieves a larger importance score than the Size family in periods 5, 6, and 9 of the three-month short-term models'. OpenStack: Review is larger in '(d) all periods of the six-month long-term models'. The quote 'consistency of the importance of Size suggests that advice about limiting change size is sound' is present. The abstract says the magnitude 'fluctuates considerably'. The claim's wording 'some OpenStack periods' understates the Review effect and omits the Qt exceptions.
- **jit-4** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Fan et al. (IEEE TSE, published online July 2019; 126,526 changes, 10 Apache projects) found that the top-ranked metric, number of files modified, is robust to SZZ mislabeling. AG-SZZ labels reduced performance by 1%-5% on average (statistically significant). B-SZZ and MA-SZZ caused no considerable performance drop. B-SZZ and AG-SZZ mislabels wasted 9%-10% and 1%-15% more inspection effort, respectively, and only the B-SZZ effect was significant. The second- and third-ranked metrics were more affected by noise, unless random forest was the classifier.
  - [Fan et al., The Impact of Mislabeled Changes by SZZ on Just-in-Time Defect Prediction, IEEE TSE (author page abstract)](https://yuanrui-fan.github.io/publication/tse2019) (2019-07): "the top-most important metric for identifying bug-introducing changes (i.e., number of files modified in a change) is robust to the mislabeling noise generated by SZZ"
  - Fact-check: https://yuanrui-fan.github.io/publication/tse2019 (abstract, fetched with curl): 'the changes mislabeled B-SZZ and AG-SZZ lead to 9%–10% and 1%–15% more wasted inspection effort, respectively'; 'But the second- and third-most important metrics are more likely to be impacted by the mislabeling noise, unless random forest is used as the underlying classifier.' The quoted 'top-most important metric... is robust' sentence is present. The claim's core holds but omits the random-forest exception and the AG-SZZ effort figure. Only the abstract was readable; the full text was blocked (core.ac.uk 403).
- **jit-5** [empirical-result]: Cabral et al. (ICSE 2019, 10 GitHub projects) show that the share of defect-inducing changes shifts over time. Labels also arrive late (verification latency), because a change is known to be defect-inducing only after its fix lands. Existing JIT approaches scored up to 97.2% lower g-mean than methods designed for this shift.
  - [Cabral, Minku, Shihab, Mujahid, Class Imbalance Evolution and Verification Latency in JIT Software Defect Prediction, ICSE 2019, doi:10.1109/ICSE.2019.00076 (abstract page)](https://research.birmingham.ac.uk/en/publications/class-imbalance-evolution-and-verification-latency-in-just-in-tim) (2019-05-25): "Based on 10 GitHub projects, we show that JIT-SDP suffers from class imbalance evolution, significantly hindering the predictive performance of existing JIT-SDP approaches."
- **jit-6** (corrected) [empirical-result]: On Zeng et al.'s data (ISSTA 2021; 310,370 changes from 6 projects), neither CC2Vec nor DeepJIT consistently beat traditional JIT models. LApredict (logistic regression on added-lines count) outperformed both 'for most cases' and was 81k/120k times faster in training/testing than CC2Vec. The result does not generalize to all open benchmarks: on JIT-Defects4J (Ni et al., FSE 2022), LApredict had the lowest AUC of the baselines (0.694), below DeepJIT (0.775), CC2Vec (0.791), JITLine (0.802) and JIT-Fine (0.881).
  - [Zeng, Zhang, Zhang, Zhang, Deep Just-in-Time Defect Prediction: How Far Are We? ISSTA 2021, doi:10.1145/3460319.3464819](https://zhangyuqun.github.io/publications/issta2021.pdf) (2021-07): "such a simplistic approach can outperform CC2Vec and DeepJIT in defect prediction, and can be 81k X/120k X faster in training/testing."
  - Fact-check: Downloaded https://zhangyuqun.github.io/publications/issta2021.pdf. Abstract quote verified. Intro: 'LApredict can outperform CC2Vec and all other existing approaches for most cases in JIT defect prediction, and can be 81k X/120k X faster than CC2Vec in training/testing.' Contradicting evidence: https://kui-liu.github.io/papers/2022-ni-best.pdf Table 4 shows AUC 'LApredict 0.694', 'DeepJIT 0.775', 'CC2Vec 0.791', 'JIT-Fine 0.881'. Text: 'building a simple supervised model cannot better capture the characteristics of defect-inducing commits.'
- **jit-7** [empirical-result]: JITLine (MSR 2021) uses bag-of-tokens features with a random forest and explains predictions with LIME (local model explanations). On 37,524 OpenStack and Qt commits, it was 26-38% more accurate (F-measure) and 70-100 times faster than CC2Vec and DeepJIT. Its line-level predictions were 133-150% more accurate (Top-10 Accuracy) than a baseline NLP approach. The same study shows that CC2Vec's F-measure falls by 38.5% (OpenStack) and 45.7% (Qt) once the test data is excluded from training.
  - [Pornprasit & Tantithamthavorn, JITLine: A Simpler, Better, Faster, Finer-grained Just-In-Time Defect Prediction, MSR 2021, doi:10.1109/MSR52588.2021.00049](https://arxiv.org/abs/2103.07068) (2021-03-16 (arXiv v2)): "the fine-grained predictions at the line level by our approach are 133%-150% more accurate (Top-10 Accuracy) than the baseline NLP approach"
- **jit-8** [empirical-result]: ReDef evaluates code language models (CodeBERT, CodeT5+, UniXcoder, Qwen2.5) on 3,164 defective and 10,268 clean function-level changes from 22 C/C++ projects. Defective changes are identified by revert commits. Performance barely moved when the diffs were deliberately distorted, for example by swapping added and deleted blocks. The authors conclude the models rely on superficial cues, not on the meaning of the change.
  - [Nam, Kim, Ryu, Baik, ReDef: Do Code Language Models Truly Understand Code Changes for JIT Software Defect Prediction? arXiv 2509.09192v2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09192) (2026-04-02 (v2)): "under counterfactual tests, performance remains effectively stable, revealing that what appears to be robustness in fact reflects a reliance on superficial cues rather than true semantic understanding"
- **meta-1** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Meta's logistic regression DRS baseline captures 18.7%, 27.9% and 84.6% of SEVs when gating the top 5%, 10% and 50% of diffs. StarBERT captures 0.61x, 0.85x and 0.81x as many SEVs. Risk-aligned iCodeLlama-34B captures 1.26x, 1.28x and 0.98x, and risk-aligned iDiffLlama-13B (diff-pretrained) captures 1.40x, 1.52x and 1.05x. Without risk alignment, both LLMs were worse than the logistic regression (0.58x-0.90x). Results come from replay on historical diffs. The logistic regression uses diff size relative to file, diffusion (files, authors), criticality (prior SEVs in file or folder, high-criticality service), file complexity and language, and author expertise.
  - [Abreu et al., Moving Faster and Reducing Risk: Using LLMs in Release Deployment, arXiv 2410.06351](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.06351) (2024-10-08): "Our baseline regression model captures 18.7%, 27.9%, and 84.6% of SEVs while respectively gating the top 5% (weekend), 10% (yellow), and 50% (red) of risky diffs."
  - [Abreu et al., arXiv 2410.06351, abstract](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.06351) (2024-10-08): "The generative LLMs, iCodeLlama-34B and iDiffLlama-13B, when risk-aligned, capture more SEVs than the logistic regression model in production: 1.40×, 1.52×, 1.05×, respectively."
  - Fact-check: Downloaded https://arxiv.org/pdf/2410.06351 (v1, 8 Oct 2024). Results summary: 'When iCodeLlama-34B is risk aligned, captures 1.26×, 1.28×, and 0.98× as many SEVs as the logistic regression... The corresponding number for iDiffLlama-13B are 1.40×, 1.52×, 1.05×.' The abstract's '1.40×, 1.52×, 1.05×' therefore belongs to iDiffLlama-13B only. 'Without aligning for diff risk... iCodeLlama-34B model only captures 0.58×, 0.65×, and 0.82×'. Evaluation: 'We mine historical data to determine how well the model would have done'. Table I lists features including 'Previous SEV in the file', 'Is file involved in high-criticality service', 'Total logical complexity of files touched'.
- **meta-2** [fact]: Meta Engineering (2025-08-06) says DRS, a fine-tuned Llama model, let Meta land more than 10,000 diffs during a 2024 partner-event freeze 'with minimal production impact' and eliminate major code freezes. The post gives no SEV-rate numbers and calls LLM explainability an open problem.
  - [Meta Engineering, Diff Risk Score: AI-driven risk-aware software development](https://engineering.fb.com/2025/08/06/developer-tools/diff-risk-score-drs-ai-risk-aware-software-development-meta/) (2025-08-06): "we landed 10,000+ code changes (that previously could not have landed during a freeze) with minimal production impact"
- **meta-3** (corrected) [empirical-result]: RADAR (Adams et al., arXiv 2605.30208v2, June 2026) reports 105.9% YoY growth in significant LOC per human-landed diff and 51% growth in per-developer diff volume, with agentic AI responsible for over 80% of that growth. DRS gates automated review and landing: P5 by default for human diffs, P20 for non-allowlisted AI/bot sources, P50 for allowlisted runbooks. RADAR reviewed 535K+ diffs and landed 331K+. Revert rate is 1/3 and production-incident rate 1/50 of non-RADAR diffs. The authors call this 'not a causal estimate' and note under internal validity that diffs are not randomly assigned to RADAR, so unobserved confounding can influence the results. Their abstract nonetheless claims the system works 'without compromising production safety'.
  - [Adams et al., Automating Low-Risk Code Review at Meta: RADAR, Risk Calibration, and Review Efficiency, arXiv 2605.30208v2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30208) (2026-06-12 (v2)): "significant lines of code per human-landed diff grew by 105.9% year over year and per-developer diff volume rose 51%, with agentic AI responsible for over 80% of that growth"
  - [Adams et al., arXiv 2605.30208, Section 6.3 Internal Validity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30208) (2026-06-12): "Because diffs are not randomly assigned to RADAR, unobserved confounding can influence observed changes."
  - Fact-check: Opened arXiv 2605.30208 (abstract page shows v2, 12 Jun 2026; PDF text read). Abstract quote matches exactly. 535K+ reviewed / 331K+ landed, revert 1/3 and PI 1/50 of non-RADAR diffs (Table 5) confirmed. Sec 2.3: 'For human-authored diffs, the default DRS threshold is P5, meaning only the lowest-risk 5% of diffs qualify'; AI/bot diffs use P50 (allowlisted RACER runbooks) or P20. Sec 6.3 quote matches. Detail to fix: the 'not a causal estimate' sentence sits in the RQ2 results ('While this is not a causal estimate, it is an important operational indicator that widening the approval envelope has not been associated with safety degradation at scale'). The reason the authors give, in 6.3, is non-random assignment and confounding: rollout, evolving eligibility, shifting diff complexity and test coverage. They do not say 'because diffs are pre-selected as low risk'; that is the checker's paraphrase. The abstract also makes a stronger claim: 'without compromising production safety'.
- **meta-4** [empirical-result]: Meta's highlighting of risky diff regions (Liu et al., AIware 2026) aggregates DRS attention weights to lines, hunks and files. On a set of outages whose root-cause lines experts labeled, the top-2 hunks covered the root-cause lines 53.85% of the time, with developers reviewing 26.28% of changed lines on average. The top-10 lines gave 30.76% coverage at 7.6% of lines. The coverage figures are consistent with a few dozen diffs, but the paper does not state the count. Early in-tool feedback was 7 positive and 48 negative. Downstream outcomes were not measured.
  - [Liu et al., A Preliminary Study on Explaining Risk of Code Changes using LLM-Based Prediction Models, AIware '26, arXiv 2607.02782](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02782) (2026-07-02): "the highlighted snippets cover expert-labeled outage-causing change lines 53.85% of the time when highlighting the top-2 hunks, while requiring developers to review 26.28% of the changed lines on average"
  - [Liu et al., arXiv 2607.02782, Section 4.4 User Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02782) (2026-07-02): "We received 55 pieces of feedback: 7 positive and 48 negative."
- **oss-1** [tool-capability]: DRS-OSS (Sayedsalehi, Rigby, Mockus) is an open-source diff risk scorer for CI built on a Llama 3.1 8B classifier, with a GitHub App. On ApacheJIT it reports ROC-AUC 0.895, and the riskiest 30% of commits contain 86.4% of risky commits. The authors warn that Llama may have seen Apache code during pretraining, which could inflate these results.
  - [Sayedsalehi, Rigby, Mockus, DRS-OSS: A Diff-Risk Scoring Tool for Continuous Integration Workflows, arXiv 2511.21964v3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.21964) (2026-06-07 (v3)): "sending only the riskiest 30% of commits for extra scrutiny captures 86.4% of risky commits in the test set"
- **goog-1** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Google deployed file-level, history-based bug prediction (TWR) in September 2011 as a line-1 comment in code review (Mondrian), flagging the top 0.5% of files company-wide. After four weeks the comment was demoted to a hidden-by-default lint warning, which reduced its visibility. Comparing reviews from three months before and after launch, mean review time (2.01 to 1.94 days) and comments per review (5.97 to 6.38) did not change significantly. The authors attribute the null result mainly to non-actionability: developers had no way to clear the flag. This was file-level prediction, not JIT change-level prediction.
  - [Lewis, Lin, Sadowski, Zhu, Ou, Whitehead, Does Bug Prediction Support Human Developers? Findings From a Google Case Study, ICSE 2013, doi:10.1109/ICSE.2013.6606583](https://static.googleusercontent.com/media/research.google.com/en//pubs/archive/41145.pdf) (2013-05): "We deployed a bug prediction algorithm across Google, and found no identifiable change in developer behavior."
  - [Lewis et al., ICSE 2013, Section V.C.3 Interpretation](https://static.googleusercontent.com/media/research.google.com/en//pubs/archive/41145.pdf) (2013-05): "unless there was an actionable means of removing the flag and “fixing” the file, developers did not find value in the bug prediction, and ignored it"
  - Fact-check: Opened static.googleusercontent.com .../41145.pdf. Both quotes match. Deployed September 2011 (launch 16 Sep), comment on line 1, 'set to flag the top 0.5% of the Google code base'. Table II: time to review 2.01 to 1.94 days, comments 5.97 to 6.38. 'Student's t-tests show that neither change was significant'. TWR is file-level and based on bug-fix history. Omitted confound: 'After four weeks, the comments were given the same priority as a high-level lint warning: developers could see it if warnings were enabled, but otherwise the comment was not visible ... [this] reduced visibility of our comments.' Measurement compared reviews from three months on either side of launch, with 5th/95th-percentile outliers removed. Non-actionability is the authors' interpretation of qualitative feedback, not a measured cause. The paper does not establish the flag's accuracy in deployment, so the relevance phrase 'an accurate risk flag' overstates.
- **fb-1** [empirical-result]: At Facebook, Infer run as a nightly batch job, with 20-30 issues hand-assigned to developers, got a fix rate near zero. The same analysis, with the same false-positive rate, reached over 70% fixes when its findings were posted as comments on the diff during code review. The batch sample was small.
  - [Distefano, Fähndrich, Logozzo, O'Hearn, Scaling Static Analyses at Facebook, CACM 62(8), doi:10.1145/3338112 (UCL author-accepted manuscript)](https://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/10084236/1/O%27Hearn%20AAM%20scaling-static-analysis-at-facebook.pdf) (2019-08): "the fix rate rocketed to over 70%. The same program analysis, with same false positive rate, had much greater impact when deployed at diff time."
  - [Distefano et al., CACM 2019, Human Factors](https://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/10084236/1/O%27Hearn%20AAM%20scaling-static-analysis-at-facebook.pdf) (2019-08): "We assigned 20-30 issues to developers, and almost none of them were acted on."
- **goog-2** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Google's FindBugs history shows that diff-time delivery is not enough on its own. The 2006 nightly dashboard 'saw little use', partly because new and existing issues were hard to tell apart. In the May 2009 Fixit, engineers reviewed 3,954 warnings and only 16% (640) were fixed. A later code-review integration was discontinued in 2011, when the review tool was replaced, for two reasons: effective false positives cost developer trust, and per-developer customization made results inconsistent. As described in 2018, Tricorder temporarily disables an analyzer when its 'Not useful' vs 'Please fix' click ratio exceeds 10%, until the authors improve it.
  - [Sadowski, Aftandilian, Eagle, Miller-Cushon, Jaspan, Lessons from Building Static Analysis Tools at Google, CACM 61(4), doi:10.1145/3188720](https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-research2023-media/pubtools/4365.pdf) (2018-04): "the dashboard saw little use because a bug dashboard was outside the developers’ usual workflow, and distinguishing between new and existing static-analysis issues was distracting."
  - [Sadowski et al., CACM 2018, Attempt 3](https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-research2023-media/pubtools/4365.pdf) (2018-04): "the presence of effective false positives caused developers to lose confidence in the tool"
  - Fact-check: Opened storage.googleapis.com .../4365.pdf (CACM 61(4), 2018; text via pdftotext). Confirmed: 2006 nightly dashboard 'saw little use'. May 2009 Fixit: 'reviewed a total of 3,954 such warnings (42% of 9,473 total), but only 16% (640) were actually fixed'. Two errors. First, the code-review integration 'was discontinued when the code-review tool was replaced in 2011 for two main reasons: the presence of effective false positives caused developers to lose confidence in the tool, and developer customization resulted in an inconsistent view of analysis results.' False positives were one of two reasons, alongside the tool replacement. Second, Tricorder: 'computing the ratio of "Please fix" vs. "Not useful" clicks. If the ratio for an analyzer goes above 10%, the Tricorder team disables the analyzer until the author(s) improve it.' The disable is temporary, the metric is a ratio, and it was described as of 2018. Code-review checks may include 'up to 10% effective false positives'.
- **cov-1** [empirical-result]: Hilton et al. (ASE 2018; 7,816 builds of 47 projects) show that whole-project coverage hides per-change effects. In a 1-million-line project, a 100-line coverage change moves the total by one hundredth of a percentage point. Patch coverage did not correlate with overall coverage (Kendall tau r = -0.01). Patches often changed the coverage of unchanged code.
  - [Hilton, Bell, Marinov, A Large-Scale Study of Test Coverage Evolution, ASE 2018, doi:10.1145/3238147.3238183](https://jonbell.net/publications/coverage) (2018-09): "a change in coverage of even 100 lines would only impact coverage by one hundredth of one percentage point"
  - [Hilton et al., ASE 2018, RQ2 finding](https://jonbell.net/publications/coverage) (2018-09): "Patches often impact coverage of existing (nonpatch) code, and high patch coverage does not correlate with increasing non-patch coverage."
- **agent-1** (corrected) [empirical-result]: In open-source repositories with at least 500 stars (AIDev-pop), within the same repositories and a shared window, merged agent PRs got verified follow-up fixes within 30 days at 1.62 times the odds of merged human PRs (95% CI 1.10-2.39; 2,012 agent vs 4,063 human PRs, drawn from 6,774 agent and 5,044 human PRs overall). Among agent PRs, with repository and number of non-boilerplate files fixed, ten times the commits gave 6.1 times the odds of a fix, and ten times the post-first-push churn (rework) gave 1.3 times. Separately, in 1,210 merged agent bug-fix PRs from Python repositories (MSR '26 mining-challenge paper), differences in SonarQube issue counts between agents largely disappeared after normalizing by churn.
  - [Takerngsaksiri, Duong, Barnett, Who Finishes the Job? A Study of Follow-Up Fixes and Commit Authorship on AI Coding Agent Pull Requests, arXiv 2609.26847v2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26847) (2026-09-24 (v2)): "a merge with ten times the commits carries 6.1 times the odds of a verified fix"
  - [Cynthia, Muttakin, Roy, Beyond Bug Fixes: Post-Merge Code Quality Issues in Agent-Generated Pull Requests, MSR '26, arXiv 2601.20109](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.20109) (2026-01-27): "apparent differences in raw issue counts across agents largely disappear after normalizing by code churn, indicating that higher issue counts are primarily driven by larger PRs"
  - Fact-check: Opened arXiv 2609.26847 (v2, 24 Sep 2026). The study follows 6,774 agent and 5,044 human PRs. The 1.62 odds ratio, however, comes from a within-repository comparison on the shared observation window: 'AI agents 2,012 ... Humans 4,063 ... Odds ratio, within repositories (218 strata) ... 1.62 [1.10-2.39]', with a 30-day fix window. RQ4 models agent merges only, with repository and 'number of non-boilerplate files shipped' held fixed: 'ten times the commits ... 6.1 times the odds ... churn carry 1.3 times (p < 0.001)'. The churn signal is 'post-first-push churn', i.e. rework after the first push, not total PR churn. Review items gave 2.4x. Pooled distributions were 'nearly identical (all effects negligible to small)'. Opened arXiv 2601.20109 (MSR '26; replication repo named MSR-Mining-2026). Quote matches, and the sample is '1,210 merged agent-generated bug-fix PRs from Python repositories'.
- **inf-1** (corrected) [inference]: Inference: for agent-authored PRs, Geiger can reasonably use change size and diffusion, file history, and PR rework (commits per PR, post-first-push churn). Size is the most consistent JIT contributor (McIntosh & Kamei), though feature importance drifts and models need recent retraining. For agent PRs, the evidence is for rework signals, not raw size. Author-experience and ownership features are untested on agent authors and may need redefining, e.g. as agent or operator history, rather than being dropped outright. Meta's LR model includes author experience.
  - [McIntosh & Kamei, TSE 2018](https://rebels.cs.uwaterloo.ca/papers/tse2017_mcintosh.pdf) (2018): "the role that code change properties (e.g., Size, Experience) play within JIT models fluctuates over time"
  - [Abreu et al., arXiv 2410.06351](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.06351) (2024-10-08): "how much experience author had modifying the changed code previously"
  - Fact-check: McIntosh & Kamei TSE (rebels.cs.uwaterloo.ca PDF) quote matches. The paper says 'The Size family is a consistent top-contributor' but that the magnitude of family importance fluctuates, and recommends retraining within three months. So it supports Size as consistently important, not 'stable features' in general. The Abreu et al. arXiv 2410.06351 quote matches: it describes predictor clusters in Meta's LR model, including author experience. Agent-1 (2609.26847) supports rework signals (commits per PR, post-first-push churn) with file count held fixed. It does not show raw change size predicting fixes, and pooled signal differences were negligible to small. An extended search found no JIT study testing experience features on agent authors; the closest work (e.g. arXiv 2601.00753) predicts review effort, not defects. That experience features 'lose their meaning' for agents is an untested hypothesis. The operator's or agent's own history could stand in for them. jit-1 and jit-4 were not provided, so I could not check them.
- **inf-2** (corrected) [inference]: Inference: the outcome evidence, all observational and mostly first-party anecdotes, supports a change-scoped score that is delivered at diff time, actionable and clearable, and low-noise. Diff-time delivery helped Infer (fb-1). The goog-1 and goog-2 failures show it is not sufficient without actionability and low false positives. None of this shows that scoring changes, rather than the whole codebase, reduces later defects. Meta's deployments report throughput gains, with safety outcomes that are observational and confounded by selection. No controlled study was found in which per-change quality budgets, like Geiger's -0.2 points per PR, reduced defects.
  - [Adams et al., arXiv 2605.30208, Section 6.2 Construct Validity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30208) (2026-06-12): "Time to close and diff review wall time capture responsiveness and queueing effects but do not directly measure defect prevention or long-term maintainability."
  - [Liu et al., arXiv 2607.02782, Limitations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02782) (2026-07-02): "we have not yet measured downstream outcomes such as reduced time-to-resolution or reduced post-landing incident rates"
  - Fact-check: Both quotes verified: 2605.30208 Sec 6.2 ('do not directly measure defect prevention or long-term maintainability') and 2607.02782 Sec 6 ('we have not yet measured downstream outcomes...'). The core conclusion follows: no evidence that change-scoped scoring reduces defects; a search for controlled studies of new-code quality gates found only vendor docs (SonarSource 'Clean as You Code'). One sentence overreaches: 'Delivery mode decides whether anyone acts: compare fb-1 and goog-2 with goog-1.' In goog-1 the flag was already delivered at review time and still failed; the authors blame non-actionability. In goog-2 a diff-time integration was also discontinued, over false positives and customization. Diff-time delivery is therefore necessary but not sufficient, and all three are observational first-party anecdotes. meta-2, jit-1 and jit-6 were not provided, so I could not check them.

### Searches without relevant results

- 'just-in-time defect prediction AI-generated code agent pull requests features author experience 2025 2026' (extended, 2026-11-07): no study evaluates JIT features, such as developer experience, on agent-authored changes; only review-effort and acceptance studies came up.
- Google Research publication page for Lewis et al. 2013 (research.google/pubs/pub41145, 2026-11-07): no PDF link on the page. The PDF was found at static.googleusercontent.com/.../pubs/archive/41145.pdf.
- core.ac.uk/download/275589728.pdf (Fan et al. TSE 2019) and the SMU ink repository page (2026-11-07) returned HTML or an empty page, not the paper; only the author-page abstract was read.
- par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10081351 (Hilton et al. ASE 2018, 2026-11-07): download failed; jonbell.net/publications/coverage worked.
- static.googleusercontent.com .../pubs/archive/46576.pdf and cacm.acm.org (Sadowski et al. 2018, 2026-11-07): returned HTML; the storage.googleapis.com pubtools/4365.pdf copy worked.
- No search found a controlled or randomized study showing that change-scoped quality gates or per-PR score budgets reduce later defects (covered by the searches above, 2026-11-07).
- Re-check (does not hold): 'just-in-time defect prediction AI-generated code agent pull requests features author experience 2025 2026' (extended): no study evaluates JIT features, such as developer experience, on agent-authored changes; only review-effort and acceptance studies came up. — I re-ran the search in extended mode and ran a standard search on agentic PRs with SZZ. Neither found a study that trains or evaluates JIT feature models on agent-authored changes, so that core still holds. The 'only review-effort and acceptance' part no longer holds. Mazloomzadeh, Morovati & Khomh, arXiv 2607.21832 (23 Jul 2026, AIDev dataset, Python repos), used PyDriller SZZ to compare bug-inducing rates. Finding 7: 'Agentic PRs maintain bug-inducing rates comparable to, and often lower than, human-generated PRs'. Differences were mostly not significant and had negligible effect sizes. It compares rates only and does not model JIT features.
- Re-check (holds): Google Research publication page for Lewis et al. 2013 (research.google/pubs/pub41145): no PDF link on the page. The PDF was found at static.googleusercontent.com/.../pubs/archive/41145.pdf. — pub41145 redirects to research.google/pubs/does-bug-prediction-support-human-developers-findings-from-a-google-case-study/. Its static HTML contains no .pdf or googleusercontent link. https://static.googleusercontent.com/media/research.google.com/en//pubs/archive/41145.pdf returned 200 application/pdf.
- Re-check (holds): core.ac.uk/download/275589728.pdf (Fan et al. TSE 2019) and the SMU ink repository page returned HTML or an empty page, not the paper; only the author-page abstract was read. — core.ac.uk returned HTTP 403 text/html. ink.library.smu.edu.sg/sis_research/4494 returned an 849-byte Cloudflare 'Just a moment...' page. The author page abstract at yuanrui-fan.github.io was readable.
- Re-check (holds): par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10081351 (Hilton et al. ASE 2018): download failed; jonbell.net/publications/coverage worked. — par.nsf.gov: curl returned code 000, so the connection failed. jonbell.net/publications/coverage returned 200 application/pdf (11 pages, 428,058 bytes).
- Re-check (holds): static.googleusercontent.com .../pubs/archive/46576.pdf and cacm.acm.org (Sadowski et al. 2018): returned HTML; the storage.googleapis.com pubtools/4365.pdf copy worked. — 46576.pdf returned 404 text/html, and the CACM article page returned 403 text/html. I could not reproduce the exact storage.googleapis.com URL because the path is elided and my guess returned 403. The web-search budget ran out before I could look it up. The saved local copy (sources/gap-change-level-risk-evidence/sadowski2018.pdf) is a genuine CACM PDF (DOI 10.1145/3188720, created 2018-03-12).
- Re-check (holds): No search found a controlled or randomized study showing that change-scoped quality gates or per-PR score budgets reduce later defects. — I found no controlled or randomized study. I could not run fresh dedicated searches because the session web-search budget ran out (200-call limit). The closest outcome evidence is observational: Mockus et al., TOSEM 2025, doi 10.1145/3722216. With risk-model gating of 16% of diffs in the winter 2023 freeze, 'despite 42% more diffs landed, there was a reduction in SEVs of 52%' against the prior year. That is a before/after comparison without a control group. The finding should cite it as the strongest available, non-causal evidence.

### Open questions

- Do JIT features such as size, diffusion and file history keep their predictive power on Rust Cargo workspaces and Godot game code? No Rust JIT dataset was found.
- Does a code-quality delta, such as complexity or architecture violations added by a PR, add predictive power beyond size and diffusion in JIT models? None of the sources tests maintainability metrics as JIT predictors.
- How should Geiger handle verification latency (labels arriving weeks after an agent PR) and model drift (retrain within three months) if it calibrates weights against defects?
- Is Meta's 1/50 production-incident rate for RADAR diffs explained by selecting low-risk diffs? The paper provides no causal estimate.
- Exact size of Liu et al.'s expert-labeled outage set: the percentages imply a few dozen diffs, but the paper does not state the count.
- Would hunk-level risk highlights help agents, as opposed to human reviewers, fix code? No study tests this.

## Has any repository- or system-level aggregate score (SIG star rating, Quamoco grade, SonarQube debt ratio, CodeScene average Code Health) been validated against maintenance outcomes at project level? Which aggregation from function to file to repository (sum, mean, max or percentile, inequality indices, risk profiles, several combined) preserves predictive signal, and how sensitive are the resulting rankings to the weights?

- **agg-1** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Luijten and Visser (SQM 2010) report Spearman rho = 0.62 (one-sided, p<0.001) between SIG's aggregate maintainability rating and a defect-resolution-speed rating. The data covered 10 mostly-Java OSS projects, 89 selected snapshots (of 107 collected) and 9,192 closed or resolved defects. Sub-characteristics ranged from 0.41 (stability) to 0.64 (changeability), so maintainability was not the highest value. Base properties were volume 0.29, duplication 0.31, and unit size/complexity/module coupling 0.51; unit interfacing was -0.14 and not significant. Team size and popularity were not measured, and snapshots of the same system were treated as independent data points.
  - [Luijten, Visser: Faster Defect Resolution with Higher Technical Quality of Software (SQM 2010)](https://webarchive.di.uminho.pt/wiki.di.uminho.pt/twiki/pub/Personal/Joost/PublicationList/SQM2010-FasterDefectResolution.pdf) (2010): "Maintainability is correlated to defect resolution time with one of the highest factors of all (0.62), showing again the reinforcing effect of aggregation in the model."
  - [Luijten, Visser: Faster Defect Resolution with Higher Technical Quality of Software (SQM 2010)](https://webarchive.di.uminho.pt/wiki.di.uminho.pt/twiki/pub/Personal/Joost/PublicationList/SQM2010-FasterDefectResolution.pdf) (2010): "We did not measure some aspects of software projects that could have an influence on the issue handling process, such as team size or project popularity."
  - Fact-check: Opened the SQM 2010 PDF (saved at .local/geiger-research/sources/aggregation-check/sqm2010.pdf). Both quotes are present verbatim. Table II gives volume 0.29, duplication 0.31, unit size, unit complexity and module coupling 0.51 each, unit interfacing -0.14 (p=0.897, not significant), analysability 0.51, changeability 0.64, stability 0.41, testability 0.53, maintainability 0.62. Table I lists '107' total snapshots and '89' selected, and '9,192' selected defects. The threats section says 'multiple snapshots of the same system might be regarded as independent data points since they are separated in time'. It also says 'Most projects in our study have Java as the main programming language.' Corrections: (a) the correlations used 89 of the 107 snapshots; (b) maintainability (0.62) is not the top value, since changeability is 0.64; (c) stability (0.41) is lower than the unit-level properties (0.51), so 'sub-characteristics correlated more strongly than most base properties' holds only against volume and duplication; (d) unit interfacing was -0.14 and not significant, and the claim leaves it out. Independent check: SIG's 2026 model-changes doc (raw.githubusercontent.com/Software-Improvement-Group/sigridci/main/docs/reference/quality-model-documents/2026-model-changes.md) says 'The Volume system property has been removed from the maintainability model.' The validated 2010 model therefore no longer matches the current Sigrid model.
- **agg-2** (corrected) [empirical-result]: A 2019 SATToSE replication (Wijnmaalen, Chen, Bijlsma, Oprescu; Chen and Bijlsma at SIG) re-scored reacquired snapshots of the Bijlsma et al. 2012 systems with SIG's 2018 model. The maintainability-to-defect-resolution correlation fell from 0.64 to 0.33, and for enhancements from 0.53 to 0.47. Most original system properties kept similar correlations, but module coupling fell from 0.55 to 0.36 and unit interfacing became non-significant. The authors attribute the drop mainly to the newly added modularity sub-characteristic and its new system properties: component balance correlated -0.34 and modularity -0.30 with defect resolution. A model revision that adds new measures to the aggregate and recalibrates thresholds can therefore halve the outcome correlation of the composite. This is not evidence about reweighting fixed measures.
  - [Wijnmaalen et al.: The Relation between Software Maintainability and Issue Resolution Time: A Replication Study (SATToSE 2019)](https://ceur-ws.org/Vol-2510/sattose2019_paper_11.pdf) (2019-07): "The overall maintainability correlation with defect resolution time decreased significantly while the original system properties correlate similar with defect resolution time"
  - [Wijnmaalen et al.: The Relation between Software Maintainability and Issue Resolution Time: A Replication Study (SATToSE 2019)](https://ceur-ws.org/Vol-2510/sattose2019_paper_11.pdf) (2019-07): "The other surprising result is the large drop of maintainability from 0.64 to 0.33."
  - Fact-check: Opened https://ceur-ws.org/Vol-2510/sattose2019_paper_11.pdf. Both quotes are verbatim. Table 5 gives defect maintainability 0.64 -> 0.33 and component balance -0.34 (p=0.001); Table 6 gives enhancement maintainability 0.53 -> 0.47. Co-authors Chen and Bijlsma are listed as SIG. Problems with the claim: (1) Not all base properties kept similar correlations. The authors write 'except for module coupling which shows a significant drop from 0.55 to 0.36', and unit interfacing went from 0.19 (p=0.042) to 0.05 (p=0.640). (2) The 2018 model did more than reweight. It added new measurements (component balance, component independence) and new sub-characteristics (modularity, reusability), and it used stricter yearly-recalibrated thresholds. The paper says 'the added maintainability sub-characteristic modularity with its system properties component balance and component independence are the biggest causing factor'. So 'aggregation/weighting changed the signal while the measurements kept theirs' is overstated: new measures with negative correlations entered the aggregate. (3) Snapshots were 'reacquired (with small deviations)'; Webkit had about 500 KLOC less. The component-depth setting followed 'our own interpretation'. The venue is a SATToSE seminar paper.
- **agg-3** [fact]: SIG ratings are benchmark-relative and recalibrated every year. The 4-star unit-complexity threshold moved from 24.3% (2017) to 21.1% (2018) of code in units with McCabe above 5, so unchanged code receives a lower rating.
  - [Wijnmaalen et al.: replication study (SATToSE 2019)](https://ceur-ws.org/Vol-2510/sattose2019_paper_11.pdf) (2019-07): "SIG calibrates the thresholds for all ratings (both systemproperties and characteristics) yearly."
- **agg-4** [empirical-result]: Quamoco's section 7 validation compared the base-model grades of 5 open-source Java systems with the Linzer Software-Verkostung ranking by 9 experts (Kendall's W = 0.6635). It found Spearman rho = 0.9 (p = 0.05), with two adjacent systems swapped. A second study showed grades improving over 4 versions (3.63 to 3.17) of one industrial system. The authors did not test whether fewer measures or other weights would give the same ranking.
  - [Wagner et al.: Operationalised product quality models and assessment: The Quamoco approach (IST 2015), local copy .local/geiger-research/sources/aggregation/quamoco2015.txt, section 7](https://arxiv.org/abs/1611.09230) (2015): "Although the base model did not provide exactly the same ranking as the experts, the calculated Spearman’s rho correlation is r = 0.9"
  - [Wagner et al.: Quamoco (IST 2015), section 7.1.4](https://arxiv.org/abs/1611.09230) (2015): "We did not explicitly test whether the same results could be obtained with a smaller number of measures or product factors"
- **agg-5** [empirical-result]: Griffith, Izurieta and Huvaere (ESEM 2017) compared Quamoco and SQALE assessments of 20 C# projects (open source and US Army Corps of Engineers commercial). They found Cohen's kappa = 0.0 for maintainability, reliability and security, and Goodman-Kruskal gamma confidence intervals that include 0.
  - [Griffith, Izurieta, Huvaere: An Industry Perspective to Comparing the SQALE and Quamoco Software Quality Models (ESEM 2017)](https://www.cs.montana.edu/izurieta/pubs/ESEM_2017.pdf) (2017): "The results of the Cohen’s κ analysis strongly indicates there is no agreement between the Quamoco and SQALE models in any of the three quality characteristics"
- **agg-6** [empirical-result]: Sjøberg, Anda and Mockus (ESEM 2012) studied 4 functionally equivalent Java systems built by different companies, on which 6 developers did 3 maintenance tasks each. The Maintainability Index, structural metrics and code smells were mutually inconsistent at system level. Only system size and low cohesion tracked effort, while 1/MI, OMMIC and Feature Envy correlated negatively with effort.
  - [Sjøberg, Anda, Mockus: Questioning Software Maintenance Metrics: A Comparative Case Study (ESEM 2012)](https://www.mn.uio.no/ifi/personer/vit/dagsj/sjoberg.anda.mockus.esem.2012.pdf) (2012): "Results: The metrics were not mutually consistent. Only system size and low cohesion were strongly associated with increased maintenance effort."
  - [Sjøberg, Anda, Mockus (ESEM 2012)](https://www.mn.uio.no/ifi/personer/vit/dagsj/sjoberg.anda.mockus.esem.2012.pdf) (2012): "at the entire system level, the simplest metric of size was the best predictor of maintainability."
- **agg-7** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Borg, Ezzouhri and Tornhill (CodeScene; arXiv 20 Aug 2024; ICSME 2024 Industry Track) benchmarked tools against Schnappinger et al.'s expert-labelled MainData. The dataset holds 519 Java files, 78% labelled maintainable, with experts disagreeing substantially on 17%. AUC was Code Health 0.95, state-of-the-art ML 0.97 and a line-count-only baseline 0.95. SonarQube's size-normalised TD Ratio scored AUC 0.60, and F1 0.12 at flagging unmaintainable files. Its absolute TD Time scored AUC 0.86, and F1 0.60 on the same task. All results are file-level, and the authors are from the vendor of the winning tool.
  - [Borg, Ezzouhri, Tornhill: Ghost Echoes Revealed: Benchmarking Maintainability Metrics and Machine Learning Predictions Against Human Assessments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.10754) (2024-08-20): "SotA ML (A), Code Health (B), and the LoC baseline (C) reach about 0.95. TD Time (E) and MS-MI (D) yield about 0.90. TD Ratio performs the worst"
  - [Borg, Ezzouhri, Tornhill (2024)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.10754) (2024-08-20): "maintainability prediction of source code files, Schnappinger et al. (2020) presented a manually annotated Maintainability Dataset (MainData) containing 519 files"
  - Fact-check: Opened https://arxiv.org/pdf/2408.10754 (v1, 20 Aug 2024). Both quotes are verbatim. MainData has 519 files, 'imbalanced with 78% maintainable files', and 'For 17% of the source code files, the experts disagreed substantially.' Table I AUC: SotA ML 0.97, Code Health 0.95, LoC 0.95, MS-MI 0.89, TD Time 0.86, average human expert 0.83, TD Ratio 0.60. UC2 (liability) F1: TD Ratio 0.12, TD Time 0.60, Code Health 0.83. The 0.86 is TD Time's AUC, not an F1. As written, the claim reads as if 0.86 were TD Time's F1 for unmaintainable files. The venue was confirmed on the researchr ICSME 2024 page as 'Industry Track Paper'. All three authors are CodeScene employees or affiliates, which is a vendor conflict of interest.
- **agg-8** [fact]: Borg et al. (CodeScene, arXiv Jan 2024) built their Code Health value model from file-level regressions only. They call project-level extrapolation future work and warn that a plain average is inflated by many small, healthy files. They propose a size-weighted average over high-churn files instead, but did not test it.
  - [Borg et al.: Increasing, not Diminishing: Investigating the Returns of Highly Maintainable Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.13407) (2024-01-24): "a project with numerous small, high-quality files would display an inflated average CH."
  - [Borg et al.: Increasing, not Diminishing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.13407) (2024-01-24): "Extrapolating file-level results to project-wide averages is an important research direction."
- **agg-9** [empirical-result]: Borg, Udayakumar and Tornhill (arXiv Dec 2024) analysed more than 1,000 proprietary projects. The size-weighted average Code Health had a mode of 9.5 and a 10th-percentile ('laggard') threshold of 5.9. Hotspot Code Health has a heavier low tail (laggard threshold 4.1). Counting each project equally versus weighting by project size changes the distributions markedly. No outcome data was used.
  - [Borg, Udayakumar, Tornhill: Industrial Code Quality Benchmarks: Toward Gamification of Software Maintainability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.06307) (2024-12-09): "Due to the dominant probability mass around high scores, the leaderboard positions around the leaders might appear volatile. That is, small CodeHealth changes might cause big moves."
- **agg-10** [empirical-result]: Zhang, Hassan, McIntosh and Zou (TSE, May 2017) studied 255 open-source projects and 11 method-to-file aggregation schemes (sum, mean, median, SD, CoV, Gini, Hoover, Atkinson, Shannon, generalised entropy, Theil). Sum alone gave the best defect-proneness model in 11% of projects and all schemes together in 40%. Mean (69%) and median (71%) were best for effort-aware ranking, and sum was adequate for predicting defect count or rank. Max and percentiles were not studied.
  - [Zhang, Hassan, McIntosh, Zou: The Use of Summation to Aggregate Software Metrics Hinders the Performance of Defect Prediction Models (TSE 43(5))](https://sail.cs.queensu.ca/data/pdfs/TSE2017_TheUseOfSummationToAggregateSoftwareMetricsHindersThePerformanceOfDefectPredictionModels.pdf) (2017-05): "when constructing models for effort-aware defect prediction, the mean or median aggregation schemes yield performance values that are significantly closer to the best performance than any of the other studied aggregation schemes."
- **agg-11** (corrected) [empirical-result]: An arXiv-only, single-author preprint (Rawad Abou Assi, Queen's University, 2015) aggregated method-level LOC, McCabe and Halstead metrics to file level in 12 releases of Eclipse, Ant and jEdit. Sum-aggregated models generally beat the other eight aggregations. A model with all aggregations gave no meaningful gain over sum. The differences were often statistically significant, but effect sizes were negligible to small and pointed in both directions. Aggregation was to file level only, never to package or repository level.
  - [Abou Assi: Investigating the Impact of Metric Aggregation Techniques on Defect Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/1503.08504) (2015-03-29): "Our results show that defect prediction models built using summation outperform those built using other aggregation techniques."
  - Fact-check: Opened https://arxiv.org/abs/1503.08504 and the PDF (saved under .local/geiger-research/sources/agg-fc/abou.pdf). The paper is single-author, from the School of Computing, Queen's University, submitted 29 Mar 2015 and listed only on arXiv/CoRR. It studies 12 releases (Eclipse 2.0-3.0, Ant 1.3-1.7, jEdit 4.0-4.3). The quote appears verbatim. Abstract: 'incorporating all aggregation types in the same model does not provide a significant improvement over using summation alone.' The tests are more nuanced. Table 8 shows All-vs-Sum MSE differences of p<0.001 on all 12 datasets, with negligible-to-small Cliff's d, and the differences point in both directions: 'the difference between the two sets of MSE values is statistically significant for all datasets. However, the effect size is negligible for most'. Section 5.1: 'We also applied aggregation at the file level only.' I could not check what 'the opposite' refers to, because the prior finding is not in this claim.
- **agg-12** [empirical-result]: Vasilescu, Serebrenik and van den Brand (ICSM 2011) lifted SLOC from class to package level across the 106 Qualitas Corpus systems. Gini, Theil, Atkinson and Hoover were highly and significantly correlated, so choosing among inequality indices adds little information. No outcome variable was used.
  - [Vasilescu, Serebrenik, van den Brand: You can't control the unfamiliar: A study on the relations between aggregation techniques for software metrics (ICSM 2011), abstract](https://research.tue.nl/en/publications/3bd12134-da86-489f-9f3d-ebeeb9fe7781) (2011-09): "Our results indicate high and statistically significant correlation between the Gini, Theil, Atkinson, and Hoover indices, i.e., aggregation values obtained using these techniques convey the same information."
- **agg-13** (corrected) [empirical-result]: In Eclipse release 3.0 (Zimmermann, Premraj, Zeller, PROMISE 2007), maximum-aggregated metrics often correlated with defects about as well as summed metrics; PAR_max was the exception. Post-release Spearman correlations at package level: nested block depth max .467 vs sum .404; cyclomatic complexity max .451 vs sum .431. At file level, max was slightly below sum (NBD .383 vs .392, VG .385 vs .401). The .483/.464 pair is for pre-release defects. The correlations cover one release, not 2.0-3.0.
  - [Zimmermann, Premraj, Zeller: Predicting Defects for Eclipse (PROMISE 2007)](https://thomas-zimmermann.com/publications/files/zimmermann-promise-2007.pdf) (2007): "many cases the maximum metrics (_max) show correlation values comparable to the accumulated ones (except for PAR_max)."
  - Fact-check: Opened https://thomas-zimmermann.com/publications/files/zimmermann-promise-2007.pdf. The quote appears verbatim. Table 3 covers release 3.0 only ('Table 3 lists the correlation values for both, file and package level (release 3.0)') and gives separate columns for pre- and post-release defects. The cited .483 (NBD_max) and .464 (NBD_sum) are package-level correlations with PRE-release defects. For post-release defects at package level: NBD_max .467 vs NBD_sum .404, and VG_max .451 vs VG_sum .431. At file level, post-release: NBD_max .383 vs NBD_sum .392, and VG_max .385 vs VG_sum .401. All are Spearman correlations.
- **agg-14** [empirical-result]: Posnett, Filkov and Devanbu (ASE 2011) used defect and process data from 18 OSS projects to show the ecological-inference risk. Relations found at one aggregation level (file, package, module) can be misleading at another.
  - [Posnett, Filkov, Devanbu: Ecological inference in empirical software engineering (ASE 2011), abstract via OpenAlex](https://api.openalex.org/works/doi:10.1109/ASE.2011.6100074) (2011-11): "In other fields it has been found that choosing the wrong level might lead to misleading or fallacious results."
- **agg-15** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Dawes (1979) showed that equal-weighted linear composites of standardized, correctly signed predictors predict a numerical criterion about as well as cross-validated regression weights; in 3 of 4 examples they did as well or better. They stay below the in-sample optimum, for example .60 vs .69. Random positive weights did only about as well as models of human judges and were often clearly below fitted weights (.30 vs .46 cross-validated). The 'flat maximum' covers near-optimal weights and requires predictors that are not negatively correlated.
  - [Dawes: The robust beauty of improper linear models in decision making (American Psychologist 34(7))](https://www.cmu.edu/dietrich/sds/docs/dawes/the-robust-beauty-of-improper-linear-models-in-decision-making.pdf) (1979): "In fact, unit (i.e., equal) weighting is quite robust for making such predictions."
  - [Dawes (1979)](https://www.cmu.edu/dietrich/sds/docs/dawes/the-robust-beauty-of-improper-linear-models-in-decision-making.pdf) (1979): "The ways in which outputs are relatively insensitive to changes in coefficients (provided changes in sign are not involved)"
  - Fact-check: Opened https://www.cmu.edu/dietrich/sds/docs/dawes/the-robust-beauty-of-improper-linear-models-in-decision-making.pdf. Both quotes appear verbatim, as does 'Weights that are near to optimal level produce almost the same output as do optimal beta weights.' The non-negative intercorrelation condition is also there: 'as long as these intercorrelations are not negative'. Table 1 shows the random-weight claim is overstated. Random-weight models scored .30/.51/.51/.39 against cross-validated regression .46/.57/.57/.38 and optimal .46/.69/.69/.54. Random weights equalled the judges' models but often fell short of fitted weights. Equal weights (.34/.60/.60/.48) matched or beat cross-validated regression in 3 of 4 examples, but stayed below the optimal in-sample model. Dawes also requires standardized predictors ('Equal or random weighting of incomparable variables ... without prior standardization would be nonsensical') and conditionally monotone predictor-criterion relationships.
- **agg-16** (corrected) [empirical-result]: The JRC toolkit (EUR 21682, 2005) says weights heavily influence composite rankings. With equal weights, linear, geometric and multi-criteria aggregation of the 23-country Technology Achievement Index ranked Finland 1st, 2nd and 3rd respectively. A Monte Carlo uncertainty analysis jointly varied normalisation, indicator inclusion, aggregation method, weighting scheme and expert selection. It produced a mean average rank shift of almost 3 positions, reaching 9 in some multi-criteria runs. Aggregation method and indicator inclusion drove most of the shift; weighting acted mainly through interactions (first-order Sobol 0.038).
  - [Nardo, Saisana, Saltelli, Tarantola: Tools for Composite Indicators Building (JRC EUR 21682)](https://knowledge4policy.ec.europa.eu/sites/default/files/eur_21682_en_1.pdf) (2005): "Weights heavily influence the outcome of a composite indicator and countries ranking in a benchmarking exercise."
  - [Nardo et al. (JRC 2005)](https://knowledge4policy.ec.europa.eu/sites/default/files/eur_21682_en_1.pdf) (2005): "Although in all cases we used equal weighting, rankings produced are very different."
  - Fact-check: Opened https://knowledge4policy.ec.europa.eu/sites/default/files/eur_21682_en_1.pdf (EUR 21682, 2005, Nardo, Saisana, Saltelli, Tarantola). Both quotes appear verbatim. Table 6.11 confirms Finland ranks 1 (LIN), 3 (MCA) and 2 (GME) across 23 countries. The uncertainty analysis varied more than weights. Its inputs were 'normalisation method, inclusion-exclusion of a sub-indicator, aggregation system, weighting scheme, expert selection'. The text says: 'The mean value is almost 3 positions ... The input factors affecting this variable the most are aggregation system plus inclusion – exclusion at the first order'. In Table 7.1 the weighting scheme has a first-order Sobol index of 0.038 and a total effect of 0.327, mostly through interactions. The 'up to 9' figure is an average shift across all countries, seen in some MCA runs: 'In some cases the average shift in country's rank when using MCA can be as high as 9 places.'
- **agg-17** [empirical-result]: Bakota et al. (ICSM 2012) related a system-level probabilistic maintainability score (Szeged group, ISO 9126/25010 based) to development cost on 3 proprietary and 2 open-source Java systems. They report an exponential maintainability-cost relationship and accurate cost prediction.
  - [Bakota et al.: A Cost Model Based on Software Maintainability (ICSM 2012), abstract](https://www.inf.u-szeged.hu/~ferenc/publication/bhl12) (2012-09): "maintainability and development costs are in exponential relationship with each other. We also found that our model is able to predict future development costs with high accuracy"
- **agg-18** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Ulan, Löwe, Ericsson and Wingkvist (EMSE 26(5), published 23 June 2021) proposed unsupervised, probability-based weighted metrics aggregation. They evaluated it on defect prediction with about 200,000 code changes and about 5,000 classes. The abstract says it 'can be used as an agnostic unsupervised predictor in the absence of a ground truth.' It also says expert weighting does not scale and that experts get confused when metrics are not independent. Whether it matches supervised models is not stated in the abstract and remains unverified.
  - [Ulan, Löwe, Ericsson, Wingkvist: Weighted software metrics aggregation and its application to defect prediction (EMSE 26:86), abstract via OpenAlex](https://api.openalex.org/works/doi:10.1007/s10664-021-09984-2) (2021-06-23): "The results show that our approach can be used as an agnostic unsupervised predictor in the absence of a ground truth."
  - Fact-check: Opened the OpenAlex record https://api.openalex.org/works/doi:10.1007/s10664-021-09984-2 (published 2021-06-23, EMSE vol. 26, issue 5). The quote appears verbatim, as do 'one on ca. 200 000 code changes, and another ca. 5 000 software classes' and 'experts get confused if the metrics are not independent, which is rarely the case.' The abstract does not say the approach predicted defects about as well as supervised models. It says only that it 'can be used as an agnostic unsupervised predictor in the absence of a ground truth.' The full text (CC-BY, Springer and DiVA) was blocked by redirects and bot checks, so I could not verify any comparison with supervised models.
- **agg-19** (corrected) [inference]: Inference, limited to the named aggregates: none of SIG maintainability stars, Quamoco grade, SQALE/SonarQube debt ratio or CodeScene average Code Health has been validated against maintenance outcomes on more than about 10 systems. The largest found is the SIG model on 10 OSS systems (about 120 snapshots; Bijlsma/Luijten, replicated by Wijnmaalen et al. 2019). A related SIG-style system rating, the test code quality model, was validated on 18 OSS systems (Athanasiou et al., TSE 2014); it correlated with 2 of 3 issue-handling metrics. No study comparing aggregators (mean, LOC-weighted mean, max, percentile, inequality index, risk profile) against repository-level maintenance outcomes was found, but this negative was not independently re-verified.
  - [Wijnmaalen et al. (2019): the largest repository-level validation found, with 10 systems](https://ceur-ws.org/Vol-2510/sattose2019_paper_11.pdf) (2019-07): "Previous research has shown that there is a positive correlation between the maintainability score measured by the SIG maintainability model and shorter issue resolution time."
  - Fact-check: Opened https://ceur-ws.org/Vol-2510/sattose2019_paper_11.pdf. The quote appears verbatim. The original study 'assessed 10 open source systems looking at multiple snapshots', with 120 snapshots in Table 4. The negative claim is too broad as worded. Athanasiou, Nugroho, Visser and Zaidman (IEEE TSE 2014, DOI 10.1109/TSE.2014.2342227; abstract read via https://api.openalex.org/works/doi:10.1109/TSE.2014.2342227) validated a SIG-style, benchmark-based system-level test-code-quality rating against issue-handling outcomes: 'the test code quality model is applied to 18 open source systems'. Results: 'a significant positive correlation between test code quality and two out of the three issue handling metrics (throughput and productivity)'. That model is not one of the four named aggregates, so the restriction to those four may still hold. The web search budget ran out during this check. I could not independently re-run the negative searches for aggregator comparisons against repository-level outcomes, so that part stays unverified.
- **agg-20** (corrected) [inference]: Inference: user-tuned weights will probably keep the score's overall correlation with outcomes if components are standardized and correctly signed (Dawes 1979). Any threshold on a weighted sum, including a 0.2-point per-PR budget, will still flip decisions for marginal PRs when weights change. JRC 2005 shows that aggregation-method and component-inclusion choices move rankings even more than weights. Borg et al. 2024 (arXiv 2412.06307) found average CodeHealth bunched near the top across 1,000+ projects (mode 9.5), so small changes move leaderboard ranks a lot. That is cross-project evidence and only an analogy for per-PR decisions. Recalibrating the model sharply reduced the SIG rating's correlation with defect resolution time (Wijnmaalen et al. 2019). Design rules (a)-(d) follow as prudent choices, not tested results.
  - [Dawes (1979)](https://www.cmu.edu/dietrich/sds/docs/dawes/the-robust-beauty-of-improper-linear-models-in-decision-making.pdf) (1979): "Weights that are near to optimal level produce almost the same output as do optimal beta weights."
  - [Nardo et al. (JRC 2005)](https://knowledge4policy.ec.europa.eu/sites/default/files/eur_21682_en_1.pdf) (2005): "In some cases the average shift in country’s rank when using MCA can be as high as 9 places."
  - Fact-check: Both quotes appear verbatim in https://www.cmu.edu/dietrich/sds/docs/dawes/the-robust-beauty-of-improper-linear-models-in-decision-making.pdf and https://knowledge4policy.ec.europa.eu/sites/default/files/eur_21682_en_1.pdf. Dawes supports overall robustness for correctly signed, standardized components. JRC rank shifts came mainly from aggregation method and indicator inclusion, with weighting as a first-order effect of only 0.038 (Table 7.1). Opened https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.06307 (Borg, Udayakumar, Tornhill; Gamify 2025 workshop; 1,000+ proprietary projects). Its bunching concerns average CodeHealth across projects ('a vast majority of projects are in the interval above 8 with a mode at 9.5'). Its volatility concerns leaderboard ranks across projects ('the leaderboard positions around the leaders might appear volatile'), not per-PR decisions within one repository. Wijnmaalen et al. support the recalibration point: 'The overall maintainability correlation with defect resolution time decreased significantly'. Recommendations (a)-(d) follow as reasonable design rules. The claim that weights flip marginal pass/fail decisions is true by construction for any threshold on a weighted sum, and needs no empirical citation.

### Searches without relevant results

- "SonarQube technical debt ratio project level validation defects maintenance effort empirical study open source projects correlation" (extended, 2026-11-07): only file- or rule-level SonarQube studies; no project-level debt-ratio-to-outcome validation
- "system-level maintainability score validated against maintenance effort or velocity across many projects empirical study star rating SIG Sigrid 2020..2025" (extended, 2026-11-07): no post-2019 multi-project validation of SIG/Sigrid ratings; only pre-2013 studies (Sjøberg 2012, Yamashita 2013, Anda 2007)
- "software quality model aggregation weights sensitivity analysis ranking of systems changes Quamoco OR QATCH OR SQALE weights robustness" (extended, 2026-11-07): no weight-perturbation or ranking-stability analysis for any software quality model
- "aggregation maximum vs sum vs mean method-level metrics to file or package level defect prediction comparison study" (extended, 2026-11-07): no study compares max/percentile aggregation against outcomes at repository level; only file/package-level correlations (Zimmermann 2007)
- Bijlsma et al. 2012 full text (Springer, scispace/typeset, academia.edu) could not be retrieved (2026-11-07); its 0.64/0.53 correlations are taken from the 2019 replication's 'old correlations' table
- Luijten, Visser, Zaidman MSR 2010 (read 2026-11-07): it only defines issue-handling risk profiles and ratings on GNOME and does not link them to code quality
- Yamashita & Counsell 2013 'Code smells as system-level indicators of maintainability' (JSS): full text and abstract pages blocked (403); not cited
- Re-check (does not hold): SonarQube TD ratio: only file- or rule-level SonarQube studies; no project-level debt-ratio-to-outcome validation — NOT RE-VERIFIED. The session's WebSearch budget (200 calls) ran out before this search, so I could not re-run it in standard or extended mode. No contradicting evidence came up in the sources I opened. The one SonarQube outcome comparison I read (Borg et al. 2024, arXiv 2408.10754) is file-level. Treat this as still open, not as confirmed.
- Re-check (does not hold): No post-2019 multi-project validation of SIG/Sigrid ratings; only pre-2013 studies (Sjøberg 2012, Yamashita 2013, Anda 2007) — NOT RE-VERIFIED: the search budget ran out. The statement is also inconsistent with the findings themselves. SIG-model validations exist from 2010 (Luijten & Visser SQM 2010), 2012 (Bijlsma et al. SQJ) and 2019 (Wijnmaalen et al. SATToSE, agg-2). 'Only pre-2013 studies' is therefore wrong as worded, while 'none after 2019' remains unverified. SIG's 2026 model-changes doc on GitHub describes yearly recalibration on 30,000 systems but cites no outcome validation.
- Re-check (does not hold): No weight-perturbation or ranking-stability analysis for any software quality model (Quamoco, QATCH, SQALE) — NOT RE-VERIFIED: the search budget ran out. Partial support: Quamoco IST 2015 section 7 contains no sensitivity analysis (grep found nothing for 'sensitiv' or 'robust'), and its authors say they did not test reduced measure or factor sets. QATCH, PIQUE and later Montana State work were not checked.
- Re-check (does not hold): No study compares max/percentile aggregation against outcomes at repository level; only file/package-level (Zimmermann 2007) — NOT RE-VERIFIED: the search budget ran out. Partial support: Zhang et al. TSE 2017 (opened) compares 11 schemes at file level only and excludes max and percentiles. Borg et al. 2024 (2401.13407) calls project-level extrapolation future work.
- Re-check (holds): Bijlsma et al. 2012 full text could not be retrieved; 0.64/0.53 taken from the 2019 replication's 'old correlations' table — Re-checked on 2026-11-07. link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s11219-011-9140-0 redirects to the Springer login (idp.springer.com/authorize, 303). The 2019 replication's Table 5 and Table 6 'Old Correlations' show maintainability 0.64 (defects) and 0.53 (enhancements), as stated.
- Re-check (holds): Luijten, Visser, Zaidman MSR 2010 only defines issue-handling risk profiles and ratings on GNOME and does not link them to code quality — Re-read the local copy (gap-aggregate-score-outcome-validity/luijten_msr2010.txt). The research questions cover assessing issue-handling efficiency and how it changes over time and across components in GNOME. The paper applies risk profiles and ratings to 300 GNOME products, and its results and conclusions never correlate them with code-quality ratings. It only cites Luijten's thesis [2] on maintainability.
- Re-check (holds): Yamashita & Counsell 2013 JSS full text and abstract blocked (403); not cited — Re-tried www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0164121213000769 on 2026-11-07 and got HTTP 403 Forbidden. I could not confirm that this PII is the correct article, but ScienceDirect blocks the fetch either way.

### Open questions

- Would a LOC-weighted mean, a churn-weighted mean, a tail statistic (p90/max) and a SIG-style risk profile rank the same repositories in the same order, and which tracks issue resolution time or change effort best? No study tests this; Geiger could test it on OSS Rust workspaces.
- How much do Geiger-style per-PR pass/fail decisions change under plausible weight perturbations (Monte Carlo over the weight simplex) near a 98/100 baseline?
- Does the Bijlsma correlation survive controls for project size, team size and activity? Volume alone correlated 0.29-0.39, and no study adjusted for confounders.
- Schnappinger's 2020 dataset and later work are file-level. Is there any expert-labelled repository-level maintainability dataset beyond Quamoco's 5-system Linzer ranking?
- Would CodeScene's untested churn-filtered, size-weighted project average validate against project outcomes? The authors list this as future work.

## Which Rust-specific signals (Clippy warning density by lint group, unsafe usage, panic sites such as unwrap/expect/indexing, clone and Rc<RefCell<_>> density, typestate or newtype use) have an empirical link to defects, vulnerabilities, review effort or maintenance cost, and which crates.io-wide distributions exist to calibrate Rust thresholds the way Alves et al. calibrated Java and C#?

- **rust-1** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Li et al. (arXiv 2310.11738 v1, 2023-10-18, unrefereed preprint) report 'On average, 21 per KLOC' for crates.io: 8,095,259 warnings over 374,424,742 LOC in the latest releases of 94,715 crates (snapshot date not given). The figure matches a pooled ratio (about 21.6), but the paper does not say how it averaged. The paper says each crate's own configuration was used. However, its top lint, default_numeric_fallback, is an allow-by-default restriction lint and accounts for 4,887,114 warnings (over 50%). That casts doubt on whether the warnings really reflect per-crate configurations; the lint setup is underspecified. The figure is neither a per-crate distribution nor a fixed-configuration baseline, and it is not linked to defects.
  - [Li et al., Unleashing the Power of Clippy in Real-World Rust Projects (arXiv 2310.11738)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.11738) (2023-10-18): "For an unbiased data extraction, therefore, we shall use individual Rust project’s own configuration to extract the warnings the team cares about."
  - [Li et al., Unleashing the Power of Clippy in Real-World Rust Projects (arXiv 2310.11738)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.11738) (2023-10-18): "The top one gets 4,887,114, which is already over 50% of all warnings."
  - [Li et al., Unleashing the Power of Clippy in Real-World Rust Projects (arXiv 2310.11738)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.11738) (2023-10-18): "On average, 21 per KLOC is the density of warnings for the Crates-IO projects."
  - Fact-check: Opened arxiv.org/pdf/2310.11738 (v1, 2023-10-18, unrefereed preprint; the PDF still carries the ACM template header 'Conference acronym XX, June 03-05, 2018'). All three quotes are in the text. Also verified: '94715 projects on Crates-IO, which has 374,424,742 LOC for their latest release'; 'Altogether we found 8,095,259 instances of 403 types of warnings'; 'The largest category default_numeric_fallback'. 8,095,259 / 374,424.742 KLOC = 21.6, which matches the '21 per KLOC' figure, but the paper only says 'On average' and never states how it computed the number. It gives no per-crate distribution, no snapshot date and no link to defects. Problem with the methodology: the paper says it used each project's own configuration, yet 4,887,114 warnings (over 50%) come from default_numeric_fallback. That lint is in the allow-by-default restriction group (confirmed in Clippy's source), and few crates are likely to enable it, so the two statements are hard to reconcile. The actual lint setup is underspecified.
- **rust-2** [fact]: The lint that dominates Li et al.'s count, default_numeric_fallback, is in Clippy's allow-by-default restriction group. Clippy's README says the restriction group should never be enabled as a whole. Clippy has over 800 lints in groups with different default levels: correctness is deny, suspicious/style/complexity/perf are warn, pedantic/restriction/nursery/cargo are allow. Warning density therefore depends mainly on which groups a project enables.
  - [rust-clippy README (master, fetched 2026-11-07)](https://github.com/rust-lang/rust-clippy/blob/master/README.md) (2026-11-07): "The contained lints may lint against perfectly reasonable code, may not have an alternative suggestion, and may contradict any other lints (including other categories)."
  - [Clippy source: default_numeric_fallback.rs (master)](https://github.com/rust-lang/rust-clippy/blob/master/clippy_lints/src/default_numeric_fallback.rs) (2026-11-07): "pub DEFAULT_NUMERIC_FALLBACK, restriction,"
- **rust-3** [fact]: Clippy's maintainers left the cognitive_complexity lint in the restriction group specifically so that users would not treat it as a measurement tool. They point to excessive_nesting and too_many_lines instead.
  - [Clippy source: cognitive_complexity.rs (master)](https://github.com/rust-lang/rust-clippy/blob/master/clippy_lints/src/cognitive_complexity.rs) (2026-11-07): "The true Cognitive Complexity of a method is not something we can calculate using modern technology."
- **rust-4** [empirical-result]: In Li et al.'s developer survey (31 responses), 42% of participants complained that Clippy produces many, frequent false positives. The longitudinal part covers only four rust-lang projects: warnings fell in three and rose in cc-rs. No defect outcome was measured.
  - [Li et al., Unleashing the Power of Clippy in Real-World Rust Projects (arXiv 2310.11738)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.11738) (2023-10-18): "a lot of (42%) participants complained that Clippy produce relatively many and frequent false positives."
  - [Li et al., Unleashing the Power of Clippy in Real-World Rust Projects (arXiv 2310.11738)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.11738) (2023-10-18): "we gathered a total of 31 responses from developers"
- **rust-5** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Tadesse et al. (arXiv 2602.00840 v1, Jan 2026, preprint) studied seven GNU coreutils programs translated by C2Rust, C2SaferRust, TranslationGym and humans. In their taxonomy, Clippy warnings in the runtime panic risk, thread safety, error handling and compatibility categories were zero for every translation. This is partly because Clippy reported some underlying problems under other labels (e.g. a missing '# Safety' doc) and partly because it emitted no lint at all. The Clippy lint-group configuration is not reported. GPT-4o, used as a linter, flagged nonzero issues in those categories, but these were not systematically validated. The manual analysis gives illustrative examples such as unsynchronized static mut. The authors conclude that Clippy is effective for idiomatic Rust and misses issues in C-style code.
  - [Tadesse, Nitin, Salah, Ray, d'Amorim, Assunção, Code Quality Analysis of Translations from C to Rust (arXiv 2602.00840v1)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00840) (2026-01-31): "several external quality categories, such as Compatibility issues, Error handling, Runtime panic risks, and Thread safety, have zero occurrences across all translation techniques."
  - [Tadesse et al., Code Quality Analysis of Translations from C to Rust](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00840) (2026-01-31): "Clippy primarily flagged issues in idiomatic Rust constructs while struggling to detect warnings in non-idiomatic translations."
  - Fact-check: Opened arxiv.org/pdf/2602.00840 (v1, 31 Jan 2026, the only version; preprint). The paper covers seven GNU coreutils programs and four translation sources (C2Rust, C2SaferRust, TranslationGym, human). Both quoted sentences appear. Overstatements in the claim: (a) Some of the zeros come from how warnings were mapped to the taxonomy. Exact text: 'Clippy either (i) reported the underlying problems under different labels or (ii) did not emit any lint at all.' Example: a thread-safety issue was reported as 'Unsafe function's docs miss # Safety section' and counted under Documentation. (b) The paper does not say which Clippy lint groups were enabled. The zero panic-risk count probably reflects that restriction lints such as unwrap_used and indexing_slicing were off. (c) GPT-4o's counts in these categories are per-1k-LOC figures in Fig. 2b and were not systematically validated. The manual analysis presents illustrative examples, such as unsynchronized static mut, rather than a measured precision. The paper's wording is 'its rule-based approach is effective for idiomatic Rust'.
- **rust-6** [empirical-result]: Zheng et al. studied 433 RustSec vulnerabilities, 300 repositories and 218 fix commits. Vulnerable code has statistically significantly higher ratios of unsafe functions and unsafe blocks than the complete code of the same packages. Yet 95% of affected functions are safe functions, and 41.5% of those contain unsafe blocks.
  - [Zheng, Wan, Zhang, Chang, Lo, A Closer Look at the Security Risks in the Rust Ecosystem (arXiv 2308.15046; TOSEM)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.15046) (2023-08-29): "vulnerable code has statistically significantly higher ratios of unsafe functions and unsafe blocks compared to complete code"
  - [Zheng et al., A Closer Look at the Security Risks in the Rust Ecosystem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.15046) (2023-08-29): "95% of the affected functions are safe functions. Among the affected safe functions, 41.5% contain unsafe blocks in their body."
