- **rust-7** [empirical-result]: Xu et al. analyzed 186 memory-safety bugs, a superset of all Rust memory-safety CVEs up to 2020-12-31. All of them require unsafe code, apart from one compiler bug. Many are mild soundness issues rather than exploitable bugs.
  - [Xu, Chen, Sun, Zhou, Lyu, Memory-Safety Challenge Considered Solved? An In-Depth Study with All Rust CVEs (TOSEM 2021, arXiv v6)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2003.03296) (2021-02-25): "Rust can keep its promise that all memory-safety bugs require unsafe code, and many memory-safety bugs in our dataset are mild soundness issues"
- **rust-8** [empirical-result]: Qin et al. (PLDI 2020) manually inspected 850 unsafe usages and 170 bugs. All memory-safety bugs involve unsafe code, and most also involve safe code. All blocking (deadlock) concurrency bugs they studied are in safe code.
  - [Qin, Chen, Yu, Song, Zhang, Understanding Memory and Thread Safety Practices and Issues in Real-World Rust Programs (PLDI 2020)](https://songlh.github.io/paper/rust-study.pdf) (2020-06): "We found that all memory-safety bugs involve unsafe code, and (surprisingly) most of them also involve safe code."
  - [Qin et al., PLDI 2020](https://songlh.github.io/paper/rust-study.pdf) (2020-06): "all blocking bugs we studied are in safe code"
- **rust-9** [empirical-result]: In Qin et al.'s extended study (IEEE TSE) of 110 real panic bugs, 107 occurred in safe code. 39 came from missing error handling: 27 from unwrap() and 12 from expect(). 18 came from explicit panic!/assert!/unreachable!, 12 from out-of-bounds indexing, 9 from other causes such as stack overflow, and the rest largely from arithmetic overflow.
  - [Qin, Chen, Liu, Zhang, Wen, Song, Zhang, Understanding and Detecting Real-World Safety Issues in Rust (IEEE TSE)](https://songlh.github.io/paper/rust-tse.pdf) (2024 (date not stated in the PDF; it cites 2024 references)): "Unsurprisingly, the majority of these issues (107 out of 110) are in safe code."
  - [Qin et al., Understanding and Detecting Real-World Safety Issues in Rust (IEEE TSE)](https://songlh.github.io/paper/rust-tse.pdf) (2024): "27 issues are triggered by calling unwrap(), while the remaining 12 are triggered when invoking expect()."
- **rust-10** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Qin et al.'s checkers flagged 7,259 potential assertion-error sites in the MirChecker comparison and 42,174 potential panic sites across their benchmarks. The authors say not all of these are real bugs but did not measure how many are. MirChecker flagged 327 potential panics and proved 2 would occur; the rest were left as potential, not shown false. All 7 of its memory-bug reports were false positives. The gap between potential panic sites and actual panic bugs is therefore likely large but unquantified. Gallant's 'If a Rust program panics, then it signals a bug' is a practitioner opinion, not evidence about site-to-bug ratios.
  - [Qin et al., Understanding and Detecting Real-World Safety Issues in Rust (IEEE TSE)](https://songlh.github.io/paper/rust-tse.pdf) (2024): "our tools identified 7259 code sites that have the potential to cause assertion errors."
  - [Andrew Gallant (BurntSushi), Using unwrap() in Rust is Okay](https://burntsushi.net/unwrap/) (2022-08-08): "If a Rust program panics, then it signals a bug in the program."
  - Fact-check: Opened rust-tse.pdf. In the MirChecker comparison on downgraded benchmarks: 'MirChecker flags 327 panic issues'; 'MirChecker reports two caused by out-of-bounds accesses as proved to occur, while reporting the other 325 as potential panic issues'; its seven memory-bug reports were all false positives; 'our tools identified 7259 code sites that have the potential to cause assertion errors.' Separately: 'We identify 42174 code sites that have the potential to trigger panics. While not all of these sites necessarily represent real bugs'. The paper never checks how many of these sites are real bugs, and it does not show that MirChecker's 325 potential panics are false. So the claim that sites 'vastly outnumber actual panic bugs' is plausible but unmeasured. The BurntSushi page (2022-08-08) contains the quote verbatim, but it is an opinion that any panic signals a bug. It does not support the numeric claim.
- **rust-11** [fact]: PanicFI/Panic4R provides 102 real panic bugs with fixes from the top 500 most-downloaded crates, usable as outcome labels for validating panic-site metrics. The authors state that over half of rustc's bugs are panic-induced crashes.
  - [Ni, Feng, Liu, Chen, Xu, PanicFI: An Infrastructure for Fixing Panic Bugs in Real-World Rust Programs (arXiv 2408.03262v2; ACM TOSEM)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.03262) (2024-11-04): "we construct a dataset, Panic4R, comprising 102 real panic bugs and their fixes from the top 500 most-downloaded open-source crates."
- **rust-12** (corrected) [empirical-result]: The crates.io-wide unsafe baselines are per-crate figures: share of crates and per-crate counts. None normalizes unsafe by code size per function or per file. Evans et al. (ICSE 2020; 15,097 crates, 81% of registered crates) found that under 30% of crates use the unsafe keyword, but more than half cannot be fully statically checked because of unsafe code in dependencies. They also publish cumulative per-crate distributions of unsafe blocks and declared unsafe functions: over 90% of crates have fewer than ten unsafe blocks, and 90% have fewer than two declared unsafe functions. Rust Foundation (21 May 2024): 24,362 of about 127,000 crates with significant code (19.11%) use unsafe, and 34.35% call directly into a crate that does. None of these is a per-function or per-file density that could calibrate thresholds the way Alves et al. did.
  - [Evans, Campbell, Soffa, Is Rust Used Safely by Software Developers? (ICSE 2020)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2007.00752) (2020-07-01): "software engineers use the keyword unsafe in less than 30% of Rust libraries, but more than half cannot be entirely statically checked by the Rust compiler"
  - [Rust Foundation, Unsafe Rust in the Wild: Notes on the Current State of Unsafe Rust](https://web.archive.org/web/2024/https://foundation.rust-lang.org/news/unsafe-rust-in-the-wild-notes-on-the-current-state-of-unsafe-rust/) (2024-05): "Of those 127,000 crates, 24,362 make use of the unsafe keyword, which is 19.11% of all crates."
  - Fact-check: Opened arXiv 2007.00752 PDF. The quote is present: "software engineers use the keyword unsafe in less than 30% of Rust libraries, but more than half cannot be entirely statically checked by the Rust compiler". The same paper refutes 'only yes/no figures': "more than 90% of the crates have fewer than ten unsafe blocks" and "the per-crate number of declared unsafe functions is small with 90% of the crates containing fewer than two declared unsafe functions" (Figures 3-4 are cumulative distributions per crate). Opened the archived Rust Foundation page (21 May 2024). It has the exact quote and "34.35% make a direct function call into another crate that uses the unsafe keyword." A secondary summary of Astrauskas et al. (OOPSLA 2020, https://alastairreid.github.io/RelatedWork/papers/astrauskas:oopsla:2020) also reports distributions of unsafe-block sizes. I could not open that paper's PDF.
- **rust-13** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Zhang et al. (arXiv 2610.21373v2, 17 Oct 2026; header says IEEE TSE, October 2026) evaluated six Rust SAST tools: Semgrep, LockBud, Prusti, Rudra, MIRAI and MirChecker. They measured false negatives on 320 real vulnerabilities, and every tool's FN rate exceeded 90% (94.1% to 99.7%). They measured false positives separately, by manually inspecting warnings on 25 repositories (the top five by stars for each year from 2021 to 2025). The text reports an average FP rate of 80.3%. Per-tool FP rates in Table II range from 15% (Prusti, 20 warnings) to 88.5%. Their unweighted mean is 68.2% and the pooled rate is 77.9%, so the 80.3% figure cannot be reproduced from the table. The paper says only 31 of the studied repositories contain vulnerability types the tools support and the other 53 fall outside their coverage scope. Clippy was not evaluated. Some tools depend on nightly compilers.
  - [Zhang, Zhao, Pei, Dong, Pan, Tan, Chen, Evaluating and Improving Static Application Security Testing Tools for Rust (arXiv 2610.21373v2; IEEE TSE)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21373) (2026-10-17): "By comparison, the average FP rate of Rust SAST tools in our study is 80.3%."
  - [Zhang et al., Evaluating and Improving SAST Tools for Rust](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21373) (2026-10-17): "All tools exhibit very high FN rates (more than 90%)."
  - Fact-check: Opened the arXiv 2610.21373v2 PDF. It has "By comparison, the average FP rate of Rust SAST tools in our study is 80.3%." and "All tools exhibit very high FN rates (more than 90%)" (Table IV: FN 301-319 of 320). The FP basis: "we select the top five repositories by star count from each year between 2021 and 2025 for FP analysis, resulting in a total of 25 repositories". Table II FP rates: 88.5%, 82.1%, 81.5%, 57.8%, 84.4%, 15%. I recomputed them: mean 68.2%, pooled 1091/1401 = 77.9%. Scope: "only 31 contain vulnerability types that are supported by the evaluated SAST tools, whereas the remaining 53 fall outside the tools' coverage scope." The word Clippy appears 0 times. Nightly: "Some SAST tools depend on nightly Rust compilers or unstable compiler internals".
- **rust-14** [fact]: RustXec (MSR 2026 Data and Tool Showcase) provides 102 reproducible vulnerabilities, each with a proof of vulnerability and a container, from 89 Rust projects, Jan 2021 to Apr 2025, in eight RustSec categories. The largest categories are memory corruption (31) and denial of service (28).
  - [Ji, Wang, Wang, Li, Yang, Zhang, RustXec: A Vulnerability Reproduction Dataset for Assessing Security Risks in Open-Source Rust Applications (MSR 2026)](https://people.cs.vt.edu/xinw/publications/RustXec26-B38KjKAe.pdf) (2026-04): "RustXec contains 102 reproducible vulnerabilities from 89 distinct Rust projects, spanning eight vulnerability categories."
- **rust-15** [empirical-result]: The Cursor difference-in-differences study (He et al., arXiv 2511.04427v3) reported a 30% rise in static-analysis warnings and a 41% rise in complexity after adoption. Its sample includes only 24 Rust repositories, and the authors say the results may not generalize beyond JavaScript, TypeScript and Python.
  - [He, Miller, Agarwal, Kästner, Vasilescu, Speed at the Cost of Quality: How Cursor AI Increases Short-Term Velocity and Long-Term Complexity in Open-Source Projects (v3)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.04427) (2026-01-26): "Our results may not generalize to other LLM agent assistants, proprietary software projects, and programming languages beyond the three dominant ones in our dataset"
- **rust-16** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Ardito et al. (PeerJ CS 7:e406, 2021) used rust-code-analysis on nine code artifacts per language to compare Rust with C, C++, Python, JavaScript and TypeScript. They report Rust's mean CC Avg as 2.0 (median 2.0). CC Avg is cyclomatic complexity averaged over rust-code-analysis 'spaces', which include functions but also other structures, so it is not strictly per function. The authors say the results cannot be generalized, and the paper derives no thresholds. Whether this is the only such comparison is a negative claim. I found no contradicting study, but I could not search exhaustively.
  - [Ardito, Barbato, Coppola, Valsesia, Evaluation of Rust code verbosity, understandability and complexity (PeerJ Computer Science 7:e406)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7959618/) (2021-02): "we acknowledge that the results cannot be generalized to all software written with one of the analyzed programming languages."
  - Fact-check: Opened https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7959618/. It has "measured on a limited number of source artifacts (namely, nine different code artifacts per programming language). Therefore, we acknowledge that the results cannot be generalized to all software written with one of the analyzed programming languages." Table 14: "Rust 25.3 (22) 2.0 (2.0)" for CC Sum / CC Avg. Definition: "the averaged value of CC over the number of spaces in a file (CC Avg ). A space is defined in rust-code-analysis as any structure that in[cludes]...". The only threshold mentions are existing MI cut-offs (e.g. 80, or 10/20 in Visual Studio). No Rust thresholds are derived. One independent search found no other Rust cross-language metric comparison.
- **rust-17** [empirical-result]: Heuer (bachelor thesis, July 2026) refactored three production backend components (Otto) with typestate and newtype. Using benchmarks, static metrics and expert interviews, he found that typestate improved faultlessness and testability but added structural code. Halstead difficulty penalized the more explicit error-state enums that experts praised. Sonar's Ann Campbell confirmed in correspondence that Cognitive Complexity counts only control flow.
  - [Heuer, Benefits of Applying Software Design Patterns to Backend Rust Applications (arXiv 2607.02744)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02744) (2026-07-02): "While the typestate pattern improves faultlessness and testability significantly, it comes at the cost of more structural code that can degrade readability."
  - [Heuer, Benefits of Applying Software Design Patterns to Backend Rust Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02744) (2026-07-02): "Especially the function that builds the final response FinalResponse::from was pointed out as positive for making the possible error states more visible, which is penalized by the HDiff metric."
  - [Heuer thesis, Appendix A.4 (correspondence with G. Ann Campbell)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02744) (2025-11-25): "In formulating Cognitive Complexity we made a conscious decision to look only at control flow."
- **rust-18** (corrected) [empirical-result]: Coblenz et al. (ICSE 2022) ran a randomized controlled trial recruiting from a 633-student class. 428 students participated in some way and 333 were randomly assigned. On a task requiring complex aliasing, Bronze (GC) participants were more likely to finish in time. Among those who finished, Bronze users took a median of 4 hours and traditional Rust users a median of 12 hours. The authors found no significant difference in total assignment time. The paper describes a typical solution for the non-GC aliasing task as Rc<RefCell<Turtle>> with RefMut. This shows an effort cost for aliasing-heavy designs among Rust beginners. It is not evidence that Rc<RefCell<_>> density predicts defects.
  - [Coblenz, Mazurek, Hicks, Garbage Collection Makes Rust Easier to Use: A Randomized Controlled Trial of the Bronze Garbage Collector (ICSE 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.01098) (2022-02-15): "those who did so required only about a third as much time (4 hours vs. 12 hours)."
  - Fact-check: Opened the arXiv 2110.01098 PDF (v2, ICSE '22). It has "collecting data from 428 students in total ... those who did so required only about a third as much time (4 hours vs. 12 hours). We found no significant difference in total time". Also "333 students were part of the random assignment, and 428 students participated in the study in some way" and "Bronze participants finished Aliasing significantly faster (median = 4 hours) than Traditional Rust participants (median = 12 hours)". On the solution: "a typical solution was Rc<RefCell<Turtle>> and RefMut<'a, Turtle>". The original claim leaves out that these are medians among completers, the randomized n of 333, and the null result on total time.
- **rust-19** (corrected) [inference]: Inference, partly corrected: of the candidate Rust signals, only unsafe and unwrap/expect/indexing panic sites have outcome-linked evidence. That evidence is retrospective. For panic sites it is the share of known bugs that involve the construct (e.g. Qin et al.: 39 panic issues from unwrap/expect misuse). For unsafe it is somewhat stronger. Zheng et al. report that vulnerable code has statistically significantly higher ratios of unsafe functions and unsafe blocks than the whole package's code. That is a case-control density comparison, though not a prospective density-to-defect prediction model. I found no validation of Clippy group density, clone density, Rc<RefCell> density or typestate/newtype use against defects, vulnerabilities or review effort. However, my independent search for Clippy-defect studies could not run (search budget exhausted), so this negative rests on the original researcher's searches.
  - [Zheng et al., A Closer Look at the Security Risks in the Rust Ecosystem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.15046) (2023-08-29): "vulnerable code has statistically significantly higher ratios of unsafe functions and unsafe blocks compared to complete code"
  - [Qin et al., Understanding and Detecting Real-World Safety Issues in Rust (IEEE TSE)](https://songlh.github.io/paper/rust-tse.pdf) (2024): "In total, 39 panic issues stem from this cause"
  - Fact-check: Opened the arXiv 2308.15046 PDF (Zheng et al.). It has "In the vulnerable packages, vulnerable code has statistically significantly higher ratios of unsafe functions and unsafe blocks compared to complete code". This is a ratio (density) comparison, which contradicts the claim's statement that the unsafe evidence is only a share of known bugs and not a density comparison. Opened https://songlh.github.io/paper/rust-tse.pdf (Qin et al., TSE). It has "In total, 39 panic issues stem from this cause, with 17 resulting from misuse of Option objects and 22 resulting from misuse" (of Result, via unwrap()/expect()). This supports the share-of-bugs framing for panics. Findings rust-6 to rust-9 were not re-verified here.
- **rust-20** (corrected) [inference]: Inference, corrected: no crates.io-wide per-function or per-file distribution exists for size, complexity, Clippy warnings by group, unsafe density or panic-site density. Only per-crate counts exist for unsafe (Evans et al. CDFs of unsafe blocks and functions per crate). So Rust thresholds cannot yet be calibrated as Alves et al. did. A benchmark looks feasible: about 127,000 crates had significant code as of May 2024, and the number is likely higher by Nov 2026. rust-code-analysis plus Clippy with a fixed configuration and fixed lint groups can extract the metrics. Calibration should stratify or exclude FFI/-sys and low-level crates, because they are extreme unsafe outliers. The rationale should not be that most unsafe is FFI: Evans et al. found that only 22% of unsafe calls go to C libraries, while the Rust Foundation says most unsafe uses are calls into non-Rust code. RustSec, Zheng et al.'s dataset, RustXec (102 vulnerabilities) and Panic4R (102 panic bugs) can serve as outcome labels, but they are small.
  - [Rust Foundation, Unsafe Rust in the Wild](https://web.archive.org/web/2024/https://foundation.rust-lang.org/news/unsafe-rust-in-the-wild-notes-on-the-current-state-of-unsafe-rust/) (2024-05): "As of May 2024, there are about 145,000 crates; of which, approximately 127,000 contain significant code."
  - [Evans, Campbell, Soffa, Is Rust Used Safely by Software Developers? (ICSE 2020)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2007.00752) (2020-07-01): "We find that only 22% of these unsafe functions are to external libraries implemented in C"
  - Fact-check: Opened the Rust Foundation archive (21 May 2024). It has "As of May 2024, there are about 145,000 crates; of which, approximately 127,000 contain significant code." and "Most of these Unsafe Rust uses are calls into existing third-party non-Rust language code or libraries, such as C or C++." The cited Evans quote works against the 'unsafe concentrates in FFI' rationale: "We find that only 22% of these unsafe functions are to external libraries implemented in C, suggesting that a majority of the Unsafe Rust is actually from Rust code". Evans does support stratifying outliers: "A very small number of crates have thousands of declared unsafe functions. These crates are typically low-level support crates for embedded devices or C library bindings." Evans also provides per-crate (not per-function) distributions.

### Searches without relevant results

- 'empirical study unwrap expect panic Rust crates bugs prevalence' (extended, 2026-11-07): no crates.io-wide unwrap/expect density study and no density-to-defect correlation; only bug-cause studies (Qin TSE, PanicFI).
- 'Rust code smells empirical study clone Rc RefCell anti-patterns maintainability' (extended, 2026-11-07): no Rust-specific results.
- 'empirical study Rust interior mutability RefCell Rc usage crates borrow errors' (extended, 2026-11-07): only tutorials and docs; no prevalence or outcome study.
- 'clone() overuse Rust LLM generated code study borrow checker workaround excessive cloning empirical' (extended, 2026-11-07): only blogs, forum threads and the Rust Design Patterns anti-pattern page; no measured study.
- 'Clippy warnings defect prediction Rust lints bug-fix commits correlation study' (extended, 2026-11-07): no study links Clippy warnings to bug-fix commits or SZZ-labelled defects.
- 'empirical study Clippy lint bugs false positives issue tracker rust-clippy study 2024 2025' (extended, 2026-11-07): no systematic false-positive rate per lint group beyond Li et al.'s survey.
- 'large-scale crates.io study code metrics distribution cyclomatic complexity function length Rust ecosystem' (extended, 2026-11-07): no registry-wide metric distribution; only tools (rust-code-analysis, mete, linthis).
- 'benchmark-based metric thresholds Rust projects calibration' (extended, 2026-11-07): no Alves- or Visser-style threshold derivation for Rust.
- 'AIDev dataset agentic pull requests language breakdown Rust' (standard, 2026-11-07): no published Rust count or Rust-specific analysis for AIDev; it would have to be computed from the dataset.
- Astrauskas et al. OOPSLA 2020 full text: author PDF URLs at UBC/ETH returned 404 or HTML and ACM DL returned 403; per-crate unsafe percentages seen only in secondary notes, not cited.
- MDPI Future Internet 18(9):471 'LLM-Assisted Porting of Security-Critical C Libraries to Idiomatic Rust' (zero Clippy warnings across six ports): mdpi.com returned 403; not read, not cited.
- Re-check (holds): 'empirical study unwrap expect panic Rust crates bugs prevalence' (extended): no crates.io-wide unwrap/expect density study and no density-to-defect correlation; only bug-cause studies (Qin TSE, PanicFI). — Re-ran in extended mode on 2026-11-07. Results: PanicFI/Panic4R (TOSEM, 102 panic bugs from the top 500 crates), Qin TSE, a rustc bug study, blogs and GitHub issues. New item: Zhuang et al., 'Don't Panic!' (CCS 2025, PanicKiller). I opened the PDF. Its section 'Prevalence of Runtime Safety Checks' counts runtime checks, including unwrap checks, in fuzzing benchmark programs. It is not crates.io-wide and does not correlate density with defects. The negative result still holds.
- Re-check (holds): 'Rust code smells empirical study clone Rc RefCell anti-patterns maintainability' (extended): no Rust-specific results. — Re-ran in extended mode. All results were general code-smell literature (OpenStack, Blob/Spaghetti, simulation software). None were Rust-specific.
- Re-check (holds): 'empirical study Rust interior mutability RefCell Rc usage crates borrow errors' (extended): only tutorials and docs; no prevalence or outcome study. — Re-ran in extended mode. Results: the Rust Book chapter, std::cell docs, blogs and FAQs, and the Bronze GC randomized trial (arXiv 2110.01098, not opened; about garbage collection, not RefCell prevalence). No prevalence or outcome study.
- Re-check (holds): 'clone() overuse Rust LLM generated code study borrow checker workaround excessive cloning empirical' (extended): only blogs, forum threads and the Rust Design Patterns anti-pattern page; no measured study. — Re-ran in extended mode. Results: a dev.to/rezvov blog, an HN thread, the Rust Design Patterns borrow_clone page, RustAssistant (fixing compilation errors) and a crate-hallucination study (arXiv 2606.08444). None measures clone overuse.
- Re-check (holds): 'Clippy warnings defect prediction Rust lints bug-fix commits correlation study' (extended): no study links Clippy warnings to bug-fix commits or SZZ-labelled defects. — Re-ran in extended mode. Results: Li et al. (arXiv 2310.11738, plus an ICSE 2024 poster at oro.open.ac.uk that returned 403 when fetched), a general study of static-analysis warning density (arXiv 2111.09188, not Rust) and generic defect-prediction papers. No Clippy-to-bug-fix study.
- Re-check (holds): 'empirical study Clippy lint bugs false positives issue tracker rust-clippy study 2024 2025' (extended): no systematic false-positive rate per lint group beyond Li et al.'s survey. — Re-ran in extended mode. Results: Li et al., the Clippy lint list, a 'Pedantic Clippy' blog post (2021), and false-positive studies for other tools (CodeQL, industrial LLM). No per-group false-positive study for Clippy. Clippy's own docs admit false positives for some lints, e.g. default_numeric_fallback 'many false positives and false negatives'.
- Re-check (holds): 'large-scale crates.io study code metrics distribution cyclomatic complexity function length Rust ecosystem' (extended): no registry-wide metric distribution; only tools (rust-code-analysis, mete, linthis). — Re-ran in extended mode. Results: tools (mete, complexity, cargo-crap), the small PeerJ 2021 study 'Evaluation of Rust code verbosity, understandability and complexity' (not registry-wide; not opened) and an FFI undefined-behaviour study on a crates.io snapshot. No registry-wide metric distribution.
- Re-check (holds): 'benchmark-based metric thresholds Rust projects calibration' (extended): no Alves- or Visser-style threshold derivation for Rust. — Re-ran in extended mode. Results: rustc-perf significance thresholds (performance, not code metrics), Bayesian hierarchical thresholds (arXiv 1804.02443, not Rust) and C-to-Rust benchmarks. No Rust threshold derivation.
- Re-check (does not hold): 'AIDev dataset agentic pull requests language breakdown Rust' (standard): no published Rust count or Rust-specific analysis for AIDev; it would have to be computed from the dataset. — Re-ran in standard mode, then opened the papers. The AIDev paper (Li, Zhang, Hassan, arXiv 2507.15003), Table 6 'Top-10 languages used in GitHub repositories across agents in AIDev-pop', lists Rust with 49 repositories (5.7%) across all agents: Codex 28, Devin 10, Copilot 6, Cursor 5, Claude Code 4. The library-usage paper (arXiv 2512.11589v2) says 'We initially included Rust (fifth most popular language)' and then excluded it because of workspace crates. So a published Rust repository count exists. A Rust PR count and a Rust-specific analysis are still absent.
- Re-check (holds): Astrauskas et al. OOPSLA 2020 full text: author PDF URLs at UBC/ETH returned 404 or HTML and ACM DL returned 403; per-crate unsafe percentages seen only in secondary notes, not cited. — Re-fetched on 2026-11-07. pm.inf.ethz.ch/publications/AstrauskasMathejaPoliMuellerSummers20.pdf returned 200 but HTML, not a PDF. cs.ubc.ca/~alexsumm/papers/... returned 404. dl.acm.org/doi/pdf/10.1145/3428204 returned 403. I could not search for alternative copies because this session's web-search budget was used up.
- Re-check (holds): MDPI Future Internet 18(9):471 'LLM-Assisted Porting of Security-Critical C Libraries to Idiomatic Rust' (zero Clippy warnings across six ports): mdpi.com returned 403; not read, not cited. — Re-fetched on 2026-11-07. www.mdpi.com/1999-5903/18/9/471 and its /pdf both returned 403 with curl, and WebFetch also returned 403. ### Open questions

- Does the per-function density of unwrap/expect/indexing in library (non-test) code predict later panic-fix commits? This could be tested with SZZ on crates in Panic4R or on top-500 crates.
- Does the warning density of each Clippy group under a fixed configuration (correctness+suspicious, then pedantic) correlate with RustSec advisories or bug-fix commits per crate, after controlling for size and churn?
- What are the crates.io percentile distributions of function length, cyclomatic/cognitive complexity, nesting, unsafe blocks per KLOC, and panic sites per KLOC, stratified by crate type (FFI/-sys, library, binary, game)? No published source gives them.
- Do coding agents increase clone, Rc<RefCell>, unwrap or allow-attribute density in Rust PRs? The AIDev Rust subset has not been analyzed.
- How should Geiger avoid penalizing typestate and newtype designs? Should type-driven constructs be excluded from size and Halstead metrics, or rewarded as their own positive signal?
- Do Rust SAST tools (Rudra, LockBud) or Clippy correctness lints have usable precision on game and ECS code such as Bevy or Godot-rust? The 80.3% FP figure comes from general vulnerability repositories.

## Gaps the completeness critic raised (researched above as gap-* areas)

- **noise-floor-and-gate-reliability**: How much do Geiger's candidate measurements vary when the relevant code is unchanged (reruns, CI runners, toolchain upgrades), and what error levels does industrial practice accept before a check may block a merge? Find published figures, or measure: line/region coverage (cargo-llvm-cov vs tarpaulin engines), cargo-mutants outcomes (timeouts, unviable mutants, --in-diff), nextest flaky tests, Clippy finding counts across consecutive stable Rust releases, and LLM-judged scores (Qlty SlopOne, desloppify). Is a 0.2-point per-PR budget on a 0-100 score above the resulting noise floor? Why: Malte's mechanism (no PR may lower the score by more than 0.2 points) works only if measurement noise is well below 0.2 points. Otherwise PRs fail at random and agents learn to retry; ImpossibleBench found that allowing more submissions raised cheating from 33% to 38%. The verified claims give no variance figures, only Google's 1.5% flaky runs, tarpaulin's engine-dependent results, cargo-mutants timeouts and SlopOne's unknown stability; metrics-tests-process lists the noise floor as open. Q4 (gate vs score) rests on inference (rust-rules synth-1): no verified source states what error level makes a check fit to block. Example of the missing evidence, read 2026-11-07: Tricorder enforces an effective false-positive rate below 10%, puts analyzers whose not-useful rate reaches 10% on probation, and by default shows most results only on changed lines.
- **competitor-and-platform-overlap**: Which existing products already deliver per-change quality scores, regression gates or bundled agent-loop checks that overlap Geiger, and how far does each go? Cover at least aislop, DRS-OSS, GitHub Copilot cloud agent's built-in validation, Cursor Bugbot, Claude Code Code Review and OpenAI Codex review. For each: deterministic or LLM-judged; whether teams can add architecture or domain rules; Rust depth; whether a non-zero per-PR budget on an aggregate score can be set; pricing; adoption. Why: land-21 infers that no tool combines a repository score, a per-PR budget, team-written deterministic gates and change-scoped Rust scoring, and the Q7 defensibility answer builds on it. aislop appears only inside a landscape negative check and was never verified, yet its README (.local/geiger-research/sources/agent-gates/aislop_readme.md) claims most of that combination: a deterministic 0-100 score per change, Rust among 11 targets, opt-in import-ban and layering rules, PR scoping via --changes --base, a hook that blocks regressions below a baseline, and a hosted tier with PR gates. The product research also omits the agent platforms, the likeliest entrants. GitHub docs (read 2026-11-07) say Copilot cloud agent by default runs CodeQL, advisory checks on new dependencies, secret scanning and Copilot code review before it completes a PR, with no GHAS license. Cursor Bugbot accepts team and repository rules (.cursor/BUGBOT.md) and can fail its check on unresolved findings. Claude Code Code Review is tuned with REVIEW.md and never blocks a PR. Without this comparison the Q7 answer is unverified.
- **agent-pr-quality-and-gaming**: What do 2026 studies of real autonomous-agent pull requests (AIDev dataset, MSR 2026 Mining Challenge) show about (a) how agent PRs change static-quality metrics once normalized for size or churn, (b) which quality signals agents degrade or game in practice (lint suppressions, threshold or config edits, function splitting, duplication, over-mocked or deleted tests), and (c) how quality decays across a sequence of agent tasks on one codebase? Why: Geiger's main users are agents, but the verified agent evidence covers AI-assisted IDE use (Cursor DiD, GitClear, Echoes), benchmarks (SWE-bench, METR) and test gaming. Gaming of static metrics rests on one practitioner report (Böckeler). The agents-quality negative result that no AIDev study rates the static quality of agent PRs was 'not opened in depth' and not re-checked. An arXiv API query on 2026-11-07 lists such studies, for example a DiD on agent adoption that reports persistent rises of about 18% in static-analysis warnings and 39% in cognitive complexity (2601.13597), and a SonarQube base-vs-merge analysis in which differences between agents largely vanish after normalizing by churn (2601.20109). The answers bear on Q4 (which agent behaviours need gates or tamper protection), Q5/Q6 (size normalization) and the premise of Q7.
- **change-level-risk-evidence**: What outcome evidence supports scoring a change instead of the whole codebase? Which change-level features predict defect-inducing changes in just-in-time (JIT) defect prediction, how stable are those models over time, do simple models match deep ones, can risk be localized to lines for a report, do diff-time findings get fixed more often than batch reports, and what did Google and Meta observe when they deployed change-risk scores? Why: Q6's verified evidence is tool mechanics (Sonar new code, CodeScene delta, Sigrid CI, diff-cover, Betterer) and the Delta Maintainability Model, which agreed with experts on 67 of 100 commits. No verified claim shows that change-scoped scores predict or prevent later defects; JIT defect prediction appears only in a corrected inference (metrics-tests-process inf-1). Deployment evidence exists and was not used: Google deployed bug prediction and 'found no identifiable change in developer behavior' (Lewis et al. 2013). Meta gates diffs with an LLM-based Diff Risk Score; a 2026 Meta paper reports risk-stratified review automation and attributes over 80% of code-volume growth to agentic AI (arXiv 2605.30208). Agent authorship removes experience and ownership features, so the answer also decides which change features stay usable for agent PRs.
- **aggregate-score-outcome-validity**: Has any repository- or system-level aggregate score (SIG star rating, Quamoco grade, SonarQube debt ratio, CodeScene average Code Health) been validated against maintenance outcomes at project level? Which aggregation from function to file to repository (sum, mean, max or percentile, inequality indices, risk profiles, several combined) preserves predictive signal, and how sensitive are the resulting rankings to the weights? Why: The aggregation research describes how SIG, Squale, SQALE, Quamoco, Sonar and CodeScene aggregate, but no verified claim compares aggregators against outcomes, and its open question on Theil- or Squale-style aggregation versus LOC-weighted means is unanswered. All verified Code Health-outcome links are file-level; Borg et al. 2024 leave project-level extrapolation to future work. Geiger's score is meant as a health signal and must accept user-tuned weights, so it needs evidence that a repository-level number tracks outcomes and that weight changes do not reverse conclusions. Known sources were not used: SIG's issue-resolution study, Zhang et al. (TSE 2017) on summation, and Quamoco's validation against an expert ranking, which is in the local Quamoco text (section 7) but was not extracted.
- **rust-specific-validity-and-baselines**: Which Rust-specific signals (Clippy warning density by lint group, unsafe usage, panic sites such as unwrap/expect/indexing, clone and Rc<RefCell<_>> density, typestate or newtype use) have an empirical link to defects, vulnerabilities, review effort or maintenance cost, and which crates.io-wide distributions exist to calibrate Rust thresholds the way Alves et al. calibrated Java and C#? Why: Every metric-validity result in the verified claims comes from Java, C/C++, Python or JavaScript. The rust-tools section is an inventory without validity data, and the static-metrics and aggregation sections list missing Rust evidence and missing Rust thresholds as open. Rust's compiler and Clippy already enforce much of what classic smells measure, so transfer is not obvious, and Q1 asks for Rust explicitly. Rust sources exist but were not used: Li et al. report an average of 21 Clippy warnings per KLOC across idiomatic crates.io projects and developer complaints about false positives; Tadesse et al. (2026) assess C-to-Rust translations with Clippy, GPT-4o and manual review; a 2026 study evaluates Rust SAST tools on real vulnerabilities.
